Abstract: Existing analytical studies of fluid antenna multiple access (FAMA) are largely confined to Rayleigh fading, independent-port assumptions, or asymptotic selection metrics, leaving the fundamental outage behavior under realistic fading and spatial correlation conditions uncharacterized.
  This paper fills this gap by presenting the first exact and asymptotically tight outage analysis of interference-limited FAMA systems under spatially correlated Nakagami-$m$ fading. By explicitly modeling the joint statistics of the desired and interference powers across fluid-antenna ports, \textcolor{black}{we first formulate the outage probability in terms of the joint
  desired-signal and interference distributions and then derive an explicit
  integral expression under a tractable single-reference-port spatial
  correlation model.} Building on this result, we establish a rigorous diversity-order characterization
  revealing that the achievable diversity is jointly dictated by the fading severity and the spatial correlation rank imposed by the finite antenna aperture, rather than by the nominal number of ports. 
  To enable accurate and tractable performance evaluation beyond the asymptotic regime, we further introduce a Gamma-matched outage approximation that preserves both the exact diversity order and the array-gain constant. The resulting closed-form expression provides an accurate characterization over a wide range of signal-to-interference ratio (SIR) thresholds and offers clear physical insight into the role of spatial correlation in FAMA systems. Extensive Monte Carlo (MC) simulations confirm the tightness of the exact analysis, the sharpness of the asymptotic results, and the accuracy of the proposed approximation.

</details>

Abstract: Understanding the origins of the modality gap in vision language models remains a significant challenge. This work investigates whether differences in information density between modality specific tokens at the embedding layer contribute to this imbalance. We show that text tokens enter the model with much lower semantic density than visual tokens, driving early layers to focus on processing text representations before combining them with vision. Through mechanistic analysis, we demonstrate that replacing vision token positions with their semantically closest text token counterparts elicits increased attention to those positions in early layers. These findings provide a foundation for addressing the modality gap and provide insight to improve multimodal reasoning in VLM architectures.

Abstract: Electroencephalography (EEG) data plays an important role in understanding human brain activity, with various major applications. While current approaches, which largely build on EEG foundation models, have demonstrated strong performance across datasets and tasks, they typically rely on a fixed temporal scale. This design choice limits their ability to capture characteristics of neural dynamic behavior, often spanning across multiple timescales, which are naturally captured in EEG recordings. To address this limitation, we propose MAGE (\textbf{Multi-timescale Adaptive Generalization for EEG}), a foundation model that dynamically adapts to capture and integrate information across different timescales. Specifically, MAGE combines two key mechanisms: (i) a novel multi-timescale attention mechanism, capable of learning representations across multiple temporal scales and (ii) a trainable aggregation module, that dynamically identifies and adapts to the most informative timescale of the input signal. Experimental results across seven datasets demonstrate that MAGE consistently outperforms existing EEG foundation models while using substantially fewer parameters. Moreover, our results support the hypothesis that different downstream tasks benefit from distinct temporal scales. All source code will be released.

</details>

Abstract: As large language model (LLM) agents get actively adopted for autonomous research, they must not only reason over existing evidence, but also design informative experiments, allocate resources, analyze sequential observations, and decide when to stop and make conclusions. Yet these capabilities remain poorly measured. We introduce Causal-Discovery-Bench (https://github.com/mshen1019/causal-discovery-bench), a verifiable sandbox with configurable structural equation models and resource constraints to evaluate sequential experimental design and causal discovery without domain-semantic cues. Across 3,802 episodes with 13 models from 5 vendors, we analyze agent behavior along 40 metrics in seven families. Agents' performance exhibits a four-level difficulty hierarchy spanning single-experiment design, sequential exploration of causal relationships, resource-aware experimentation balancing cost and statistical power, and autonomous resolution of conflicting evidence. We identify a substantial performance gap between causal inference from correctly curated evidence and navigating causal discovery through autonomous experimentation: Analyzing agents' action trajectories attributes 94.5% of their mistakes to experimental design rather than to inference, which is further consolidated with ablation studies. Relaxing resource constraints alone does not reliably resolve failures in sequential discovery. In contrast, basic statistical tools substantially improve performance across multiple model families, while advanced tools provide little additional benefit when models lack prerequisite lower-level capabilities.

</details>

Abstract: Resource-constrained IoT devices cache learned models for real-time decisions, but frequent updates incur substantial bandwidth, energy, and service overhead. Because decision-relevant model differences need not grow with elapsed time, we define Age of Intelligence (AoInt) as a model-version-relative, client-specific, and action utility distortion, and formulate synchronization as a tradeoff between stale-decision regret and refresh cost. Furthermore, we develop Kullback-Leibler-aware AoInt Synchronization (KL-AoIntSync), which accumulates output-distribution changes using client-specific unlabeled probes, whereas Parameter-aware AoInt Synchronization (Param-AoIntSync) uses a server-broadcast scalar parameter change. Then, we establish pathwise guarantees with fixed client distributions, action sets, and utilities. The numerical results show that calibrated Param-AoIntSync reduces joint synchronization cost by 6.15% relative to an Instant-Parameters policy while performing comparably to KL-AoIntSync.

</details>

Abstract: This paper describes the 2026 edition of the Topological Deep Learning Challenge: Bridging the Gap, hosted at the second Topology, Algebra, and Geometry in Data Science (TAG-DS) Conference. Where previous editions grew the modelling and data ecosystem of Topological Deep Learning (TDL), this edition set out to place Graph Neural Networks (GNNs) and Topological Neural Networks (TNNs) side by side under a single evaluation pipeline. Participants implemented recent state-of-the-art architectures in TopoBench, with Track 1 covering GNNs and Track 2 covering TNNs. They evaluated these implementations on shared synthetic benchmarks generated with GraphUniverse, which independently controls homophily, average degree and degree-distribution heaviness. The challenge yielded 68 qualifying submissions from 34 teams, spanning the graph, simplicial, cellular, hypergraph and combinatorial domains. For the first time, submissions were also tracked on a public leaderboard, which we summarize and analyze here. Our analysis finds that the differences between the submitted tracks are strongly task-dependent: the mean accuracy gap is modest on community detection, while on triangle counting the Track 1 mean error is 1.85 times the Track 2 mean. Several structurally informed GNNs also outperform the Track 2 mean on triangle counting.

Abstract: When an optimization problem uses a surrogate constraint, a decision that is feasible for the surrogate need not be feasible for the true constraint. We propose Selected-Risk Calibration (SRC), a framework that calibrates a learned constraint against the optimizer's returned decisions for objectives drawn from a given distribution. SRC guarantees that, with probability at least $1-δ$, every margin it certifies on held-out objectives has selected risk, the probability that the returned decision is infeasible, of at most $γ$. We also show that the random-input coverage guarantee of split conformal prediction does not bound the selected risk, and that likelihood-ratio reweighting for feedback covariate shift does not apply when the learned constraint is active at the returned decision. Our experiments show that a conformal margin covering about $90\%$ of random inputs covered as few as $17\%$ of the returned decisions, and every certified margin had a violation rate below its target on unseen objectives. Moreover, at equal cost, SRC's correction had fewer or equal infeasible solutions to the baselines in 57 of 62 comparisons. The SRC framework applies to any setting in which a surrogate model chooses a decision whose feasibility can be verified for a sample of instances.

</details>

Abstract: Operator learning seeks to approximate function-to-function maps in a way that remains stable when the input discretization changes, a property sometimes called "discretization invariance". We consider a quantitative refinement of this idea, called $\textit{uniform discretization stability}$, which builds on a common representation of input functions and their discretizations as probability measures and provides a Hölder bound relating output changes to the Wasserstein distance between these measures. Our main result is a representation theorem showing all uniformly discretization stable operators can be written exactly as a $\textit{stable Markov kernel network}$---an abstract architecture that is a joint generalization of transformers, Fourier neural operators (FNO), Flowers, and other successful learned operators. Our representation theorem characterizes a design space for discretization-stable operator learning; designing new architectures becomes a question of which parts of a Markov kernel network to parameterize, and how. We further show that the read kernels of these networks can be realized as solutions of regularized variational problems, with particular choices of costs and regularizers recovering the kernels of transformers, FNO, Flowers, their hybrids, and beyond. This variational formulation provides a systematic approach to architecture design, together with sufficient conditions for uniform discretization stability.

</details>

Abstract: Functional near-infrared spectroscopy (fNIRS) is a promising modality for brain-computer interfaces and cognitive state decoding, but progress in machine learning for fNIRS has been limited by small, isolated datasets and inconsistent evaluation protocols. We introduce fNIRSAtlas, the largest open benchmark for fNIRS classification to date, comprising 23 classification tasks from 19 public data collections, spanning diverse experimental paradigms, including motor execution, motor imagery, mental arithmetic, working memory, and emotion recognition. fNIRSAtlas provides a fully automated pipeline from raw data to evaluation and defines standardized benchmark tasks for both cross-subject and within-subject classification, ensuring reproducible comparison of methods. We evaluate a variety of baseline models from fNIRS and related neuroimaging and time-series machine learning literature on the benchmark. Our results demonstrate that performance varies substantially across tasks and that methods validated on limited data -- especially deep learning approaches -- often fail to generalize beyond their original evaluation. fNIRSAtlas serves as an extensible, open, and reproducible benchmark to support more robust and cumulative progress in machine learning for fNIRS data. Our code and data can be found at https://fnirsatlas.github.io.

Abstract: Sheet metal components often consist of a global geometry, such as the corner radii and forming depth of a corner component, and local features added to it. During forming, both affect material flow. A surrogate learning all their combinations may need large training data. However, the same local feature type is often used on many global geometries. This study therefore partly decouples local features from the global geometry. The local-feature effect is first learned on one global geometry, needing fewer simulations than learning all combinations, then transferred to other global geometries via transfer learning, which can need fewer target simulations than training from scratch. A baseline-conditioned residual surrogate, R-BiLoGo, is proposed for ribs on the side walls of hot-stamped corner components. Its inputs are the baseline response and the geometric perturbation introduced by the ribs. Its outputs are the changes in displacement and thinning from the baseline. R-BiLoGo gave lower errors than a hierarchical graph-based model and a Transolver-based model on the source geometry, and on two of three selected target geometries after transfer. Across the 22 target geometries, transfer learning with 20 target simulations gave errors similar to or lower than training from scratch with 80 target simulations for about half of the targets. With the same 80 target simulations, transfer learning reduced errors for 95% of targets. The effect of the global geometry on transfer was studied with four parameter-free geometric descriptors and controlled variations across 4 corner subclasses. The benefit of transfer decreased with increasing surface distance and high-curvature change between source and target, and for targets shallower than the source. These trends were also seen in other corner types with a different magnitude.

</details>

Abstract: LLM-based multi-agent systems solve complex tasks through collaboration, but their growing, simultaneously resident key-value (KV) caches create a memory bottleneck. Applying single-model quantization policies independently ignores differences in agents' precision needs, potentially degrading collective reasoning. We introduce Coordinated Mixed-Precision KV Cache Quantization (CoMixKV), which combines online sensitivity estimation with joint agent-layer bit-width allocation under a shared memory budget. To our knowledge, CoMixKV is the first mixed-precision KV-cache quantization framework to jointly allocate precision across simultaneously resident agent caches under such a budget. The estimator derives sensitivity from a local attention-error bound using recent queries, keys, and values, without calibration data, trial quantization, or additional model passes. A shared controller combines these scores with remaining quantizable capacity and byte-level storage costs to minimize total estimated error. Allocations are refreshed as agents start or finish and new observations become available. Selected widths apply only to newly quantized blocks; recent states retain native precision and previously quantized blocks remain unchanged. Across four benchmarks and three LLM backbones at average 2-bit, 3-bit, and 4-bit settings, CoMixKV achieves strong accuracy against the evaluated quantization baselines. At the average 3-bit setting on Gemma-4-31B-it, it achieves 68.35% average accuracy, exceeding the strongest quantized baseline by 0.59%. On Qwen3-32B, it reduces peak KV-cache memory by 32.08% and achieves a 1.38x inference speedup over FullKV.

</details>

Abstract: In clinical applications, patients undergo different combinations of tests, and machine learning models must therefore ensure accurate predictions across heterogeneous feature spaces. Current approaches upweight the patients with the highest loss, without accounting for the limited predictive information in their features preventing their loss from going any lower. Moreover, when patients' feature spaces are fully disjoint, current methods reduce to training independent models, preventing knowledge transfer across them. We propose a framework for group robust learning across heterogeneous feature spaces, where groups are patients sharing a feature space. Since a high loss may simply reflect uninformative features, we instead optimize for worst-group excess loss, the gap between a group's loss and an estimate of its achievable optimum. To allow collaboration between groups, we use group-specific encoders mapping into a shared latent space in which learnable Gaussian anchors cluster patients by outcome. On tabular and imaging medical datasets, including fully disjoint feature settings, our framework outperforms state-of-the-art methods when groups differ in their optimal losses or cannot train a good model on their own, reducing worst-group loss by up to 79% and excess loss by up to 95% with up to 18$\times$ fewer parameters.

Abstract: Neural additive models (NAMs) offer interpretability but lack expressivity due to their rigid additive structure, whereas multi-layer perceptrons (MLPs) achieve universal approximation at the cost of transparency. We establish the first theoretical foundation for projected neural additive models (PNAMs), which augment NAMs with a learnable linear transformation $\boldsymbol{T}$ to capture cross-variable interactions. While standard NAMs cannot approximate even the simplest interaction term $χ_1 χ_2$ because their cross-derivatives are identically zero, we prove that PNAMs are universal approximators and characterize the minimum number of projections required for exact polynomial representation. Furthermore, we derive an approximation rate of $\mathcal{O}(M^{-s / (N - 1)})$ for $\mathcal{C}^s$ functions (i.e., functions with $s$ continuous derivatives), where $M$ denotes the number of projections and $N$ the input dimension; this rate is known to be optimal for ridge function approximation. To recover interpretability from the projected inputs, we introduce structured sparsity regularizers and post-hoc symbolic regression techniques that enable global input ranking, parameter pruning, and the conversion of learned bases into compact analytical expressions. Experiments on knot theory invariants, MNIST, and phase field fracture mechanics demonstrate that PNAMs match or surpass similar architectures in accuracy while discovering salient features and yielding symbolic models with orders of magnitude fewer parameters.

Abstract: In quantum systems, physical quantities of interest can be expressed as expectation values of a probability distribution, which allows their estimation by Markov Chain Monte Carlo (MCMC) methods. This approach, an instance of Quantum Monte Carlo (QMC), plays a central role in obtaining predictions of system properties relevant to condensed matter and high energy physics. The use of Hamiltonian dynamics to design a Markov kernel, known as Hybrid or Hamiltonian Monte Carlo (HMC), is widespread in quantum applications. In this context, it is standard to use an HMC kernel that is ``adjusted'' with the Metropolis-Hastings (MH) criterion, so that estimates of expectations converge to their true value in the limit of infinite sample size. However, recent work in computational statistics suggests that an \emph{unadjusted} HMC kernel can be significantly more efficient, while safely maintaining an asymptotic bias which is small relative to the error arising from the variance of the finite sample size. We adapt this approach to the QMC setting, focusing on a challenging and high-dimensional \emph{spin-fermion} model of a quantum phase transition. We find that unadjusted methods outperform adjusted HMC in terms of effective sample size per gradient call, while retaining accurate results.

</details>

Abstract: Stochastic bilevel optimization provides a general framework for many machine learning applications, such as hyperparameter learning, data reweighting, language model alignment, and actor-critic reinforcement learning. However, most existing bilevel optimization methods treat matrix-valued gradients as flattened vectors and perform optimization in the resulting vectorized space, ignoring the matrix structures common in modern neural networks. Such limitations of existing bilevel optimization methods motivate us to develop BiMuon, a geometry-aware stochastic bilevel optimization method that integrates implicit hypergradient estimation in bilevel optimization with Muon-style orthogonalized updates at the UL. To our knowledge, BiMuon is the first stochastic BLO method with Muon-style orthogonalized matrix updates and an $O(ε^{-4})$ iteration-complexity guarantee, matching the best-known rates achieved by Muon-type methods based on matrix parameterizations. To improve computational and parallel efficiency for models with multiple matrix parameters, we further study BiMuon for blockwise diagonal matrix approximation and derive a relative stepsize allocation rule based on UL progress and blockwise LL sensitivity. Abstract: High-dimensional Bayesian optimisation (HDBO) is an increasingly crowded field with new methods proposed at every major conference, making it ever harder to answer a deceptively simple question: "Which method is currently state-of-the-art?" In this paper, we question the efficacy of benchmarking practices in the field, and find them to be systematically ineffective: multiple published works fail to benchmark against the strongest available baselines, or even include incorrect implementations of key methods. Without a shared reference implementation, such errors are difficult to detect during peer review. We therefore introduce BO-Arena, an actively maintained and extensible package providing canonical implementations of state-of-the-art algorithms, which allows researchers to develop new methods and benchmark them rigorously. Using BO-Arena, we find that what currently drives performance in HDBO is even simpler than previously thought. We use these insights to propose a new algorithm which is competitive with the state of the art on problems with many observations, without resorting to the complex GP approximations used in recent methods. Finally, we question whether there is currently sufficient evidence that any more complex method outperforms a simple Gaussian process baseline for HDBO.

Abstract: We demonstrate that transformer architectures trained with discrete denoising diffusion and decoded via guided test-time beam search can learn to recover the prime factors of semiprime integers directly from data. Our approach establishes a new frontier for learning-based semiprime factorization, successfully factoring 68-bit integers-a substantial advance over the prior 56-bit state of the art. While computationally less efficient than classical factoring algorithms, our model requires no domain-specific number-theoretic priors and learns purely from training examples. In practice, the diffusion model acts as a generative prior, identifying promising factor candidates where exact verification or lattice-based post-processing can complete the factorization. Furthermore, we derive empirical scaling laws relating approximation quality to training data volume, model size, and test-time computation. In contrast to language, problem size is the key driver of scaling behavior. Achieving a fixed accuracy on a held-out test set requires compute that scales exponentially with bit length. Consequently, our findings pose no threat to security, as the projected compute cost required to factorize cryptographically relevant integers remains prohibitive.

Abstract: In recent years, singular-spectrum-based optimizers have attracted increasing attention due to their ability to preserve the matrix structure of neural network gradients, thereby enabling geometry-aware optimization. However, most singular-spectrum-based (SSB) approaches remain centralized, limiting their use in distributed learning infrastructures that exploit massive parallelism. Distributed SSB optimization is still at an early stage, with open questions about convergence guarantees and communication efficiency. To fill this gap, we propose the Distributed Momentum-based Spectral Estimator (DisMUSE), an efficient distributed SSB optimization framework for both data-center and federated settings. Using Muon-style exact orthogonalized momentum as the spectral update, DisMUSE integrates a recursive variance-reduced momentum estimator and achieves an $O((KT)^{-1/3})$ convergence rate, where $K$ and $T$ denote the number of nodes and local iterations per node, respectively. This rate matches the best-known rates for variance-reduced nonconvex distributed optimization. Building on this variance-reduced framework, we study two extensions. First, our DisMUSE method with finite Newton-Schulz (NS) iterations preserves the same convergence order under a uniform normalized support-spectrum condition, up to a multiplicative factor. Second, we develop a communication-efficient variant based on low-rank (LR) momentum transmission and quantify the error it introduces to the stationarity bound, characterizing the trade-off between accuracy and communication cost.

Abstract: Neural operators learn mappings from PDE inputs, such as initial conditions and coefficient fields, to the corresponding solutions. Once trained, they can rapidly predict solutions for new inputs. However, obtaining input-solution pairs for supervised training often requires expensive numerical simulations, whereas PDE inputs and governing equations provide inexpensive physical information. This raises a question: how can cheap physical information reduce the need for labeled solutions? A natural approach is to use physics to select inputs for labeling, but our acquisition experiments show no consistent advantage over random sampling. This observation motivates exploring an alternative: using cheap physics to improve neural operator initialization before supervised training. We propose Physics-Informed Initialization (PI-Init), a simple yet effective method that pretrains neural operators on unlabeled inputs using physics-informed losses and retains the learned parameters for supervised fine-tuning on the labeled dataset. A linearized analysis shows that pretraining can improve downstream prediction accuracy by reducing initialization errors that limited labeled data cannot effectively correct. Experiments across diverse PDE benchmarks and neural operator architectures demonstrate improvements in predictive accuracy, out-of-distribution generalization, and label efficiency. Representative results show error reductions of up to 62.7% at the same label budget and comparable or better accuracy with 90% fewer labels, relative to supervised-only training.

Abstract: Bilevel optimization is a fundamental framework for machine learning problems, where an upper-level (UL) objective depends on the solution of a nested lower-level (LL) problem. Existing bilevel algorithms typically assume that the LL objective is available for evaluation, differentiation, or unrolling. However, this assumption can fail when the LL response is produced by a black-box solver, simulator, adversary, or learned predictor. In this paper, we study LL agnostic bilevel optimization, where the UL learner has access only to an approximate LL solution $\bar{\mathbf{y}}(\mathbf{x})$ and cannot query the LL objective, its gradients, Hessian/Jacobian information, or the procedure that generates the LL response. We propose LeGo-BiO, a coordinate-wise pseudo-hypergradient method that estimates the missing Jacobian $\nabla _{\mathbf{x}}\bar{\mathbf{y}}(\mathbf{x})$ using finite differences of $\bar{\mathbf{y}}(\cdot)$. For each UL coordinate, LeGo-BiO constructs a decision vector subject to 1-sparse updates, thereby isolating the corresponding Jacobian column needed in the UL gradient computation. This avoids the directional-projection limitation of full-parameter response differences and, unlike derivative-free/zeroth-order approximations of the entire UL gradient, preserves the available first-order gradient information with respect to the UL variables for more accurate UL gradient evaluation. We establish convergence guarantees for LeGo-BiO in both deterministic and stochastic settings under an LL error-bound/Polyak-Lojasiewicz-type condition, without requiring LL strong convexity. Our bounds yield an $O(T^{-1})$ rate in the deterministic setting and an $O(T^{-1/2})$ rate in the stochastic setting when the LL approximation error decreases geometrically. Experiments demonstrate competitive performance of LeGo-BiO despite being agnostic to the LL objective.

</details>

Abstract: The development of tabular foundation models (TFMs) has accelerated in recent years, outperforming traditional ML methods on many tabular benchmarks. A key finding is that TFMs can be pretrained entirely on synthetic datasets, opening opportunities to design data generators that encourage desirable model properties. Prior work has mainly focused on crafting high-quality priors over generators to improve overall pretraining performance. Our key insight is that parameterizing the generator distribution enables an adversarial, distributional robustness perspective: during training, we can adapt the generator to emphasize datasets that are particularly challenging for the model, which we formalize by introducing an optimality gap measure. Further, we develop a method to align the synthetic data generation with real-world datasets from a given domain, constraining the adversary to generate realistic data. Together, these algorithms comprise the Robust and Domain-Adapted Tabular Foundation Models (RAD-TFM) pipeline, a model-agnostic adversarial training framework. Applied to the TabPFN V2 classifier, RAD-TFM improves performance across 6 diverse tabular benchmarks, with up to a 10.8 percentage-point improvement in mean normalized AUC over the strongest baseline, using fewer than 100k additional synthetic training datasets, less than 0.1% of the original pretraining data. Applying the same pipeline to TabICL V2 also improves performance across three major benchmarks, demonstrating the generality of the framework. These results highlight a promising new direction for targeted adversarial continued training of TFMs using synthetic data alone.

Abstract: Linear State Space Models (SSMs) and linear autoregressive sequence models reuse the same parameters across a sample-dependent number of steps. As a result, different examples impose constraints through different predictors, even though all predictors share the same weights. We study this phenomenon through a general model of multiple non-homogeneous polynomial predictors with shared parameters, in a classification setting. Under standard interpolation and directional-convergence assumptions, together with a trajectory-dependent effective-degree condition, we show that gradient descent on the exponential loss converges in direction to a KKT point of an effective-degree max-margin problem. In this problem, only examples with minimal effective degree impose hard margin constraints; examples with faster-growing margins are asymptotically inactive except for feasibility.
  Our result provides a first-order characterization of the implicit bias in linear SSMs and linear autoregressive models. It exposes a mechanism of length bias: a longer input sequence or additional autoregressive steps may increase the example's effective degree, and the examples with the smallest effective degree dominate the limiting classifier. Our result highlights how non-homogeneity and parameter sharing alter classical homogeneous implicit-bias results.

</details>

Abstract: Long-horizon LLM agents accumulate sessions of up to millions of tokens, far more key-value (KV) cache than one GPU holds, so serving systems keep a small budget of tokens and evict the rest. Eviction methods differ in which tokens they keep but share one goal: that the tokens a later step needs are still cached. We show this is not enough. In 100K-token coding sessions on Qwen3-8B with a 2,048-token budget, a planner-guided policy that keeps over 99% of the needed tokens resident still fails late steps increasingly often: its accuracy falls from 93% to 64% as the gap between a definition and its use grows from 2K to 90K tokens, at residency 0.996. The needed tokens are resident but unretrievable: what grows is their rotary-position distance to the query. We present PACE (Planner-Aware Context Engine), a KV-cache engine that couples plan-aware retention, in which the agent's planner tells the cache which context future steps will read, with bounded positions, which renumber kept tokens so their rotary distance to the query stays below 8K at any session length. In a pre-registered evaluation on fresh tasks, PACE passes 97% of hidden unit tests at 100K tokens, against 42% for H2O and 3% for SnapKV under the same budget, and needs both components: either alone reaches 63% (the combination beats each, p < 10^-4). The result holds with length: in 1M- and 2M-token sessions on one 24 GB GPU, with definitions written 0.9M and 1.8M tokens before use, PACE passes 99% and 100% of checks and recalls every session fact, while bounded positions without foresight falls from 33% to 13%; PACE holds under 0.7 GB of KV state where the full cache would need 147-295 GB. Renumbering is exact, costs no measurable perplexity, and adds 9% wall time. On Mistral-7B, whose coding ability caps it at 40% even with nothing evicted, PACE stays at that ceiling while retention alone falls below it.

</details>

Abstract: Wireless foundation models learn reusable representations across tasks, configurations, and deployment environments. Their pretraining requires environmental breadth, yet wireless datasets often sample few sites densely. We introduce Channels4World, a scene-centered dataset of 100,000 distinct outdoor RF scenes and a procedural method for generating them. Each scene links a generated environment and RF material assignments to 32 base-station (BS) positions, 16,384 UE positions, and aligned variable-length multipath and ordered interaction records at 3.5 and 7~GHz. The corpus comprises 52.43 billion physical BS-UE pairs, 104.86 billion frequency-specific evaluations, and a 29.14-TB packaged payload. Scene identity aligns geometry, placements, channels, and interactions across frequencies, allowing environmental breadth and within-scene observation density to vary independently. The public release offers predefined subsets at multiple scales and separate access to corpus components. A deterministic 90K/10K scene-level train-test split keeps both frequencies and all scene components together. We characterize morphology, RF materials, and transceiver placement across three generation profiles. Controlled propagation and path-retention analyses show that diffraction materially affects retained-link availability and multipath structure, while the retention policy reduces storage substantially with little loss in represented aggregate power. Under matched training exposure, models trained on broader scene collections perform better on held-out scenes, although an equal-episode control shows that scene count alone cannot explain the gain. By making environmental breadth explicit and controllable, Channels4World supports the development and evaluation of wireless foundation models for generalization across deployment environments.

The practical lesson is to control the candidate support and the acquisition maximizer before attributing a gain to the surrogate.

Abstract: Transit-Oriented Development (TOD) is a key strategy for advancing sustainable urban development by promoting high-density, mixed-use land development within walking distance of public transit stations. However, traditional TOD planning often relies on heuristic methods, making it difficult to efficiently identify quantitatively optimized transit-oriented land use patterns. This paper presents DRL4TOD, a deep reinforcement learning framework that automatically optimizes land use types and floor area ratios (FARs) around transit stations in alignment with TOD principles. It jointly optimizes multiple objectives, including land use density, land use diversity, and transit capacity utilization, while minimizing reconstruction costs to account for development feasibility. Applied to two real-world station areas in Shanghai, DRL4TOD consistently outperforms rule-based approaches and genetic algorithms, achieving more effective TOD outcomes with lower reconstruction costs. By adjusting objective weights, the framework enables flexible prioritization, such as placing greater emphasis on cost minimization when financial feasibility is a concern. These results demonstrate DRL4TOD's potential as a decision-support tool for generating coordinated land-use and FAR configurations under planner-specified priorities, rather than prescribing a single fixed design. For policymakers, the framework provides parcel-level, constraint-aware alternatives that make trade-offs among competing objectives explicit and support targeted decisions about development intensity and feasibility.

Abstract: How many observations are needed to estimate a common rotation in two-dimensional overcomplete independent component analysis? We study equally spaced mixing columns with known source count, common source law, and column norm. For a fixed non-Gaussian fourth-order Gram-Charlier source law, the rotation is identifiable modulo the column spacing. Yet the sample size for fixed relative angular accuracy grows exponentially with the source count. We characterize this sample size, up to absolute constants, by the reciprocal of the squared Hellinger distance between two rotations separated by half the column spacing. An explicit radially weighted phase estimator attains this scale uniformly over the rotation family, using the observation radius to prevent cancellation in the mean of an angular harmonic. We also determine the exact exponential decay rate of single-observation total variation for the same pair. This gives bounds on the sample size exponent; its exact value remains open. Finally, a noisy binary-source example shows how an exact zero line of the observed characteristic function can have an exponentially small normal derivative.

Abstract: Stabilizing long-horizon reasoning requires intelligent agents to sustain useful progress across successive reasoning steps and tool interactions. Such agents are typically trained with reinforcement learning (RL), where actor-critic RL uses a learnable critic network to estimate step advantages, while critic-free methods such as GRPO eliminate critic models and rely mostly on the group variants of sparse outcome-only rewards. Both reward types leave partially useful failed trajectories either implicit or indistinguishable from entirely unproductive ones, and as reasoning horizons grow, it becomes difficult to maintain consistent reasoning trajectories. In this work, we propose a \textit{plug-and-play verifier} framework for stabilizing long-horizon reasoning through explicit verification of intermediate reasoning artifacts. Specifically, the framework decouples process verification through a unified interface that extracts intermediate reasoning artifacts from agent running trajectories, evaluates them using domain-specific verifiers, and aggregates the verification scores as bounded process reward. Then, we combine our dense verified process rewards and the sparse outcome rewards through a two-stage post-training schedule. We showed that with plug-and-play verifiers, we further stabilize the reasoning steps as last-mile self-reflection and self-consistency. Typical evaluation on HotpotQA using Qwen3.5-4B achieves a superior accuracy (60.18\%), outperforming the standard GRPO baseline (57.73\%). Our results suggest that plug-and-play verifiers provide a modular approach to learn more stable and reliable reasoning behaviors over long horizons.

Abstract: Evaluations of LLM forecasters increasingly measure how forecasts improve as richer evidence is supplied. We identify two confounds in this update-slope metric. First, outcome-aware evidence construction substantially inflates measured responsiveness: on the same models and questions, slopes are roughly four times larger than with resolution-blinded evidence ladders. Second, update slope is a change score with a prior-dependent structural ceiling, so raw comparisons can reflect differences in starting calibration rather than updating behavior. The ceiling defines only a feasible region; whether slope and prior calibration are empirically coupled depends on headroom utilization and varies across our Manifold, ForecastBench, and ACLED pools. Despite this variability, conditioning on prior calibration roughly triples the association between intermediate updating and final Brier score under both blinded and outcome-aware construction. Question identity also explains substantially more variance than model identity across the evaluated pools. We recommend building evidence ladders blind to outcomes, conditioning on prior calibration, and reporting headroom-normalized measures alongside raw slopes.

Abstract: Retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) can surface passages that support a claim, contradict it, or fail to address it, yet existing calibrated grounding methods are typically one-sided and model only supporting evidence. We introduce Conformal Evidence Sets (CES), a two-sided framework that returns separate support and refutation sets with finite-sample inclusion coverage of at least $1-α$ on each side under exchangeability; a Bonferroni union yields joint coverage of at least $1-2α$. On labeled candidate pools from the contrastive VitaminC benchmark, CES closely tracks nominal coverage across $α\!\in\!\{0.05,0.10,0.15,0.20,0.30\}$, with maximum deviations of $3.0$ percentage points for support and $5.4$ for refutation, and reaches essentially the same empirical coverage--size frontier as a test-tuned fixed-threshold heuristic without any test exposure. Stress tests mark the boundary of that guarantee: under FEVER distribution shift both sides under-cover, by up to $12.7$ percentage points, and support-side group-stratified recalibration substantially reduces the support gap. Scorer swaps, hard-negative retrieval, cross-dataset transfer, and a live end-to-end pilot expose a clear separation: when exchangeability holds and relevant evidence is retrieved, evidence inclusion remains calibrated even with an imperfect scorer, whereas the support/refute verdict tracks scorer quality directly. Calibrated evidence coverage and reliable verdicts are therefore distinct properties and should be reported separately.

Abstract: Many practical anomaly detection problems come with only a handful of confirmed anomalies, and the unreviewed remainder of the data cannot be trusted to be normal. We study this setting under a curated-label protocol, in which unconfirmed anomalies are removed from training rather than relabelled as normal, and propose TripletAD, a weakly supervised detector that learns a triplet-margin embedding and calibrates a linear scoring head with two soft penalties: a partial cross-entropy on labelled anomalies and a relative-contrast hinge that separates the mean scores of anomalous and normal samples. Because every mined triplet contains a labelled anomaly, the few labels are reused across the entire training set instead of being diluted by it. On 24 ADBench datasets whose inputs are pre-trained embeddings (text, image and speech vectors), TripletAD outperforms established state-of-the-art weakly supervised detectors (DevNet, PReNet and DeepSAD) and gradient-boosted trees when anomalies are rare. At a 1% anomaly rate it has the highest mean AUCPR of six methods, 20% above DevNet; on the naturally rare Speech dataset it doubles the best baseline (0.394 vs. 0.196 AUCPR); and on MVTec-AD it is best when only a quarter or half of the anomalies are labelled. Pooled over 70 rare- or sparse-label settings, it has the best average rank (2.21 of 6) and outperforms each baseline under Wilcoxon signed-rank tests with Holm correction. When anomalies are more frequent it performs on par with these methods. The method needs no per-dataset tuning and is insensitive to its loss weights and training length; on raw tabular features, gradient-boosted trees remain the stronger choice.

</details>

Abstract: Graph Neural Networks (GNNs) are an important tool for fMRI prediction, where empirical functional connectivity matrices serve both as features and for graph construction. However, recent studies and our own experiments show that these methods often fail to outperform simpler graph-agnostic baselines. We propose a statistical explanation for this failure by formulating connectome prediction as an errors-in-variables (EIV) problem: the true connectome is latent, while the observed covariance matrix is a noisy finite-time-series estimate. In a linear asymptotic setting, we decompose the effect of graph convolution into three mechanisms: smoothing-induced bias and variance reduction, graph estimation error, and a double-dipping effect arising when the same noisy matrix determines both graph and features. Under a latent community model adapted to neuroscience, we identify a narrow favorable regime, set by the brain community structure, the predictive signal, the graph construction and the recording length, in which message passing can improve prediction. Simulations validate this decomposition, while real-data experiments illustrate that connectome GNN pipelines often fall outside this regime. Our results provide a first step towards explaining when graph convolutions can help brain connectome prediction, and why they often do not.

Abstract: Crystal Structure Prediction (CSP) aims to predict a material's 3D structure from its chemical composition, with applications from energy storage to biomedical devices. Despite its importance, existing evaluation frameworks suffer from key limitations: they rely on threshold-sensitive or incomparable metrics, focus predominantly on geometric performance while overlooking physical plausibility, and fail to account for polymorphism, where a chemical composition can crystallize into multiple stable structures. In parallel, recent Symmetry-Informed CSP (SICSP) methods often report results alongside standard CSP baselines while receiving crystallographic templates at inference, which constrain the search space and make SICSP a fundamentally different task. To address these limitations, we introduce an Orientation-invariant, Polymorph-Aware, Large Benchmark (OPAL-Bench) that evaluates CSP and SICSP as separate tasks. We evaluate 7 CSP and 2 SICSP methods under OPAL-Bench across 4 datasets using threshold-free geometric and physical distance metrics. Our results show that no method dominates across all metrics, and that geometric performance is not strongly correlated with physical plausibility. We also release OPAL-CSP, an SE(3)-equivariant flow-matching baseline for the CSP track that performs competitively on geometric metrics and ranks among the best on physical ones. By unifying geometric and physical metrics under a polymorph-aware protocol, OPAL-Bench enables a more comprehensive assessment of generative quality in CSP.

</details>

Abstract: Data pruning can substantially reduce training cost by removing examples that contribute little additional learning signal, but it has not been studied for DNA language-model pretraining. We introduce Sequence Pruning (SP), a dynamic pruning framework that removes genomic sequences as they become easy for the model to predict. SP uses per-sequence pretraining accuracy computed directly from the model's forward pass, requiring no separate scoring model or additional pass over the corpus. We evaluate SP across five pretraining settings spanning two corpora, three model families, and both MLM and CLM objectives, with downstream evaluation on five genomic benchmarks. SP reduces pretraining data processing by up to 96% while largely preserving downstream performance. At moderate savings, pruning improves performance by up to 1.2 MCC points over full-data pretraining; at the highest savings, pretraining uses only 4% of the baseline GPU hours with little performance loss. SP also outperforms the dynamic pruning methods InfoBatch and BPAS across most comparisons. Beyond accuracy, our framework supports alternative criteria such as perplexity and EL2N, although accuracy yields the strongest downstream performance at matched savings. These results show that genomic pretraining does not require repeatedly processing all available sequences and that dynamic sequence-level pruning can substantially improve its data and computational efficiency. Abstract: Supervised fine-tuning over a large pool of heterogeneous tasks is often computationally expensive, leading practitioners to train language models on a fixed training budget of carefully chosen SFT tasks. Thus, achieving strong downstream performance hinges on selecting an effective subset of tasks, or equivalently, identifying an optimal task mixture under a fixed budget constraint. In practice, supervised finetuning mixtures are often determined using simple heuristics (e.g., uniform or size-proportional sampling) that ignore task interactions, which can hurt transfer and waste budget on redundant task sources. We introduce TASKMIXPGM, a framework for learning continuous task mixtures through energy-based modelling over SFT tasks. Each task forms a node in a Markov random field, with unary potentials capturing task utility and pairwise potentials modelling inter-task relationships via behavioral divergences between predictive distributions of single-task fine-tuned models. Optimizing this objective yields task mixtures that balance utility, coverage, and redundancy. We further show that the induced set function defined over the set of such tasks is weakly submodular under budget constraints, enabling approximation guarantees for discrete task selection. Empirically, we provide a fixed, benchmark-independent mixture that improves over heuristic mixing across multiple model families and different training budgets. TASKMIXPGM also uncovers interpretable structure in task interactions, revealing complementarities and redundancies that inform effective SFT mixtures.

</details>

Abstract: Estimating the principal eigenpair of a positive operator from stochastic evaluations gives rise to a noisy form of normalized power iteration: each step applies a sampled operator estimate and then renormalizes. We establish finite-time stochastic-approximation guarantees for this procedure, working in Hilbert's projective metric, which is well-suited to the multiplicative geometry of Perron--Frobenius eigenproblems. Our main application is risk-sensitive average-cost reinforcement learning (RL) with exponential utility. In this setting the Bellman equations are multiplicative, and value functions are characterized by nonlinear eigenvalue problems rather than additive fixed points. Hanner matrices give lower bounds of the same order, for square matrices and for explicit families with unequal logarithmic aspect ratios. The upper bound is obtained by constructing nested acyclic sets in the faces of a cube. A critical-point estimate supplies highly connected regions, weighted cell attachments keep the sets compatible across faces, and a cubical carrier theorem converts them into accurate finite covers and learning rules. Appending one constant coordinate turns list replicability into an exact geometric quantity, the face width, and graph-orientation classes satisfy exact product formulas. In particular, there are explicit classes with list replicability $4k+1$ and equivariant index $3k$, so the additive gap between the two is unbounded. We also develop entropy-tilting and two-sided Gibbs constructions, give combinatorial upper bounds adapted to structured matrices, compute exact values on classical families, and study the index of Paley matrices.

We cast it as a sequential decision problem on a 3-dimensional generalised map: an agent splits edges, faces and volumes with three insert operations until every cell is a hexahedron and the mesh passes a quality threshold, and a policy that convolves over the map's involutions chooses each insert. A successful decomposition takes over a hundred decisions, and a uniformly random policy never finds one. We clone a policy from constructed decompositions and improve it by expert iteration. On held-out chamfered solids that share no boundary topology with the cloning data, the final policy solves an expected $94.4\%$ in $20$ samples, and expert iteration raises the best-of-20 solve rate on large chamfered solids from $0.203$ to $0.355$. At matched compute, PPO, GRPO and VinePPO updates all make the cloned policy worse.

Abstract: Membership inference attacks (MIAs) aim to determine whether a sample was used during the training of a target model. Strong MIAs rely on reference (or shadow) models to estimate target-conditional score distributions, but training such ensembles is computationally expensive for modern large-scale Vision--Language Models (VLMs). In this work, we propose a scalable alternative that replaces explicit reference-model training with a Bayesian approximation over low-rank adaptation parameters. We instantiate this approach using Stochastic Weight Averaging Gaussian (SWAG), enabling efficient approximation of target-conditional score distributions at a fraction of the cost of conventional shadow-model ensembles. To isolate the effect of pretraining knowledge, we additionally introduce a controlled synthetic dataset. Our approach outperforms standard reference-based attacks while requiring only a single reference-model fine-tuning run, making membership inference more practical for large VLMs.

Abstract: 1-bit key-value (KV) cache compression can combine quantizer calibration with selective high-precision storage to reduce reconstruction error. We propose Calibration and Fisher-guided Anchors for KV cache compression (CALFA-KV). We also estimate channel-wise Fisher-type sensitivities from squared gradients of a reference next-token cross-entropy loss. Online, we use these sensitivities to weight the squared reconstruction errors of each KV state. Here, a KV state is one token's key-value pair within one layer and KV head. We retain the highest-scoring states at high precision under a per-layer, per-KV-head budget. Under exact restoration, we bound the excess risk of score-based anchor selection relative to the optimum at the same anchor budget, where risk is the conditional mean square of the first-order loss change. With a budget of 56 additional FP16 anchors per layer and KV head, CALFA-KV (d8b8) outperforms all evaluated nominal 1-bit baselines on the mean score across seven benchmarks for each of the three models. Abstract: Diffusion Transformers (DiTs) have established themselves as the preeminent paradigm for high-fidelity generative modeling across various modalities, yet their practical deployment is hindered by the severe computational overhead of iterative sampling. While recent feature-caching methods mitigate this latency through feature reuse or forecasting based on integer-order operators, they remain limited in adapting to the non-stationary and highly curved dynamics of DiT latent trajectories. In this work, we reveal that DiT feature evolution exhibits anomalous diffusion characteristics and a certain degree of spatiotemporal heterogeneity, rendering static, integer-order forecasting operators inadequate and prone to error accumulation. To address this, we propose Fractional Anomalous Dynamics Extrapolation (FADE), a unified framework for training-free diffusion transformers acceleration. FADE models DiT feature evolution via anomalous diffusion and employs a Mittag--Leffler fractional operator to better adapt to high-curvature and non-linear latent trajectories of DiTs. Leveraging the empirical observation that these complex dynamics maintain cross-sample stability, we further construct a Look-Up Table with multiple dimensions through offline profiling. This enables negligible LUT-retrieval overhead, spatiotemporally adaptive parameter retrieval during online inference. Extensive evaluations across diverse architectures and modalities demonstrate that FADE achieves state-of-the-art acceleration, preserving exceptional generation fidelity under aggressive skip intervals with negligible latency overhead. Abstract: Off-policy evaluation (OPE) estimates the expected return of a target policy from previously collected data without additional environment interaction. While OPE methods for flat Markovian policies are well studied, little work has addressed evaluating hierarchical policies in which a high-level policy selects temporally extended options that generate primitive actions until a termination condition is met. The primary existing approach applies per-decision importance sampling at the option level, but requires option-annotated trajectories and exhibits variance that still grows with the horizon. For non-hierarchical policies, fitted Q-evaluation (FQE) typically achieves lower error than importance sampling by leveraging the classic Bellman equation for policy evaluation; however, a naive extension of FQE to hierarchical policies results in a biased policy value estimate. In this paper, we introduce Intra-Option Fitted Q-Evaluation (IO-FQE), which estimates a value function in the augmented state-option space; this approach enables OPE of hierarchical policies even when we lack annotations of what option was run in the data. We develop two continuous-action instantiations of IO-FQE and show on long-horizon hierarchical continuous control tasks (AntMaze navigation and OGBench Puzzle manipulation) that IO-FQE eliminates the bias incurred by a naive application of FQE to hierarchical policies and substantially lowers mean squared-error compared to option-level importance sampling.

</details>

Abstract: Learning with noisy labels has been extensively studied for classification, yet its regression counterpart remains relatively underexplored. Unlike classification, regression involves continuous-valued targets where label noise exhibits heterogeneous magnitudes across instances, which poses conflicting requirements on the model: correcting small-to-moderate deviations requires sensitivity to label values for instance-level refinement, whereas large deviations caused by corrupted or outlying annotations render exact label values unreliable, necessitating reduced reliance on exact labels and instead leveraging ordinal structure among samples. These requirements call for methods that capture both global structure and sample-specific deviations in a unified manner. To this end, we propose GRAIN, a dual-granularity framework for robust noisy regression. At the fine-grained level, we derive an explicit residual learning mechanism from a robust loss formulation, introducing instance-specific auxiliary variables to decouple label noise from predictive signals. At the coarse-grained level, we introduce a binning-based contrastive regularization that enforces an order-preserving feature structure aligned with label proximity, addressing noise beyond the capacity of local residual refinement. The two levels are coupled via a histogram-based bridging mechanism that enables bidirectional information flow: coarse-grained structural guidance establishes a well-ordered feature space that anchors residual learning, while fine-grained residual refinement corrects instance-level deviations that structural regularization alone cannot resolve. Experimental results show that GRAIN consistently improves robustness and generalization under diverse noise conditions.

</details>

Abstract: Meta-reinforcement learning (meta-RL) trains agents to adapt to novel tasks from test-time experience, but requires diverse training environments and reward functions. While environments are becoming cheaper to generate, it remains difficult to anticipate every objective an agent may be asked to pursue at test time. Behavior Foundation Models (BFMs) learn policies from reward-free data and support zero-shot RL (ZSRL): adaptation to new objectives at test time using a large reward-labeled dataset. Recent work has shown that BFM task inference can instead be performed online without prior task-specific data. We note that this capability positions BFMs and meta-RL as alternative approaches for online adaptation to new reward functions in a fixed environment. We then ask whether we can extend BFMs to compete with meta-RL more broadly, including in novel environments, and identify four challenges: environment identification, online data collection, test-time exploration, and learning from mixed reward supervision. We study each individually, then propose MetaBFM, a hybrid method that addresses all four. During training, it combines supervised reward-following with unsupervised learning; at test time, it can interpolate between exploration, exploitation, and ZSRL-style reward inference. We demonstrate that MetaBFM can recover the in-distribution performance of supervised meta-RL while generalizing to reward functions outside the training set. Our work suggests that BFMs and meta-RL are complementary research directions whose combined strengths allow adaptive agents to learn more diverse behaviors from a fixed set of supervised objectives.

</details>

Abstract: Mechanism-AI coupled dynamical systems are increasingly used to study complex biological systems. However, uncertainty associated with noisy, sparse, and partially observed data limits their reliability. This paper presents a systematic mathematical formulation for treating these uncertainties from the perspective of optimal transport and information geometry. We formulate the uncertainty quantification problem as the minimization of an energy functional on a probability density space equipped with a Riemannian metric, with the loss evaluated through a differential equation, and give a unified description of the gradient flows of the energy functional under specific metrics, such as the Wasserstein and Fisher-Rao metrics. Furthermore, we present corresponding numerical methods for solving these gradient flows using abstract adjoint sensitivity analysis. The formulation connects a range of Bayesian inference methods for dynamical systems; numerical experiments compare posterior approximation and predictive uncertainty under a common computational budget. Abstract: Reliable forecasting of Municipal Solid Waste (MSW) production is pivotal for sustainable urban management, as it enables efficient resource planning, optimized collection schedules, and reduced environmental impacts. Existing works on MSW forecasting have some limitations, such as unavailability of granular data from lower administrative units, small datasets, limited or sometimes no consideration of important factors that can affect MSW generation, including holidays, weekends, seasonality, changes in temperature or humidity.

A detailed factor-wise analysis is conducted to examine waste generation behaviour considering holidays, weekday-weekend variations, seasonal patterns, temperature and humidity. The results show lower waste generation during holidays and weekends, higher generation during Summer and Winter, and comparatively lower generation during Monsoon and Post-Monsoon. Temperature and humidity do not exhibit a direct linear relationship with waste generation, although their seasonal variations are associated with the changes in waste generation across different seasons. All model performances are evaluated using Mean Absolute Error (MAE), Mean Absolute Percentage Error (MAPE), Root Mean Squared Error (RMSE), and the coefficient of determination (R2). Among the evaluated models, LSTM and SARIMAX achieve the best overall forecasting performance.

Abstract: Reproducible evaluation, verifiable inference and on-policy reinforcement learning (RL) need an LLM engine to give a request the same output bits whichever requests share its batch. Batch-invariant kernels exist, but engines still select each kernel and its parameters from the batch. Two kernels that compute the same sum can round it differently. The engines' deterministic modes remove the choice by keeping one conservative kernel per operator and pay for it in latency. Many fast kernels are batch-invariant by themselves, so it is enough to fix the few batch-dependent values that kernel selection reads. Detent expresses kernel selection as a program that the engine executes. It finds the fewest values in this program that must be fixed and tunes them for the workload before deployment. On multi-GPU deployments of Llama-3.1-70B and Qwen3-30B-A3B in vLLM and SGLang, Detent is batch-invariant. Tuned per load, it serves at 1.04-1.17x the latency per output token of Stock (each engine with its standard settings), against 1.67-10.3x for the deterministic modes. In SGLang it matches the GSM8K accuracy of SGLang's deterministic mode. Detent covers multiple deployment combinations, including multiple engines, GPU generations, tensor-parallel degrees, and dense and MoE models. Seeded RL rollouts of models up to Llama-3.1-70B replay bit for bit under other batches and cache splits. A trainer on Detent's kernels and values matches every rollout logprob bit for bit at 1.03x Stock's rollout time per token. BF16 GRPO then stays stable where Stock's run collapses.

</details>

Abstract: Medication recommendation must combine patient-specific treatment evidence with the interaction structure of a prescription. ### [176] [What Do Collusion Probes Read? Three Pitfalls in Evaluating Multi-Agent Collusion Monitors](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.10620)
*Mainul Hasan Mohsin,Mohammad Shahidur Rahman*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The study shows activation‑probe monitors mainly detect collusion instructions rather than true collusion decisions. Even so, probes still detect real collusion without instructions and when collusion is fine‑tuned. A new testing protocol and online monitoring tool are introduced.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To scrutinize whether current activation‑probe based monitors truly capture a language model agent’s decision to collude or merely detect that it was instructed to collude, thereby assessing the reliability of collusion monitoring.

Method: The authors created a benchmark with 2,500 committee and 1,476 code‑review episodes, including agents that receive a collusion instruction but refuse it, thereby separating instruction from decision. They evaluated existing activation probes (detectors) and developed their own probes, measuring AUROC scores with and without the instruction. They also analyzed pitfalls such as post‑vote scoring and peripheral public arguments. Moreover, they introduced a control protocol and adapted scan statistics for online group monitoring.

Result: Existing detectors perform poorly at distinguishing agent instruction from spontaneous collusion (AUROC drops from 1.00 to 0.61 when instruction omitted). A probe trained solely on agents’ own choices still reaches AUROC 0.73 without the instruction. Probes can identify colluders whose collusion objective is fine‑tuned into their weights with AUROC 0.995. The study presents a testing protocol and an online scan‑statistic monitoring method for arbitrary group sizes.

Conclusion: Activation probes can detect collusion decisions in LLM agents, but only when instructed to collude; they largely rely on reading the collusion instruction rather than the agent’s decision. Even so, probes can still detect genuine collusion when no instruction is present, especially for agents whose collusion goal is fine‑tuned into the weights. A standardized testing protocol and online monitoring algorithm are proposed for collusion detection.

Abstract: Activation probes look like near-perfect monitors for secret collusion among large language model (LLM) agents. We show that they mostly detect that an agent was told to collude, not that it decided to. Our benchmark (2,500 committee and 1,476 code-review episodes) adds agents who receive the collusion instruction and refuse it, separating instruction from decision. Published detectors barely tell colluders from these refusers, and our best probe separates them mostly by reading the instruction: without it, agent AUROC falls from 1.00 to 0.61. Two further pitfalls inflate reported results: scored after the vote, a rule that only reads the votes reaches AUROC 0.990 but finds nothing before it; and in 57% of attempts no colluder argues for the target in public. The decision is still there: a probe trained on agents' own choices reads it (AUROC 0.73 without the instruction), and probes find colluders whose goal was fine-tuned into the weights (AUROC 0.995; pre-registered). We turn the controls into a protocol for testing any collusion monitor, and adapt scan statistics into an online monitor for groups of any size.

</details>

Abstract: Neural methods for graph-structured optimization have achieved strong performance within individual problem classes, but are typically trained for fixed formulations, limiting reuse across related problem variants. Recent progress in foundation models has motivated shared neural backbones trained across multiple graph-structured optimization tasks; however, existing approaches primarily share instance-level representations while treating feasibility constraints implicitly. Constraint specifications are parsed into operator graphs and encoded using a graph neural network to produce invariant constraint embeddings, which condition a shared backbone model for solution prediction. Abstract: Sparse attention accelerates long-context decoding by reading a subset of the KV cache, but existing methods are open-loop: the kernel commits to its selection with no signal indicating whether enough attention mass was preserved and no recovery path when it was not. The needed signal already exists inside the kernel. The fraction of softmax mass on the selected tokens, which we call coverage, satisfies an exact algebraic equality with per-step output error and serves a dual role as the acceptance criterion and the interpolation weight for recovery. Coverage-Verified Sparse Attention (CVSA) turns coverage into a training-free, closed-loop verify-then-correct decode loop that accepts high-coverage drafts and recovers only the heads that fall short, with no sampling, no draft model, and no speculative-decoding infrastructure. Quality varies by only $0.14$ points across the threshold sweep; budget and scheduling ablations characterize the deployed operating point. Retaining these transforms online avoids cross-layer dependencies and enables a simple streaming implementation of the quantization process, in which only one layer must reside in memory at a time. To improve over round-to-nearest quantization, we introduce Flip-Refinement, a Hessian-aware coordinate-descent procedure that greedily moves individual FP4 codes to adjacent representable values. Despite executing the activation transforms at inference time, MACaQ adds only 2.3% end-to-end overhead over pure MXFP4 on Qwen3-32B in vLLM. MACaQ reaches lower perplexity than DuQuant++ and MR-GPTQ, and already matches their best perplexity on Llama-3-8B in 11 times less quantization time. We further apply MACaQ without architecture-specific modifications to an image diffusion model, illustrating the generality of its layer-local design and show quantization of a 122B parameter model in 64 minutes, using only 32.5GB GPU RAM.

Abstract: Continual learning suffers from catastrophic forgetting, leading to the loss of past knowledge and unstable adaptation across task streams. Existing approaches typically address this issue through replay buffers, architectural changes, or additional regularization terms. We argue that forgetting is a consequence of unstable learning dynamics, which may benefit from an optimizer-level solution. The main challenge is that learning dynamics are time-dependent, as demonstrated by empirical phenomena such as the stability gap. First, we formally prove that, for distance-based classifiers, bounded parameter drift guarantees bounded margin erosion under a local score-stability condition. Based on this insight, we propose STABLE, a primal-dual optimizer with adaptive drift control. STABLE enforces a drift budget by increasing the dual variable when the budget is violated and decreasing it otherwise. This results in high stability without penalizing plasticity. Experiments on long-horizon unimodal streams and CLIP-based multimodal streams show that vanilla PEFT baselines trained with STABLE match or outperform specialized continual learning methods. In the multimodal setting, STABLE further preserves zero-shot transfer, matching or exceeding dedicated multimodal continual learning methods.

Abstract: Discovering causal structures in multivariate time series (MTS) is critical in domains such as finance and neuroscience, yet remains challenging in practice due to three distinct obstacles. First, MTS are routinely corrupted by missing values, where naive impute-then-discover pipelines distort the underlying data distribution and yield spurious causal graphs. Second, identifying instantaneous effects is difficult even on complete data, and missingness further entangles imputation with graph recovery. Third, real-world data frequently exhibits heteroscedastic noise with variance driven by both instantaneous and lagged causes, violating the core assumptions of standard discovery methods. Existing approaches fail to address these challenges concurrently, typically presuming complete data, homoscedastic noise, or strictly time-lagged relationships. Crucially, we show through ablation studies that resolving these issues sequentially, rather than jointly, leads to a substantial degradation in performance. To overcome this, we introduce Cheesefill, an optimal transport framework for causal structure learning under missingness and heteroscedastic noise. Cheesefill parameterizes causal mechanisms using conditional normalizing flows and jointly learns a stochastic correction map that refines naive imputations into trajectories consistent with the inferred causal mechanisms. Theoretically, we prove that optimizing over stochastic correction maps is equivalent to a conditional kernel reformulation of the Kantorovich problem, from which our neural parameterization yields a tractable objective. Extensive experiments on synthetic and real-world benchmarks demonstrate that Cheesefill consistently outperforms existing baselines across diverse missingness regimes.

</details>

Abstract: Safe offline reinforcement learning (RL) seeks to learn policies that maximize return while satisfying safety constraints, using a static dataset. To characterize state-wise safety, reachability analysis identifies the feasible region, defined as the set of states from which constraints can be satisfied at every future step. Policies are commonly extracted from learned value functions with advantage-weighted regression (AWR). However, AWR relies on exponentiated weights that amplify value estimation errors, whereas behavior-regularized policy gradient methods directly utilize the action gradient of the value function and often make more effective use of learned critics. We propose Feasibility-Optimized Conditional Actor Learning (FOCAL), a method that incorporates behavior-regularized policy gradients into a conditional variational autoencoder (CVAE) actor. FOCAL optimizes this policy using a first-order objective partitioned by feasibility, updating the actor to increase return on transitions estimated to be feasible and to reduce constraint violations on transitions estimated to be infeasible. During inference, FOCAL dynamically adjusts the scale of the latent prior according to the estimated feasibility of the state to adjust the conservatism of action sampling. Theoretically, we show that for AWR, the policy update variance depends exponentially on the advantage error, whereas for FOCAL it scales linearly with the variance of the value estimation error. Across the DSRL benchmark, FOCAL satisfies the constraints on all evaluated tasks while achieving competitive or higher returns compared to baselines.

</details>

Abstract: We develop a variational and geometric theory based on statistical physics for the joint evolution of Transformer attention and token representations at fixed model parameters. To this end, we introduce a free energy for the attention and model the evolution of the attention weights by its Fisher--Rao gradient flow. At fixed token representations, we prove that the softmax matrix is the unique free-energy minimiser and that every positive attention trajectory converges to it. For prescribed time-dependent representations, we show that the attention dynamics becomes linear after a change to logit variables. This result yields tracking bounds relative to the instantaneous softmax and sufficient conditions for attention to converge to a fixed matrix. Hamiltonian dynamics for the representations and their momenta are then coupled to the attention relaxation driven by the same energy. This coupling results in an energy-dissipation law. We also develop two alternative Hamiltonians incorporating the attention value map. We prove global well-posedness of the attention flow and all three coupled systems.

Abstract: Neural networks trained by gradient-based methods learn the low-frequency content of a target function faster than its high-frequency content. This spectral bias is well documented, but it is much less clear which architectural choices mitigate it and which cannot. We study the question for shallow ReLU models linear in their trainable parameters, where each mode is learned at a rate given by a Gram-matrix eigenvalue, and for a general symmetric bilinear form $a(\cdot,\cdot)$, so that least-squares regression ($a=$ the $L^2$ inner product) and variational or physics-informed losses ($a=$ the $H^1$ energy form) are two instances of one analysis.
  Within this framework we propose gated local ReLU dictionaries, obtained by multiplying each ReLU feature by a hat partition-of-unity weight, in the spirit of partition-of-unity finite elements and overlapping Schwarz methods. The resulting Gram matrices are block-banded for any local bilinear form, with a block-Toeplitz interior whose matrix-valued symbol governs the spectrum.
  The two forms then behave very differently. For $L^2$ regression, the symbol is bounded away from zero, the ratio is uniform in the number of windows, and the measured spread of frequency-dependent learning rates falls from $1.9\times10^{5}$ to $4.5$ at matched model size. For the $H^1$ stiffness matrix, the symbol vanishes at zero frequency: gating leaves the $Θ(N^2)$ conditioning intact and inverts the frequency ordering instead of removing the bias. A preconditioner built from the same interior stencil will fix the spectral bias, showing that architecture alone is not enough. Experiments on $1$D regression and Poisson problems confirm the dichotomy, and the regression effect persists when the hidden-layer parameters are trained as well.

</details>
