Abstract: Abductive reasoning is the inference of plausible explanations for observed evidence. Evaluating this capability requires more than a single task: a system may need to generate an explanation, discriminate among competing hypotheses, revise its hypotheses as evidence arrives, or decide what evidence to seek next. Yet these capabilities are typically evaluated through isolated benchmarks with different task formulations and scoring procedures, so claims that a model is ``good at abduction'' may reflect only a narrow aspect of this capability. We introduce AbductionBench, a unified framework whose taxonomy characterizes abductive tasks. We instantiate this framework on 27 datasets spanning ten domains and evaluate eight LLMs from four model families evaluated on single-choice selection tasks. Beyond harmonizing heterogeneous tasks, AbductionBench separates final-answer evaluation from analysis of the observable reasoning traces and interaction histories produced alongside those answers. We analyze written traces as observable model outputs rather than faithful representations of internal computation, characterizing evidence use, hypothesis comparison, uncertainty, contradiction handling, and answer commitment. Jev matches or exceeds small LLMs when selecting among explanations over compact evidence but falls substantially behind on longer cases, suggesting that candidate discrimination and extended evidence integration place different demands on a system. In interactive tasks, the proportion of actions judged relevant declines at later steps, and a relevant first question is associated with a correct final answer; in the passive task, models rarely revise their initial hypothesis as new evidence arrives. Together, these findings highlight the value of evaluating abductive reasoning through both final answers and observable evidence use, hypothesis revision, and information gathering.

</details>

Abstract: Anomaly detection plays a critical role in securing the Internet of things (IoT). Although existing federated principal component analysis (PCA) methods can learn the subspace of normal samples without sharing raw data, they often suffer from feature redundancy that may degrade detection performance. To address this limitation, we propose a novel and effective federated sparse Grassmann Newton (FedSGN) learning method for IoT anomaly detection. Abstract: Under power-law signal decay across directions, an optimizer can fit strong directions and miss weak ones while average loss hides it. A precision wall measures the steepest decay where the weakest direction recovers within 5% in a fixed budget. On low-rank matrix sensing and quadratics one constant sets the wall: once it dominates the denominator, Adam or grafted Shampoo is momentum descent at step $η/c$, setting the wall; in Shampoo, matching grafting's constant to damping restores the wall after three hypotheses failed. In zeroth-order Adam, $\varepsilon$ acts against $\varepsilon^\ast$ from a mean-square spectral model; below $\varepsilon^\ast$ error hits a noise floor. Set from true curvature or spectrum on new shapes, the rule raised the wall above the same-step default by a forecast amount: on two sensing shapes by $1.96, 1.15$ (Adam) and $1.59, 1.74$ (grafted Shampoo), and on two Hadamard-rotated quadratics by $1.12, 0.73$ (zeroth-order Adam, longer budget), rising on all seeds; fixed-step momentum at that step reaches those walls. A cosine-scheduled default (small constant giving weak modes large steps) is higher on one sensing shape, matched on new seeds of the other, and lower with the rule's constant. A default step retuned per budget catches up on rotated quadratics and population logistic regression, fails at 1 query/step on two diagonal quadratics (catching up at 16), and exceeds the rule on digits logistic regression (full-batch and minibatches, where gradient noise sets the $η= 0.01, 0.1$ default walls). On logistic regression the rule did not raise the first-order wall: same-step default Adam was higher (its wall on 4 new shapes at the forecast stability limit), while in zeroth order on the population loss setting $\varepsilon$ raised the wall by $0.98, 0.88$.

</details>

Abstract: This work presents a one-class anomaly detection framework for vibration-based condition monitoring in machining processes using a convolutional neural network autoencoder. Three-axis vibration signals acquired during industrial broaching are transformed into time--frequency spectrograms and combined into a compact three-channel representation, enabling convolutional feature learning on multivariate time-series data. The autoencoder is trained exclusively on normal process data and detects anomalous strokes based on reconstruction errors. Labeled validation data are used for threshold calibration and an independent test set is reserved for final performance evaluation. To improve sensitivity to short-lived process instabilities such as chatter, a localized residual-based metric termed Segmented Vertical Error (SVE) is introduced. Experimental results on approximately 10{,}000 broaching strokes show a clear separation between normal and anomalous process conditions under strong class imbalance. The processing pipeline is additionally evaluated on an external public turning dataset as a proof of concept, indicating preliminary applicability beyond the original broaching setup. The results suggest that spectrogram-based autoencoder models can provide a practical and scalable basis for one-class anomaly detection in industrial vibration monitoring.

Abstract: Aligning Large Language Models (LLMs) with human preferences via Reinforcement Learning from Human Feedback (RLHF) has driven substantial improvements across a wide range of tasks, but the cost of acquiring preference labels remains the dominant bottleneck in the alignment pipeline. While many recent alignment methods improve sample efficiency through active exploration over candidate responses, they typically query the oracle uniformly across contexts, even for contexts where the learned reward estimates are already reliable enough to provide synthetic labels. We propose Adaptive Ensemble-disagreement Routing for Oracle feedback (AERO), a fully online RLHF algorithm built on a Dyna-style model-based RL architecture, in which an ensemble-based Epistemic Reward Model (ERM) serves both as a reward posterior for exploration and as a learned preference-feedback model for synthetic labeling. AERO uses ensemble vote entropy, a Query-by-Committee disagreement measure, combined with an adaptive thresholding mechanism to decide which contexts require oracle feedback and which can instead receive ERM-based synthetic preference labels, concentrating expensive labels where they are most informative. Empirical results across different model families and scales show that AERO achieves highly competitive alignment performance while reducing the required oracle-query budget compared to online DPO and strong active-exploration baselines.

Abstract: Deep neural networks achieve universal function approximation by stacking interconnected nonlinearities, but as a consequence they are sensitive to noise and resist mechanistic interpretation. In contrast, the local update rules of cellular automata, such as Conway's Game of Life, give rise to not merely interpretable but programmable dynamics. We explore a bridge between these worlds: a lateral inhibition in neural networks based on continuous Game-of-Life rules that derives inhibitory and excitatory responses from the layer input and blends them into the activation map in a state-conditioned way. The mechanism is differentiable and architecture-agnostic, and its regulation is imposed rather than learned: the rule's parameters are fixed and are not optimised, while the rule's values and derivatives shape training throughout the network. Wired into fully connected, convolutional, wide-residual and graph convolutional networks, it gives rise to several properties. Activation maps become coherent across space, depth and seeds, with coarse structure persisting through depth while fine detail is renewed at every layer. The co-activation graph of the hidden units departs from an Erdős--Rényi random graph: it is laid out on the image and its giant component grows in steps, as in a spatial graph. Networks started from nearly identical initialisations, under label noise, disagree on thirty times fewer test images. Saliency maps rest on the object rather than the background, and resilience to pixel noise not seen in training is substantially improved.

</details>

Abstract: We prove universal approximation for the bilinear-readout equivariant message-passing network (BREMP), a reference architecture for equivariant graph neural network interatomic potentials. Its layers combine equivariant convolutions with affine maps, and its readout forms invariants from products of the equivariant features. We construct a one-layer feature that determines the local environment up to permutations of atoms of the same type. The construction uses spherical harmonic filters of degree no larger than the largest number of neighbors at a common distance from the center. Iterating the construction recovers the environment reachable in as many hops as there are layers, using the same bound on filter degrees at the layer cutoff. The readout approximates every continuous invariant function of the features uniformly on compact sets. Together these give universal approximation of atomic energies at a prescribed physical range using a shorter layer cutoff. We assume that, throughout the admissible class, message passing reaches all neighbors in each physical environment within a common number of layers. Finally, DPA4, an interatomic potential architecture in production use, inherits this result by approximating the BREMP layers constructed in the proof and realizing the bilinear readout.

Each participant's brain responses were recorded while they attended to specific semantic features (e.g., smiling, hair color) in a rapid serial visual presentation paradigm. The dataset contains EEG signals from 30 participants, totaling 67,200 time-locked EEG recordings. We present benchmarks for single-trial semantic classification under single-subject, cross-subject, and subject-adapted settings, as well as for cross-subject voting that aggregates per-stimulus predictions across participants. We further demonstrate the dataset's unique utility with two complementary applications: deriving semantic directions in the latent space for brain-supervised image editing, and conditioning a generative model on brain-decoded labels for brain-supervised image generation. Our dataset paves the way for advancing brain-computer interfaces (BCI) for semantic-level visual saliency detection and brain-supervised generative modeling. Abstract: We introduce a proximal preference optimization framework that unifies the implicit reward formulation of Direct Preference Optimization (DPO) with the trust-region stability of Proximal Policy Optimization (PPO). Our framework yields two complementary algorithms. P$^2$O (Proximal Preference Optimization) introduces a clipped importance ratio directly into the DPO preference margin, producing a reward-model-free algorithm that supports safe dataset reuse, bounded policy divergence, and monotonic preference improvement guarantees. PKTO (Proximal Kahneman-Tversky Optimization) extends P$^2$O by replacing the DPO margin with KTO's prospect-theoretic utility margins, adding a self-regulating adaptive baseline that tightens the effective trust region as the policy drifts, and an asymmetric weighting that handles imbalanced or asymmetric preference datasets. We provide a unified theoretical analysis, including Kullback Leibler (KL) trust-region bounds, a policy-improvement lower bound, and convergence guarantees under multi-epoch proximal updates. Empirically, P$^2$O and PKTO match or exceed DPO and KTO in alignment quality while maintaining better stability under repeated dataset reuse across multiple LLMs on three preference benchmarks. The code base is available here.

Abstract: LLMs are powerful but incur substantial inference cost, which can be exploited by malicious users to induce overly long outputs, increasing latency and operational expense. Prior resource consumption attacks either coerce repetition or suppress the end-of-sequence token by lowering its logit value. However, with autoregressive, sampling-based generation, the search space grows exponentially with length and small deviations or unstable trajectories can derail optimization, limiting attack effectiveness and stability. To address this issue, we introduce Chatter Attack, a novel class of resource consumption attacks that optimize adversarial prompts to induce predefined short target phrases, thereby avoiding search-space explosion while still eliciting long responses. Specifically, Chatter Attack triggers self-doubt using short doubt-inducing phrases as the target, and sustains this state throughout generation via inference attention loss and global entropy loss. To extend to black-box settings, we further propose a novel Bayesian Prompt Optimization (BPO) method, which models the objective function globally by constructing a discrete token kernel, efficiently exploring the entire search space using prior information. Extensive experiments across multiple models and datasets show that our method outperforms baselines, with maximum response length increases to 31.5$\times$, and revealing practical resource-depletion risks for real-world LLM inference services.

Abstract: Machine unlearning in large language models (LLMs) aims to remove specific learned knowledge, capabilities, or behaviors while preserving model utility. Existing methods have mostly focused on the design of unlearning objectives, while largely overlooking the intrinsic matrix structure of transformer weights. We propose spectral unlearning, an update framework that combines standard unlearning objectives with matrix-structured optimization. Our approach is applied on top of existing unlearning methods and evaluated across complementary axes of memorization, privacy, and utility, summarized via their harmonic mean. Abstract: Zeroth-order (ZO) optimization estimates descent directions from function values alone, which removes the activation storage required by backpropagation and makes it attractive for memory-efficient fine-tuning of large language models (LLMs). Existing low-rank ZO methods either parameterize the additive update through Euclidean factors with a non-unique representation, or restrict each finite-difference direction to be low-rank. In this work, we propose to constrain each additive update block to a fixed-rank matrix manifold while keeping the pretrained weights frozen, so the learned update remains rank-constrained throughout fine-tuning. On this geometry, we build a Riemannian ZO gradient estimator that projects a random matrix onto the tangent space, normalizes it to correct the scale mismatch induced by projection, and further propose Zeroth-order Riemannian Gradient Descent (ZO-RGD) and its smoothed variant, ZO-RGD-S. We show that the ambient finite difference matches its retracted counterpart, the normalized estimator recovers the Riemannian gradient in expectation, and ZO-RGD converges under trajectory-regularity conditions. Experiments on LLM fine-tuning tasks show that the proposed algorithms are competitive with strong forward-only baselines across model families and tasks, with gains in several settings.

Abstract: Iterative neural policies usually spend the same computation on every decision, although the benefit of additional processing varies across states. We introduce Adaptive Internal Computation (AIC), an iterative policy that learns when to stop refining its latent representation and act. In AIC, we train action selection and stopping jointly, an expected-depth penalty values computation and intermediate value predictions provide auxiliary supervision. We show that when only some states benefit from further refinement, an ideal state-dependent allocator outperforms every state-independent stopping schedule at the same expected depth. With at most eight refinement steps per decision, AIC executes 3.3 steps on average across twelve benchmark tasks and lowers per-decision latency by 31--44% relative to a fixed eight-step policy on four control and robotics tasks. Its mean reward exceeds the best fixed-depth policy on seven tasks and trails it on four. Ablating adaptive stopping and value supervision separately shows that stopping accounts for most of the reward gain on PickCube and AntMaze, where it also executes more refinement steps and that supervision accounts for most of it on RareEvent.

Abstract: We propose a new mathematical formalism in which all kinds of tensors and of operations on those tensors are represented as simple function compositions. Tensors are functions from indices to values. An element-wise operation (e.g. an activation function) is a composition with a function applied to values; re-indexing (e.g. reshape, transposition) is a composition with a function applied to indices; and a reduction is a composition with both an index function, mapping each index to several indices, and a reduction operation (e.g. product, sum, max). Combined with tensor stacking, these compositions can represent most interesting kinds of tensors (e.g. dense, jagged, all kinds of sparsity patterns, matrix factorizations), as well as lazy versions of almost every operation available in modern tensor computation frameworks, including einsums between tensors of any kind. Identities between compositions can then be used to rewrite such expressions into equivalent ones, without evaluating them. For instance, the product of two sparse matrices can be rewritten so that only products between non-zero values are computed, turning a sparse problem into a dense one. Using this algebra as the intermediate representation of a tensor computation engine would shift the focus from defining operations on tensors (e.g. einsum between sparse tensors) to defining an algebra on index functions. This should reduce the codebase complexity from $\mathcal{O}(mn^2)$, with $m$ the number of binary operations between tensors and $n$ the number of tensor types, to $\mathcal{O}(m + k^2)$, with $k$ the number of types of index functions. A draft implementation of a tensor computation framework based on this algebra is publicly available.

</details>

Abstract: Training datasets for unsupervised anomaly detection are often assumed to be anomaly-free, but in practice, they frequently contain malicious or anomalous contamination. This contamination severely degrades the dependability and security of downstream detection models. While some contamination-robust methods exist, they rarely generalize across different model architectures and typically rely on a priori knowledge of the contamination ratio\textemdash information that is rarely available in real-world security operations. In this paper, we propose a novel, self-contained preprocessing framework for anomaly detection that identifies and removes training set contamination without relying on known contamination ratios. Our approach performs a dynamic analysis of the cosine similarity between each data point and its reconstruction during the early training stages of an auto-encoder, mapping points into a dynamic space where anomalous regions naturally exhibit lower density. By executing ``hard cleaning''\textemdash removing all identified anomalous candidates from the training pool\textemdash we systematically improve data quality. Because this cleaning occurs at the data level, the security and reliability improvements generalize seamlessly across various downstream anomaly detection architectures. Evaluating our framework across 8 distinct models and 10 security-relevant datasets, our approach achieves up to 100\% contamination reduction, yielding average improvements of 11.2\% in F1 score and 7.3\% in classification accuracy.

Abstract: Continuous DAG learning commonly enforces acyclicity through scalar functions of a weighted adjacency that aggregate evidence from weighted closed walks. We add a complementary layer: matrix-valued closure logits---explicit multi-hop structural relation scores for each ordered pair---supervised alongside data fit, sparsity, and a DAGMA-style barrier on the weighted adjacency. The distinction is not scalarization of the overall objective but how path evidence is represented and exposed before aggregation. SMNC (scale-normalized multi-scale closure) builds these scores on a structural gate by repeated squaring in log space and divides each scale by its path-length budget, counteracting product-style attenuation along long cycles; diagonal entries support node-wise cycle supervision and off-diagonals optional pairwise reachability cues. In the linear model, a bounded structure--magnitude factorization ties strong effective edges to strong gates; a nonlinear induced gate offers another construction with the same intent. For sufficiently strong cycles, normalized logits admit a length-independent lower bound; under a unique-active-cycle assumption, the hard final-scale readout yields a polynomial log-gate gradient factor rather than exponential product-style decay. On cue-free, unstandardized Gaussian linear and nonlinear ER benchmarks, these models improve over DAGMA, run with default hyperparameters at a reduced budget, on denser graphs and are close to it on sparse ones.

</details>

Abstract: Learning predictive models for dynamics is difficult because observations are often sparse and noisy, while the underlying dynamics evolve on nonlinear configuration manifolds and are affected by environmental dissipation and stochastic disturbances. Standard neural dynamics models can fit short-term trajectories but often violate physical structure, accumulate error over long horizons, or rely on parameterizations that introduce singularities such as gimbal lock. We introduce LieSPHGP, a stochastic Gaussian-process framework for learning rigid body dynamics with port-Hamiltonian structure and stability-aware robustness. LieSPHGP represents motion on Lie groups and Lie algebras, enabling coordinate-consistent optimization without singular parameterizations. Learned mass, potential energy, dissipation, and control components retain physical roles and a separate diffusion term represents stochastic forcing. Each component is modeled by Matérn kernel Gaussian processes which capture uncertainty induced by noisy observations. To improve physical validity, Lie group exponential map updates preserve rotational constraints, while positive-definite damping ensures nonnegative deterministic energy dissipation. The implicit-explicit integrator additionally provides unconditional stability for the frozen linear damping subsystem. The resulting model learns predictive dynamics that are robust to noise, respect rigid body geometry, and capture both intrinsic motion and environmental dissipation. Experiments on various noisy rigid body benchmarks show improved prediction accuracy, stronger long-horizon stability, and better satisfaction of manifold constraints compared with neural dynamics baselines. Code, data and interactive results are available on the project website: https://thribhuvanrapolu.github.io/liesphgp/

Abstract: How little of a pretrained large language model (LLM) has to change for it to acquire new capabilities? We find that zeroing fewer than 0.05% of its weights, with no other modification, matches full fine-tuning (FFT) at scale across three LLM post-training regimes: reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards, supervised fine-tuning, and on-policy distillation. Our method, Deletion Tuning, learns a binary mask over the gate projection of transformer blocks. Two practical advantages follow: the trained mask is hundreds to thousands of times smaller than the corresponding FFT checkpoint, and Deletion Tuning forgets far less, which addresses a central challenge in continual learning. Under reinforcement learning, its prior-task accuracy stays near the base level while FFT, LoRA, and random sparse fine-tuning lose up to 5.4 points. To understand why masking alone suffices, we analyze the geometry of the learned weight changes under reinforcement learning using stable rank, spectrum drift, and principal-weight overlap. Compared with LoRA and random sparse fine-tuning, Deletion Tuning is closest to FFT across the three measures.

Abstract: We study the problem of controlling instability in the Vlasov--Poisson plasma systems, a fundamental challenge for nuclear fusion control. Motivated by the partial observability of plasma states in practice, we investigate imitation learning algorithms that learn from a fully-observable expert controller, but operate under macroscopic measurements. We theoretically establish a separation between online and offline imitation learning --- even a small behavior cloning error leads to error compounding that exponentially amplifies instability over time. By way of contrast, the online imitation learning loss can polynomially control the stability of rollout trajectories. We then propose a DAgger-style algorithm that learns the stabilizing controller under partial observability. Simulation results on a 1D Vlasov--Poisson system demonstrate that our algorithm can effectively stabilize plasma instabilities over longer time horizons than behavior cloning.

Abstract: Reinforcement learning for large language model post-training often reuses each batch of sampled rollouts for several gradient passes to amortize the cost of generation. Under such multi-pass reuse, existing objectives fail in different ways: unclipped updates collapse as the policy drifts away from the sampling policy, adding a negative baseline avoids collapse but reinforces every sampled response, and PPO-style ratio clipping cuts off gradients abruptly. We first ask why reuse is fragile. Measuring how well an update estimated from a small rollout group aligns with the KL-regularized target on held-out rollouts of the same prompts, we find that this alignment drops by nearly an order of magnitude from in-sample to held-out rollouts across three base models, suggesting that repeated passes largely overfit the sampled group. Motivated by this finding, we propose Bounded Residual-Aligned Training (BRAT), motivated by a proximal update toward a bounded reweighting of the sampling policy. BRAT assigns each token a gradient weight that decreases linearly with its probability ratio and vanishes at a trust-region boundary, and normalizes group-centered rewards by their maximum magnitude, keeping advantages zero-mean and bounded without extra hyperparameters. With four reuse passes, BRAT avoids training collapse, limits policy drift, and achieves higher average accuracy with lower seed variance on most benchmarks.

</details>

Abstract: Standard ensemble methods can improve robustness by mitigating individual model stochasticity, but they frequently fail to generalize beyond the training distribution, leaving systems vulnerable to out-of-distribution (OOD) data. We introduce the Robust Satisficing Ensemble (RSE), a scalable probabilistic aggregation framework designed to secure model combinations by minimizing their fragility against distributional changes. By bridging a robust satisficing objective with a quadratic risk surrogate, RSE derives an analytical inner adversary. This fundamental reformulation enables highly efficient weight updates without the computational bottleneck of standard minimax optimization, crucially decoupling optimization complexity from sample size to scale effectively to large datasets. In particular, we prove a geometrically decaying truncation error for the fragility approximation via Lagrange-Burmann expansion and establish stationarity guarantees for RSE. We evaluate RSE across a comprehensive spectrum of distribution shifts, including adversarial label shift (SST-5, TREC), natural subpopulation and temporal shifts (CivilComments, HuffPost). Our results demonstrate that RSE consistently outperforms strong robust baselines, including GroupDRO, IRM, and SRM. These findings establish RSE as a practical, theoretically grounded safeguard for reliable model aggregation in dynamic environments.

Abstract: Real-world deployment of federated learning (FL) requires resilience against adversarial clients and data privacy. Byzantine fault captures the worst-case behavior of adversarial clients, while differential privacy (DP) provides statistical guarantees against privacy loss. In particular, user-level DP guards a client's entire contribution and provides more realistic protection against information leakage. Despite intensive efforts, the minimax-optimal convergence bound of Byzantine-resilient FL with user-level DP remains open. We establish an information-theoretic lower bound for any Byzantine-resilient empirical risk minimization problem under user-level DP and heterogeneous clients. We propose ByzULDP, a central DP algorithm featuring a two-level momentum across the client and server. Client-side momentum mitigates stochastic gradient variance during robust aggregation. Unlike the fault-free case, naively combining non-linear Byzantine-resilient aggregation with central DP noise can be insufficient to guarantee convergence to a bounded neighborhood of a stationary point. Our key contribution is a server-side momentum that leverages a property specific to user-level DP, where privacy sensitivity depends on how client updates are constructed, to empower convergence. ByzULDP achieves the order-optimal convergence bound for strongly-convex objectives, with vanishing error terms decaying at the optimal $O(1/T)$ and $O(1/\sqrt{T})$ rates for strongly-convex and non-convex objectives, respectively. We corroborate our analysis with numerical experiments on real-world datasets across diverse privacy budgets.

Abstract: Binary decision-making under uncertainty arises across a wide range of applications, including energy systems scheduling, transportation, and workforce planning. While machine learning has been increasingly applied to accelerate or replace mixed-integer solvers in such settings, existing approaches rely on predictive learning, training models to reproduce optimal decisions from labeled data, which lacks knowledge of counterfactual outcomes and provides no guarantees that the resulting decisions will be feasible. This limitation is further compounded under distributional shift, where policies trained on historical data may produce systematically infeasible decisions. We propose a prescriptive learning framework for binary decision-making that addresses both limitations. Rather than predicting optimal labels, the framework jointly optimizes over linear decision policies and feasible assignments across all training scenarios, allowing feasible alternatives and their operational performance to be considered directly during training. We further extend the framework to a distributionally robust chance-constrained formulation using a Wasserstein ambiguity set, which enforces feasibility with a prescribed probability under the worst-case distribution in the ambiguity set and enables a tunable trade-off between robustness and solution quality. We demonstrate the framework on two case studies, namely a unit commitment problem and a staff allocation problem. Out-of-sample experiments under test distributions that either match or differ from the training distribution show that the robust model is more conservative when the two distributions coincide, while under distributional shift it substantially reduces unmet demand and lowers total cost by up to 76.5% relative to classical predictive learning.

</details>

Abstract: Learning accurate off-policy state-action value functions (Q-functions) from offline datasets becomes harder as the task horizon grows. One-step temporal-difference (TD) learning bootstraps after every transition, so small per-step errors accumulate along the horizon, and early states can be badly misvalued even when the TD loss is small. Multi-step TD shortens this chain by following the dataset trajectory for several steps; however, since the behavior policy is often suboptimal, every followed action adds a bias, and no fixed number of steps suits a whole trajectory. In this paper, we propose Horizon-Scalable Q-Learning (HQL), a simple method that enables accurate long-horizon value learning by automatically balancing between one-step and multi-step returns. HQL extends a target through a dataset action only when the critic values it at least as highly as the best-of-N actions sampled from the BC policy, and otherwise falls back to a one-step target. We show that this rule is a principled off-policy correction that needs no action densities, which makes HQL compatible with expressive flow and diffusion policies. Experiments show that HQL avoids the failures of both one-step and multi-step TD on a combination-lock task across horizons, and outperforms prior horizon-reduction methods on long-horizon OGBench and OCBench tasks.

</details>

Abstract: In offline reinforcement learning, diffusion models fill two roles with complementary strengths. Typically guided by a critic, a diffusion policy is value-aware and favors high-return, behavior-supported actions, but it is short-sighted: it never lays out the states it intends to visit. A diffusion planner is far-sighted, synthesizing such a long-horizon trajectory, but it is only behavior-driven at execution, realizing each planned transition using only offline behavior.Each is strong exactly where the other is weak. We introduce DPDP to bring them together at the planner's execution step, where a planned transition becomes an action. Reading the planner's inverse-dynamics executor as a posterior over actions: the environment's transition likelihood relates an action to the planned next state, while the behavioral prior determines its weight under the offline data. Replacing this prior with a value-optimized diffusion policy preserves the planned transition likelihood and favors valuable actions that can serve the plan. We target the resulting clean-action distribution in two ways. A training-free sampler composes policy and inverse-dynamics scores at matched action noise levels and applies Langevin correction; this route uses a shared action noise schedule and an approximately common terminal prior. A preference objective instead amortizes the transfer into a single diffusion executor. Across D4RL, DPDP improves the planner it builds on, staying competitive with reactive diffusion policies on locomotion and leading on long-horizon navigation.

</details>

Abstract: Machine learning models, particularly large foundation models, are increasingly evaluated along multiple axes that are relevant for their usage. We then consider evaluation algorithms that can query the model on additional inputs and demonstrate connections to self-correctors studied in program testing. Using these, we show that a small amount of query access can increase the power of the evaluator for natural distributions and concept classes.

Abstract: Learned representations (or embeddings) are the foundation of modern machine learning systems, yet they are typically trained as fixed-size embeddings, without accounting for varying downstream resource or task constraints. Matryoshka Representation Learning (MRL) addresses this limitation by learning nested representations, but requires costly end-to-end retraining and does not support post hoc expansion, while Contrastive Sparse Representations (CSR) reduce training overhead via lightweight adaptors but operate at a 4x larger dimensionality. Additionally, all prior works restrict their framework towards compression only. In this work, we introduce Hyperbolic Elastic Representation Learning (HERL), a framework for learning dimension-adaptive embeddings (compression and expansion) through a lightweight adaptor without retraining the base encoder. Our key idea is to exploit the exponential capacity of hyperbolic geometry by learning a non-linear mapping from the encoder space to hyperbolic space. We train a simple MLP to downsample the representations before projecting them onto a Lorentzian and learn adaptive representations that respect the Lorentzian geometry. Our approach is model-agnostic and effective in both supervised and unsupervised settings. Through extensive experiments across image and text modalities, we show that HERL preserves downstream performance across a wide range of embedding sizes, exhibits smooth interpolation between dimensions, and achieves these benefits at a fraction of the computational cost. Code: https://github.com/arjunrkaushik/herl.

Abstract: Standard training recipes make a fixed number of passes over the training data, even when an epoch mostly adds mini-batch noise. We study whether a large language model (LLM) that reads the weight and gradient history of a run can replace some of these epochs with updates that compute no gradient and read no training image. In GradReason, an LLM agent is called every ten epochs. In a restricted sandbox, it analyses recent weight snapshots, epoch-mean gradients, and validation metrics, then submits a Python program that computes new weights and a plan marking each of the next ten epochs as data or virtual. A virtual epoch is kept only if it passes a forward-only check on 5,000 held-out validation images. In 120 runs on CIFAR-10 and CIFAR-100 with three architectures, Claude Opus 5.5 used 32.8% fewer data epochs than the 80-epoch recipe at 0.31 percentage points lower test accuracy (95% CI -0.72 to +0.09; 0.94 points on ResNet-18), on the recipe's own frontier of data against accuracy. Unprompted, Opus averaged recent weight snapshots in every run. This choice produced the savings: a fixed rule with the same acceptance check but extrapolations from prior work saved 5.4% of the data epochs, and the same rule with averaging added saved 25.0%. The LLM's contribution is to find a working update within a single run; once found, a fixed rule implements it equally well. An Opus run cost 0.68 USD, but at this scale the agent's latency exceeded the GPU time it saved.

Abstract: Runtime anomaly detectors for cyber-physical systems are either learned as black boxes from normal data, which miss stealthy manipulations that keep every signal inside its normal range, or derived by hand from physical models, which requires expert effort for every plant. We introduce physicsinformed agents (PIA), in which a large language model agent writes the detector itself as a set of physical residuals, such as pump characteristics, tank mass balances, and controller logic, and refines every candidate against normal operating data through an evaluation workbench before deployment. The result is a compact, human-readable detector that runs in milliseconds and names the component behind each alarm. On the BATADAL water distribution attack benchmark, PIA with Claude Opus 5.5 reaches an F1 of 0.878 on the test attacks against 0.504 for the strongest of six statistical and deep baselines, keeps detecting attacks concealed by replayed and offset sensor readings, and attributes every detected test attack to the attacked component. Ablations show that testing hypotheses against data is decisive: agents that write residuals from the plant description alone lose up to 0.28 F1, whereas agents that see only anonymized signals recover most of the physical structure.

Abstract: Animals integrate information across sensory modalities to guide exploration, suggesting that they maintain internal estimates of where actions are expected to reduce uncertainty. However, how such action-conditioned information maps are learned from multimodal sensory experience remains unclear, as previous studies have been largely confined to unimodal sensing frameworks. We introduce a multimodal predictive-coding framework for learning uncertainty-reduction maps from visual and mechanosensory observations in hawkmoth flower interaction tasks. A variational generative model trained on simulated environments recovers modality-specific information landscapes over the action space, capturing how visual patterns, surface geometry, and mechanosensory cues shape the value of exploratory actions. These learned maps are consistent with several features of hawkmoth behavior, including nonzero tracking offsets, probing along visual patterns, and geometry-dependent exploration. Applying the model to behavioral data from flower tracking and nectary search, we find that natural trajectories tend to occupy regions of high uncertainty reduction in these learned maps. Building on these observations, we develop an uncertainty-gated lexicographic algorithm that switches between information gathering and reward exploitation based on perceptual uncertainty. In nectary search, uncertainty gating improves learning relative to exploit-only, $ε$-greedy, fixed-time, and uncertainty-calibrated non-lexicographic controls, with better generalization and robustness to sensory and curvature shifts. These results link information landscapes learned through generative modeling to biological sensing and embodied exploration.

Abstract: This paper explores an unexpected connection between the quality of a neural network's (NN's) internal representation and its vulnerability to catastrophic forgetting. The representational forgetting hypothesis is that networks with unified, factored representation (UFR) can resist catastrophic forgetting without any modification to the underlying fine-tuning algorithm or network architecture. The key insight is that catastrophic forgetting may largely result from fractured entangled representation (FER): fine-tuning on only part of the input domain results in disruptive changes for other input patterns, because of how individual features are globally entangled. In contrast, if a gradient update is compatible with how a UFR network factors its representation, such an update may result in coordinated change across the input domain, alleviating catastrophic forgetting. Experiments in two domains provide evidence for this hypothesis: (1) an illustrative 1D regression task, in which UFR networks constructed by hand are compared against networks trained from scratch with SGD, and (2) an image-regression task with NNs previously shown to exhibit UFR. In both settings, UFR networks forget substantially less than their FER counterparts when repeatedly fine-tuned on UFR-compatible variations. Further experiments show that a training loss that distills UFR representations into fresh NNs reduces forgetting; along with several additional controls, these results help isolate representation as the critical factor. Together these results suggest that representational quality is an underappreciated contributor to catastrophic forgetting, and that the encouragement and maintenance of such quality may require attending to what patterns a particular NN is ready to learn.

</details>

Abstract: Embodied intelligence systems rely on fast and reliable inference to enable real-time perception, reasoning, and control. Recent advances in large-scale artificial intelligence (AI) models, including Large Language Models (LLMs) and Vision-Language-Action (VLA) models, have significantly enhanced the capabilities of embodied agents in complex environments. However, deploying these models remains challenging due to the limited computational resources and energy budgets of robotic platforms. A fundamental question is where inference should be executed. Collaborative inference, which partitions models across end devices, edge servers, and the cloud, has emerged as a promising solution. Yet determining the optimal partitioning point remains an open problem, as it depends on wireless network conditions, model structure, and privacy requirements. This article reviews the state of the art in collaborative inference for embodied intelligence and proposes an edge-centric framework in which the edge coordinates model partitioning and execution. Through both simulation and real-world experiments over WiFi and 5G networks, we demonstrate that the optimal partitioning strategy varies with network conditions and model structure. We further identify latency-minimizing partition points for ResNet-101, Qwen2.5-VL-7B, and OpenVLA-7B in collaborative inference between NVIDIA Jetson Nano or AGX Xavier devices and an AGX Thor edge server under varying wireless conditions. Finally, we discuss the future directions for collaborative inference in 6G-empowered embodied intelligence systems.

Abstract: Extended reasoning can help language models solve complex problems, but long responses often contain both useful computations and redundant checks. Conventional length penalties encourage shorter responses without distinguishing the functions of intermediate steps. We investigate two classification routes: PCRS-H with a hidden-state classifier and PCRS-T with a frozen text classifier. Abstract: E-commerce catalogs increasingly live in lakehouse tables: Apache Iceberg metadata over Parquet files in object storage. Product search, however, still runs on copies of that catalog, in a lexical engine and a vector database, each with its own ingestion pipeline, schema, staleness window and bill. We show that search applications can be hosted directly on the data lake, with the lakehouse table as the single system of record. We extend Apache Pinot, a real-time OLAP engine that already serves Iceberg tables in place, with BM25 relevance scoring over its Lucene text index, corpus-wide term statistics so that scores are comparable across files, and reciprocal rank fusion in plain SQL; a real-time table fed by a stream can sit behind the same table, merged by the broker. On the Amazon ESCI benchmark (1.2M US products, 22,458 test queries), hybrid search served from the lake matches exact retrieval (nDCG@10 .361). The lake table is queryable 149 s after its files are registered, and a lake commit is searchable in 3.8 s, close to indexing directly into a search engine. On a brand-sorted lake of 277M products (0.8 TB), one 16-vCPU node answers brand-scoped vector, BM25 and client-fused hybrid searches in 6.5-9 ms (p50), sustains 1,100 vector and 635 BM25 searches per second at under 180 ms p99, and makes a commit searchable in 4.3 s. Search adds only its indexes: no copy of the catalog, no sync pipeline and no second schema, which cuts storage, rebuild time and the operational work of keeping copies consistent.

Abstract: Building on SODA 2026 work Kearns et al. (2026), we study learning in directed acyclic graphs in which each agent observes only a subset of the input coordinates and may use the predictions of its parents as additional features. In the finite-sample protocol, the message sent along each edge is the agent's prediction vector on a shared sample, so the communication cost is linear in the sample size $m$. In this paper we study this model under communication constraints.
  First, we revisit the finite-sample analysis of the full-message protocol. We identify a gap in the finite-sample proof of Kearns et al. (2026) and develop a different argument that establishes a finite-sample population-risk guarantee along paths satisfying deterministic $M$-coverage.
  Second, we introduce a shared-sketch protocol in which each agent sends a $k$-dimensional linear sketch of its prediction vector instead of the full $m$-dimensional message, where $k\ll m$. We analyze this protocol through a reduction to least squares on the compressed sample together with an oblivious subspace embedding argument. The resulting bound separates statistics from communication: the statistical term is still governed by the original sample size $m$, while the additional error due to compression is controlled by the sketch dimension $k$. For Gaussian sketches, we obtain an explicit high-probability population-risk bound, showing that row subsampling is not the only possible communication-statistics tradeoff for networked information aggregation.

</details>

Abstract: Reasoning models often continue generating after producing an answer, yet stopping prematurely may interrupt useful verification or revision. In this work, we investigate whether targeted interventions in a model's internal computation can provide a reliable signal for when to stop reasoning. We introduce Prune-to-Probe, an inference-time controller that uses causal activation interventions to probe the stability of a model's current answer. Specifically, we identify late-MLP coordinates that contribute to post-answer continuation while excluding those important for answer completion. Once an answer is stated, Prune-to-Probe temporarily suppresses the selected coordinates and measures the resulting stopping response relative to matched random interventions. Crucially, these interventions serve only as probes: the model either returns its existing answer or resumes generation from an untouched cache. On MATH, Prune-to-Probe reduces reasoning-token forwards by 2.60%, preserving all naturally correct trajectories while repairing six errors. On MMLU-Pro, it achieves 1.145% pooled savings with no observed harm. Further analysis shows that the intervention captures decision stability rather than correctness (AUROC 0.502). These results demonstrate the potential of causal activation probes for efficient inference-time control without permanent model modification.

Abstract: Medical machine learning models often learn from diagnoses observed only in patients clinicians chose to test. The biases of such selective labels are well known, but less understood is what happens when a diagnosis includes cases the model's inputs cannot detect. Because testing decisions draw on information the model does not see, these cases can appear predictable among tested patients, producing relationships that fail, and can even reverse, among the untested. We formalize this mechanism and show in simulations that it worsens as testing becomes more selective or undetectable cases more common, while models trained only on detectable cases remain informative. We empirically study electrocardiogram (ECG)-based prediction of acute coronary syndrome (ACS), which is confirmed only by invasive testing and whose subtypes differ in how clearly they appear on the ECG. Three ECG foundation models fine-tuned on revascularization, our ACS proxy, performed well among catheterized patients (AUROC 0.740-0.770) but worse than chance for 30-day major adverse cardiovascular events among untested patients (AUROC 0.381-0.478). Models trained on ECG-visible subtypes retained discrimination (AUROC 0.680-0.709), whereas a model trained on unstable angina, which can occur without ECG changes, was inversely related to risk (AUROC 0.310). Under selective labels, effective learning therefore requires prediction targets that can be inferred from the model's inputs and evaluation in the intended deployment population, which often extends beyond the patients who were tested.

</details>

Abstract: Discrete diffusion is a promising framework for categorical generative modeling, but current evaluation protocols often fail to reveal whether models have actually learned the target distribution. We introduce the Discrete Diffusion Playground, a diagnostic benchmark that converts known 2D continuous distributions into binary token sequences through an exact discretized bijection. This creates a rare setting in which models are trained on discrete sequences, but generated samples can be decoded back into 2D and compared directly against a known ground-truth distribution. The benchmark spans smooth Gaussian mixtures and tilted checkerboards with sharp boundaries, disconnected support, and coordinate-dependent structure, allowing it to expose failures that are invisible in standard proxy metrics. It supports exact grid divergences, Wasserstein distances, density visualizations, and nonparametric two-sample tests, providing both quantitative evaluation and interpretable failure diagnosis. Using this testbed, we show that masked diffusion models can learn meaningful adaptive reveal orders but remain less stable than autoregressive baselines on smooth targets and largely fail on tilted checkerboards, where autoregressive models preserve the target geometry. Control experiments with reversed and interleaved token orderings show that autoregressive performance is not explained by favorable bit order alone. The Playground provides a simple but stringent benchmark for stress-testing discrete generative models and for developing evaluation metrics that detect real distributional mismatch rather than proxy performance. Our code is available at https://github.com/shaojus/DiscreteDiffusionPlayground .

Abstract: Large language model safety evaluations often focus on a compliance-refusal tradeoff: models should refuse unsafe requests without refusing safe ones. We argue that this misses a broader form of prompt-conditioned task selection, where the same request can elicit different behaviors depending on the system prompt. We investigate this using a controlled evaluation crossing eight safety-task prompt families with three clarity levels across HarmBench, XSTest, and IHEval. Holding benchmark items fixed, we evaluate 13 models and use signal detection theory to distinguish two possibilities: whether models become better at telling safe from unsafe requests (discriminability), or simply more willing to refuse (response criterion). We find substantial differences in discriminability across models, but system prompts primarily shift the willingness to refuse, with smaller and less consistent effects on discriminability. Increasing prompt clarity makes models more willing to refuse without reliably improving their ability to distinguish safe from unsafe requests. Beyond refusal, prompt families also shift responses among six distinct behavioral policies, including clarification and safe help. Together, these findings show that system prompts primarily change how models respond to safety-relevant requests rather than consistently improving their ability to distinguish safe from unsafe ones.

Abstract: In reinforcement learning from limited feedback (RLLF), only a small fraction of an offline dataset can be labeled with rewards, and the central question is which samples should be labeled to learn a high-performing policy from the resulting partially labeled dataset. Prior work formalized this as a reward-selection problem by focusing on the selection stage while treating downstream policy learning as a black box, in a regime where queried rewards are not retained for reward-model training. We instead study the retained-label setting, where queried rewards can be stored and used to fit a reward model before policy learning. We bound the suboptimality of the learned policy in terms of two sources of error: one arising from offline RL on a fixed, offline dataset, and the other from uncertainty in the reward model. Since reward selection cannot change the offline dataset, the limited labeling budget must be used to strategically reduce reward uncertainty. Motivated by RLLF's observation that useful rewards tend to keep the agent on high-return trajectories, we introduce Successor-Guided Uncertainty REduction (SURE), a novel method that uses successor features to select rewards that are both uncertain and associated with states from which high-valued states are reachable. We formally derive SURE from a bound-induced design objective and characterize its exact one-step marginal gain. Empirically, SURE reaches near full-feedback performance with few reward labels across a variety of domains and offline-RL backbones, yielding an effective method for feedback-efficient reinforcement learning.

</details>

In a cohort with parseable initial submissions, candidates added no correct answers when the previous draft was supplied, but added 27 points when it was absent. Abstract: In edge intelligence systems, computation offloading and dynamic voltage and frequency scaling (DVFS) are widely used to improve energy efficiency. However, as applications like deep neural network (DNN) inference evolve in complexity, their demands for memory resources have progressively increased, establishing memory as a critical bottleneck that impacts both latency and energy consumption. In this paper, we propose a joint optimization scheme integrating memory, computing and offloading for energy-efficient DNN inference. We formulate the energy minimization problem under deadline constraints as a mixed-integer nonlinear programming (MINLP) problem, where the main challenge arises from the coupling between offloading and resource optimization. To address this issue, we decompose the original problem into two subproblems under a fixed offloading policy. The local subproblem is transformed into an equivalent geometric programming (GP) problem to derive the optimal memory frequency and computing frequency, while the edge subproblem is solved via convex optimization. An efficient coordinate descent (CD) algorithm is further developed to jointly optimize all variables. Simulation results demonstrate that our proposed algorithm achieves near-optimal performance with the gap from the optimal policy being at most 9.3%, and reduces the energy consumption of devices by up to 16.1% compared with benchmarks without memory frequency optimization.

</details>

Abstract: Recent advances in reinforcement learning have made verifiable rewards a central post-training paradigm for improving multimodal reasoning. However, methods such as GRPO remain data- and compute-inefficient, spending rollout and annotation budget on prompts that provide little learning signal. We propose DISCOVER, an online data-discovery framework for budgeted multimodal GRPO that enables vision-language models to dynamically identify prompts that are most useful for the current policy. DISCOVER first organizes the multimodal sample pool into semantic clusters and queries a small set of density-diverse representative samples to obtain initial reward observations. During training, it estimates the utility of unannotated samples by transferring sparse reward feedback from queried samples. Since sample utility changes as the policy evolves, DISCOVER dynamically updates these estimates with recency-weighted rollout observations. It then selects only high-utility prompts for annotation using a policy-conditioned score that accounts for reward variance and difficulty. Once the discovery budget is exhausted, DISCOVER continues with utility-based replay. Experiments on ViRL39K and VLAA-Thinking with Qwen3-VL-2B show that DISCOVER substantially improves multimodal mathematical reasoning under a strict 10% unique-sample budget, outperforming budget-matched random selection, hard-example sampling, full-pool GRPO under the same training-step budget, and online selection baselines. Ablations further validate the importance of semantic coverage, online reward transfer, and stable discovery cadence, establishing DISCOVER as a practical framework for data-efficient multimodal RL post-training.

</details>

Abstract: Few-shot CLIP adaptation is often treated as a parameterization problem. Full fine-tuning is assumed to overfit, so parameter-efficient fine-tuning (PEFT) methods are preferred. In this paper, we show that this conclusion conflates parameterization with optimization. Across 11 datasets, 3 CLIP backbones, and 5 shot levels, dense full fine-tuning with plain SGD outperforms representative PEFT methods under the reported evaluation protocol, whereas Adam/AdamW collapses. We trace this collapse to unreliable support-set preconditioning. The few-shot second moment systematically underestimates the corresponding full-data statistic, already at initialization. Adam's inverse-square-root normalization converts this error into an oversized adaptive update. Matching Adam's first-step update budget to SGD resolves most of the collapse, whereas numerator-side alignment does not explain the failure. Shampoo and SOAP also fail, so richer preconditioning alone does not resolve the problem. Theory formalizes how few-shot estimation error is amplified into unsafe updates. SGD succeeds because it avoids unreliable preconditioners and stays close to pretrained CLIP. Interpolation probes show low-loss connections along tested directions, and curvature probes show favorable directional curvature. These results identify optimizer-induced preconditioning, not dense full fine-tuning itself, as the main source of failure in few-shot CLIP adaptation.

</details>

Abstract: Test-time reinforcement learning with majority-vote rewards trains a language model on unlabeled questions by rewarding the samples that agree with its most frequent answer. During training, each question locks into one answer, on hard questions often a wrong one, but which answer it locks into is known only after training. In this paper, we show that this outcome is decided early, in a short race driven by sampling noise between the answers that are close at the start, and we derive the probability of each outcome. With a separate logit for each answer, the runner-up wins with a normal tail probability of the initial gap, scaled by an explicit noise constant. In a network, however, the answer logits depend on shared parameters, and through the kernel of these parameters the updates of the trailing answers also change the gap of the two leaders. This spillover shifts the boundary between the starts won by each leader, and a closed form computes the shifted probability from a kernel probe and the start logits. In LoRA experiments with three models from two families, predictions made before training with the kernel frozen at its start value forecast which answer each question locks into better than a logistic model refitted on the outcomes, although they use no fitted constants. The closed form matches these predictions on the runs in which a check before training finds that the two leaders stay above the other answers, and it explains why a law that ignores the spillover needs a strongly inflated step.

</details>

Abstract: Large-scale device-level load forecasting for distribution transformers is challenged by substantial cross-device heterogeneity and the deployment burden of maintaining device-specific forecasting models. Existing approaches typically rely either on device-specific models, which are difficult to scale to large transformer populations, or on global models, which reduce deployment complexity but may overlook heterogeneous load patterns across devices. To address this trade-off, this study develops a trend-clustered forecasting framework that enables model sharing among transformers with similar temporal behaviors. Representation Learning for Time Series using Time-Embeddings (T-Rep) is employed to learn compact temporal representations of transformer load series, which are subsequently grouped using a Gaussian mixture model (GMM). Representative transformers are sampled within each cluster to train cluster-specific Patch Time Series Transformer (PatchTST) models while retaining device-level predictions. The framework is evaluated using real-world 15-min operational data from nearly one thousand distribution transformers. The proposed framework reduces mean absolute error (MAE) by approximately 20% relative to the direct PatchTST baseline, achieves a high-load warning accuracy of 95.66%, while requiring only seven cluster-specific forecasting models. These results demonstrate that behavior-based device organization provides an effective balance between forecasting accuracy, deployment scalability, and risk-oriented operational support for distribution transformer management.

Abstract: Multimodal-Attributed Graphs (MAGs) organize heterogeneous data in graphs whose nodes are characterized by modalities such as text and images rather than traditional unimodal attributes. However, many representative MAG methods organize propagation around node-level states and topology-defined neighborhoods, which can obscure fine-grained modality contributions: a bounded neighborhood may contain semantically weak evidence, while compatible context need not be confined to it. To address both limitations, we propose TEAM, a modality-centric framework that constructs and encodes graph context with node-modality tokens rather than treating each node as a single interaction unit. Concretely, TEAM unfolds the local topology into a Modality Encoding Tree and performs tree-anchored semantic evidence acquisition, organizing local structural context while expanding the accessible evidence space outside the constructed local Tree. We further introduce the Modality-Source Context Transformer (MSCT), which encodes the selected node-modality tokens and coordinates interactions among modality-source groups. By retaining textual and visual attributes as separate interaction units until readout, it supports fine-grained cross-node cross-modal exchange without first collapsing them into whole-node features. Across the evaluated baselines, TEAM attains the highest reported mean on all graph-learning metrics, all three G2Text metrics, and both G2Image metrics. On Toys, it surpasses the strongest baseline by 3.37 and 2.93 percentage points in Accuracy and Macro-F1, respectively.

Abstract: On the Web, user profiling builds compact user representations from interaction histories for efficient personalization, and dynamic user profiling updates them as users' behavior and preferences change. However, new user behavior can be ambiguous, and its interpretation differs across users. To address this, we introduce Uncertainty-aware Adaptive Profile Manager (UAPM), which separates the claims in a user profile from interpretations of new behavior that are not yet confirmed, through a Profile, a Buffer, and a Rulebook. UAPM records user-specific interpretation and update rules in the Rulebook, keeps ambiguous evidence in the Buffer outside the Profile as evidence for later decisions, and writes supported general and situational characteristics to the Profile. We train UAPM with Privileged On-Policy Distillation with Dual Specialization (POPD-DS), which learns from recorded future interactions during training without access to them at deployment. Its first stage, Dual Specialization, distills a stronger model into a current-only Student and a future-informed model that becomes the Teacher. In the second stage, privileged on-policy distillation (POPD), only the fixed Teacher receives future interactions and guides the Student's own management decisions alongside task rewards. Experiments on three Web personalization tasks show that UAPM outperforms multiple baselines under the same downstream input budget, improving on the best same-budget baseline by up to 5%.

Abstract: MeanFlow learns interval-average velocity for few-step generation, but its fixed temporal sampler mixes diagonal and interior supervision throughout training. We interpret temporal coordinates as collocation points: diagonal samples provide a stochastic boundary target, whereas finite intervals supply a differential-consistency target. The diagonal fraction controls how often the consistency component appears, while interval length enters its gradient through an explicit factor. Motivated by this distinction, EbbFlow uses Temporal Supervision Allocation (TSA) to schedule the diagonal fraction and interval geometry. It starts with diagonal supervision, then admits interior samples and expands their maximum interval; a left-anchored interval law preserves coverage across time. The per-sample loss, target, backbone, and inference procedure remain unchanged. In matched class-conditional ImageNet $256\times 256$ runs without classifier-free guidance (DiT-B/2, one training seed per method, 400k updates), EbbFlow achieves 50k-sample FID of 39.65 at one network evaluation and 36.02 at two-evaluation ODE sampling, versus 44.82 and 39.86 for MeanFlow. Gradient probes show a mid-training difference in component geometry. Ablations support allocation scheduling and left-anchored intervals, although a fixed full-horizon variant performs better at one evaluation. These results suggest that temporal sampling is a useful training control whose benefits depend on the inference protocol.

</details>

Abstract: Activation sparsity can reduce FFN weight traffic during single-user LLM decoding, but active channels must be identified early enough to avoid fetching unused weights. In pretrained SwiGLU models, training-free routing with the native gate performs substantially worse than routing with the up activation at high sparsity. We show that this ordering reverses after sparsity-aware training. Based on this insight, we introduce SGA-Sparse, which converts pretrained SwiGLU LLMs into fixed-budget activation-sparse models by training the existing gate projection directly as a top-k router. SGA-Sparse updates all model parameters end-to-end while preserving the original SwiGLU activation and introducing no auxiliary router parameters. After approximately 30B training tokens, LLaMA models from 1B to 8B achieve uniform per-token 75-85% FFN channel sparsity while remaining within 0.2-2.7 pp of their original dense checkpoints in average zero-shot accuracy. Because post-training quantization degrades sparsity-trained checkpoints, we further study two complementary quantization-aware training (QAT) recipes: continued-QAT largely recovers performance at 4-bit weight quantization, while joint-QAT performs better for the more aggressive sub-4-bit target. To compare activation sparsity with dense low-bit quantization, we translate the sparsity-induced reduction in weight traffic into an equivalent bit-width. Under this unified weight-traffic metric, our 2.5- and 1.5-bit-equivalent models establish a new accuracy-traffic Pareto frontier: the 2.5-bit-equivalent models match dense 3-bit QAT accuracy, while the 1.5-bit-equivalent models approach dense 2-bit models and outperform dense ternary models by 1.3 pp on average. These results establish native-gate training as a practical approach for combining high FFN sparsity with low-bit weights.

</details>

Abstract: Post-hoc explanation methods for neural networks are often heuristic and lack provable guarantees. A common alternative to such methods that was studied in recent years is to compute a cardinally-minimal subset of input features that is provably sufficient to determine the model's prediction. For general neural networks, however, finding such explanations is computationally intractable. In contrast, recent work showed that this problem becomes more tractable for a particular family of neural networks, namely neural additive models (NAMs), though standard NAMs are highly restrictive because they exclude feature interactions altogether. In this work, we study the computation of provably minimal and sufficient explanations for neural networks with restricted feature interactions, bridging the gap between fully general neural networks and purely additive models. We first prove that even with constant-order interactions, the problem is $Σ_2^P$-hard, implying that worst-case exponential complexity is unavoidable. We then identify two provably tractable settings: one based on sparsity in the interaction structure and another based on a stronger interaction-aware notion of sufficiency. Under either of these settings, we develop algorithms that compute provable explanations for neural networks with any-order interactions while retaining much of the computational efficiency of additive models. Empirically, we show that these explanations are significantly smaller and faster to obtain than those produced by standard algorithms, while being derived from models that achieve much higher accuracy than standard NAMs. Overall, our results help expand the class of neural networks that admit efficient provable explanations, while clarifying the fundamental role of feature interactions in shaping their complexity.

</details>

Abstract: Machine Learning Interatomic Potentials (MLIPs) based on Equivariant Graph Neural Networks (EGNNs) have achieved remarkable progress in atomistic modeling. However, accurately representing atomic interactions across different spatial scales remains challenging. In this work, we investigate the radial spectral properties of SO(3)-equivariant message passing and establish that the cutoff radius controls the characteristic spatial-frequency scale of radial interactions. Based on this analysis, we propose Multi-Cutoff Spectral Aggregation (MCSA), a plug-and-play module that aggregates radial kernels with different cutoff radii to enrich multi-scale geometric representations. To achieve efficient implementation, we exploit the compact support of radial kernels to fuse cutoff-dependent radial weights before angular tensor-product operations, avoiding redundant angular computations while preserving rotational equivariance. Extensive experiments across representative EGNN backbones demonstrate consistent improvements in energy, force, and stress prediction, as well as enhanced accuracy in higher-order properties, including phonon dispersion and lattice thermal conductivity. Further evaluations on layered materials with distinct interaction scales demonstrate the effectiveness of physically motivated cutoff selection. Abstract: Direct Preference Optimization (DPO) is a simple and widely used alternative to RLHF for aligning large language models (LLMs), but its sequence-level objective cannot precisely locate or suppress the local patterns that make an otherwise acceptable response unsafe or undesirable. We propose TLRS-DPO (Token-Level Risk Scoring for Better Direct Preference Optimization), which augments DPO with fine-grained token-level control while remaining fully offline and reference-based. TLRS-DPO introduces a token-level robust compensation term that converts the policy-reference log-ratio on rejected responses into localized risk scores, a curriculum-style schedule that gradually increases the strength of this compensation during training, and a risk-adaptive screening mechanism that selectively penalizes only high-risk tokens instead of entire sequences. This design bridges sequence-level preference fitting and token-level behavior control, and can be implemented as a drop-in modification to standard DPO trainers. Empirically, TLRS-DPO improves alignment quality and safety-relevant behavior with minimal impact on helpfulness compared to strong DPO baselines.

Abstract: Asynchronous pipeline parallelism can accelerate distributed model training, but suffers from instability caused by inconsistent gradient computations. We identify a fundamental source of this inconsistency: error signals are coupled to forward-time features, not just parameter states. This means that parameters may evolve as long as they preserve forward-equivalence. Leveraging this insight, we introduce FRESCO, which frames asynchronous updates as a constrained optimization problem: it finds the minimal update modification that acts trivially on the activation subspace. We demonstrate that FRESCO consistently outperforms representative asynchronous baselines in LLM pre-training, with the gains most pronounced under deep pipelines and high inconsistency levels: at pipeline depth 32, it reaches the same loss as the strongest baseline with 43% fewer iterations and 30% less wall-clock time, while requiring 8~921$\times$ less memory overhead. Moving beyond the current limitations, FRESCO establishes a new foundation for high-utilization pipeline parallel training, providing a scalable path for large-model development on diverse, resource-constrained systems.

</details>

Abstract: Learning neural solvers for spatiotemporal partial differential equations (PDEs) under real-world constraints remains a key challenge in scientific machine learning, especially for inverse tasks with sparse and noisy boundary observations. We present the Aletheia dataset, the first 3D benchmark for learning data-driven solvers in the context of nondestructive testing (NDT). The dataset simulates eddy-current-induced heating in conductive solids and models the resulting transient heat propagation governed by the heat equation. Aletheia contains over 4,700 high-resolution samples across 10 excitation frequencies (1-100,kHz), each providing volumetric heat source and temperature fields over time. It supports both forward prediction of temperature evolution and inverse reconstruction of internal heat sources or defects from surface infrared measurements. Real infrared thermography data from cracked rail specimens are included for calibration and comparison. We define four canonical tasks on both regular and irregular grids and benchmark them using various neural operators. Aletheia establishes a unified platform for evaluating neural PDE solvers under realistic NDT conditions, enabling progress in reliable, data-driven inverse modeling. The dataset and code are publicly available at https://github.com/sherlock-0327/ALETHEIA.

Abstract: Does reinforcement learning teach language models new capabilities, or merely amplify ones acquired during pre-training? The literature offers evidence for both: pass@$k$ studies suggest RL reweights latent behaviours while some controlled composition studies demonstrate gains beyond simple amplification. We argue that this question has no answer independent of the capability state of the base model, and propose a two-dimensional state space for post-trainability. The first axis, \emph{effective full-solution support}, measures whether successful trajectories can be sampled under a feasible exploration budget. The second axis, \emph{atomic primitive coverage}, measures whether the constituent skills of a solution can be executed when the burden of composition is removed. Together, these dimensions motivate three regimes -- amplification, composition-limited, and acquisition-limited -- each predicting a distinct empirical signature and calling for a distinct intervention. We propose a prospective measurement protocol and investigate the framework using synthetic composition tasks across three small language models. We find that primitive mastery alone does not guarantee that RL can learn a composition. Checkpoints that execute every primitive reliably can fail to improve when complete solutions are never sampled during training, whereas modest composition supervision that establishes nonzero support enables substantial RL gains. The framework reframes mid-training as preparation for post-training: its role is to move models into states from which RL can operate effectively.

</details>
