Abstract: We evaluate AI agents in an online setting where tasks arrive sequentially and, before running each task, we construct an upper predictive bound on its \emph{time-to-success}---the number of interactions until the task is completed successfully. Under a limited budget, some runs are stopped early, so their time-to-success is unobserved (right-censored), and the correctness of the bound may be unknown. We introduce \emph{\textbf{PR}edictive Bounds for \textbf{O}nline \textbf{B}udget \textbf{E}fficiency} (\texttt{PROBE}), a method for constructing calibrated upper predictive bounds while adaptively allocating a limited budget across agent tasks to reduce the anticipated coverage deficit. Our method calibrates the bounds online using inverse-probability-weighted feedback together with a correction designed to minimize coverage deficit while remaining unbiased. We prove that \texttt{PROBE} provides a coverage guarantee for any finite time horizon while satisfying the budget constraint. Experiments on software engineering, retrieval-augmented generation, and instruction following show that \texttt{PROBE} attains a coverage rate closer to the target level with tighter predictive bounds than a calibrated baseline.

Abstract: Quadratic manifold fitting provides a flexible framework for modeling nonlinear low-dimensional structures embedded in high-dimensional spaces. However, existing methods typically employ prescribed noise models or fixed loss functions, limiting their adaptability to varying noise characteristics. To address this limitation, we propose an alternating maximum likelihood estimation method for subspace-constrained quadratic matrix factorization, termed AMLE-SQMF, which jointly estimates the quadratic manifold and the underlying noise distribution. Specifically, we model the residuals using a generalized Gaussian distribution (GGD), whose shape parameter controls the tail behavior and determines the associated fitting loss. By exploiting the closed-form maximum likelihood estimator of the scale parameter, we eliminate the scale variable and derive a profile negative log-likelihood. Based on this formulation, we develop an alternating optimization algorithm that iteratively updates the manifold parameters and estimates the noise shape parameter. This enables adaptive loss selection within a unified likelihood-based framework, without requiring prior specification of the noise characteristics. Experiments on synthetic and real-world datasets demonstrate the effectiveness of AMLE-SQMF in quadratic manifold fitting and its robustness to diverse noise distributions.

</details>

Abstract: High-resolution weather prediction is important for resolving local atmospheric patterns missed by coarse global forecasts. Large pretrained weather backbones have improved global forecasting, but adapting them to produce future high-resolution fields from recent coarse states remains challenging and costly. We formulate this setting as Predictive Spatiotemporal Weather Downscaling (PSWD), a task that jointly refines spatial and temporal resolution while predicting future high-resolution trajectories. To this end, we propose a framework that decomposes prediction into spatial and temporal stages using a shared pretrained backbone, fixed numerical scaffolds, and lightweight residual heads. For backbone adaptation, we introduce PROLA (PRincipal-Orthogonal Low-rank Adaptation), which allocates a fixed low-rank budget between pretrained weights' principal directions and their orthogonal complement. PROLA further rescales rank-1 optimizer updates using gradient signals, improving adaptation without increasing trainable rank. Experiments in the PSWD setting show that PROLA outperforms representative low-rank adaptation baselines. Further experiments across diverse datasets and tasks support the broader applicability of PROLA, showing that its benefits extend beyond the primary weather downscaling setting.

Abstract: Sensor outages can conceal prediction-interval coverage deficits behind acceptable marginal performance, while unavailable future readings leave some issued forecasts unscorable. We present a measurement-record audit that links issue-time outage duration, original target availability, and time-uniform statistical evidence. The audit retains complete forecast keys, batches all sensors and overlapping horizons at each measurement tick, and projects simultaneous covered-count and scored-count bounds onto a cumulative conditional expected coverage ratio. This target is distinguished from empirical coverage and outage-conditional population coverage. Selected traffic and air-quality results illustrate the gap between pooled and long-outage coverage; in METR-LA, only 7.6% of issued long-gap forecasts have originally observed targets. Of 30 primary sequential outage-state audits, 26 are inconclusive and four lack scored support. This concise presentation gives the core construction and selected aggregate evidence without the full experimental supplement.

Abstract: Agentic reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards trains language models through multi-turn tool interactions, yet their reasoning performance and tool-calling ability can decline during training. We trace this degradation to three coupled mechanisms: (1) tool-token imbalance produces negative overall tool credit when incorrect trajectories contain more tool tokens per turn than correct ones; (2) aggregation amplification can further strengthen this negative signal as token-mean aggregation gives longer incorrect trajectories greater weight in policy updates; and (3) failure-driven retries prolong trajectories as models tend to call tools again after execution errors, extending potential negative credit to recovery attempts. To counter this suppression, we propose Transition-Aware Credit Assignment (TACA), which targets the transition from reasoning to tool use through supervision of both call initiation and generation. Specifically, TACA uses Advantage Projection to enforce a nonnegative mean advantage on tool-call tokens within each prompt group through a minimal uniform shift. Additionally, TACA guides tool-use decisions with Decision Contrast, comparing rewards for tool-assisted and direct answers to the same prompt. Through Supervised Replay, it reinforces tool-call generation using calls from previously sampled correct trajectories. Across models and tasks, TACA improves performance and sustains more reliable tool interaction during training, raising mean accuracy over MT-GRPO on six reasoning benchmarks by 4.61 percentage points for Qwen2.5-1.5B and 3.61 for Qwen2.5-3B. Our code is available at https://github.com/tmlr-group/TACA.

</details>

Abstract: Modern state-space models owe much of their computational efficiency to their linear, often diagonal, dynamics. Classical recurrent neural networks instead allow nonlinear state-to-state interactions, which can be beneficial when memory updates need to depend on the past context. In this paper, we ask whether state feedback through a simple gate regulating memory updates can enhance the expressivity of diagonal state-space models. To investigate this question, we compare gates that depend on the current input, the recurrent state, or both. We establish a strict expressivity separation: for scalar models, state gating solves induction-head tasks for arbitrary vocabulary size and sequence length, whereas input gating fails on a finite instance under standard stability assumptions on the state transition matrix. Experiments show that state gating retains its advantage on induction-head tasks in higher-dimensional models, whereas input gating performs better on selective copying tasks. Together, our results show that even a simple, diagonal form of state-feedback gating can unlock capabilities unavailable to input-controlled models, while input and state gating offer complementary strengths and achieve the strongest overall performance when combined.

</details>

Abstract: We study online reinforcement learning for the stochastic shortest path (SSP) problem with unknown transition dynamics. Although existing posterior-sampling methods perform well empirically on SSPs, their regret guarantees do not achieve the minimax-optimal order. We address this limitation by proposing OPSRL-SSP, an optimistic posterior-sampling algorithm for the SSP setting. The algorithm operates in epochs and uses a logarithmic, time-dependent schedule for the number of posterior samples. To induce optimism, we augment the transition model with a pseudo-state assigned an optimistic boundary value. We establish a high-probability regret bound of $\tilde{O}(B_\star \sqrt{SAK} + B_\star S^2 A)$, where $B_\star$ is an upper bound on the expected cost of the optimal policy, $S$ and $A$ are the numbers of states and actions, respectively, and $K$ is the number of episodes. In addition, we extend the analysis to general zero-cost SSPs using a universal cost-perturbation argument. The leading term matches the minimax lower bound $Ω(B_\star \sqrt{SAK})$, thereby addressing the open problem raised by Jafarnia-Jahromi et al. [19] for posterior sampling in the SSP setting.

It can, but less than it looks. We tested an open 9-billion-parameter model with fixed weights; a larger teacher model wrote its notes. On generated customer-service decisions, where both note writers were told the target rules, notes based on the agent's mistakes improved accuracy by 4.0 percentage points over notes from the same process with the mistakes withheld. That process alone, trying and keeping better-scoring notes, reached about 60% of the total improvement. In full customer-service conversations the same notes showed no demonstrated improvement. Separately, a strict screen of single lessons admitted two customer-service lessons, still awaiting tests on new data, and one tool-use lesson, written without seeing the agent's mistakes, which showed no demonstrated gain on new tool-use tasks. Abstract: Spatial reasoning requires understanding how objects and observers are arranged in a scene and how their relations change with viewpoint or movement. Vision-language models (VLMs) typically perform both steps through model-internal reasoning, forming an implicit scene interpretation from partial views and imagining the consequences of spatial transformations. A misread scene can lead to a wrong answer, while even a correct interpretation does not ensure that imagined transformations satisfy spatial constraints. We introduce Spatial Harness, which represents scenes as explicit and executable world states to ground spatial reasoning in physics-engine feedback. A Perceiver integrates scene constraints extracted from the question with specialized visual estimates to place objects and cameras in a shared coordinate frame. The resulting state is instantiated in a physics engine, where a Reasoner executes viewpoint changes and uses computed measurements and rendered views to guide reasoning. We evaluate on MindCube, SAT, MMSI-Bench, and SpatialRGPT-Bench with open- and closed-weight VLMs. On MindCube, Spatial Harness improves Qwen3.5-27B from 52.70% to 71.14%, surpassing pySpatial by 7.52 percentage points. Given identical annotated object and camera poses, it reaches 98.25% versus 71.81% for model-internal reasoning. Code is publicly available at: https://github.com/tmlr-group/Spatial-Harness

Abstract: Diffusion Transformer (DiT) models, while having become a powerful backbone for generative synthesis, suffer from high inference cost on generating high-resolution images. Diffusion caching has emerged as a promising acceleration technique to reduce inference cost. However, existing caching strategies fail to accurately identify the varying importance of different computational modules and rarely coordinate cache reusing across the timestep and layer granularities, making it difficult to control error accumulation. To address these limitations, we propose multi-granularity caching (MG-Cache) for DiTs, a novel and fully training-free approach to exploit computational redundancy both on timesteps and Transformer layers. Our core innovation contains a two-stage caching strategy: an offline timestep profiling analysis that identifies redundant timesteps for cache reuse, followed by an adaptive layer-wise caching strategy that combines input stability with importance of each Transformer layer to selectively cache stable Attention and MLP submodules. Extensive experiments on mainstream open-source DiT models validate the effectiveness of our approach, achieving significant acceleration while maintaining high fidelity to the uncached output, with up to a 4.18x acceleration on HunyuanVideo.

Abstract: Block-diffusion drafters propose a whole block of tokens in one forward pass, and draft trees built from their position-wise marginals increase the expected accepted length of speculative decoding. Every tree node occupies a query row in target verification, so under concurrent serving low-yield branches compete with other requests for verification capacity. Existing tree methods select verified nodes by draft probability under a fixed or cost-selected per-request budget, and thus always retain a prefix of the drafter's ranking. We present D3Tree, which learns which nodes of a block-diffusion draft tree are worth verifying. A tree-size head with 2H+2 parameters predicts each candidate's conditional survival from the draft state, the candidate embedding, and its draft log-probability. Thresholding the product of these scores along parent chains yields an ancestor-closed subtree whose size adapts to each request and decoding round. The head is trained on a frozen drafter with conditional labels on the full candidate tree; an optional joint mode also steers the drafter toward the retained reference path. At serving time, D3Tree verifies whole trees under light load and, under heavy load, allocates one batch-wide node budget by predicted survival, sized to the target's matrix-multiplication tiles. Retained trees share one ancestor-masked target pass, while construction, pruning, and scheduling run on the GPU and overlap with the host. Across six vision-language verifiers and 17 text and multimodal benchmarks, D3Tree is fastest in 70 of 72 load settings, with 3.2-4.0x speedup over autoregressive decoding for one request and 1.3-1.8x at 64 concurrent requests, where fixed-budget trees fall below autoregressive speed. Under a time-varying arrival trace, it has the lowest mean end-to-end latency in every arrival phase.

Abstract: Domain adaptation or DA methods are indispensable when target labels or computational resources are scarce, yet the domain kernel density and subspace alignment approaches completely assume a Hilbertian geometry.
  They measure distributional discrepancy with symmetric L2 divergences over Euclidean feature spaces or reproducing kernel Hilbert spaces (RKHS). This preprint summarizes the manuscript Non-Hilbertian Domain Adaptation. Total Variation Alignment via L1 Density Regularization on Feature Manifolds, which proposes Banach Manifold Transfer Learning or BMTL. BMTL makes two coupled design decisions. The first one is that features are embedded on a geodesic manifold rather than treated as points of a flat Euclidean space, and the second one is that the density matching regularizer is formulated in an L1 Banach norm, whose minimization is equivalent, by Scheffe's theorem, to contracting the Total Variation or TV distance between domain densities, a bounded metric that weights all regions of the sample space uniformly. The L2 formulation, by contrast, squares deviations and therefore amplifies errors in the tails, over emphasizing density outliers that do not reflect meaningful distributional shift. Experiments on MNIST-USPS, COIL20, and Office+Caltech10 show consistent gains; the L1 variant beats an otherwise identical L2 ablation on every task, for example 92.44% vs. 89.80% on USPS->MNIST and surpasses the strongest baseline DMDA by +3.68% on average over twelve Office+Caltech transfers. This document situates the contribution with an explicit comparison of Euclidean, Hilbert, and Banach geometries.

</details>

Abstract: Single-loop minimax methods are appealing for modern machine learning, but standard stochastic gradient updates can be unstable and oscillatory under tightly coupled primal-dual dynamics, making performance notoriously sensitive to stepsizes. To fundamentally improve stability, we develop an orthogonal update framework tailored to minimax optimization, which applies updates along orthogonalized directions to reduce update anisotropy. However, orthogonalization reshapes the optimization geometry and makes coordinating primal-dual stepsizes even more delicate. To overcome this challenge, we develop AdaSGDA, which equips orthogonal updates with an adaptive stepsize rule driven by accumulated gradient norms. This mechanism automatically balances coupled primal-dual progress under orthogonalization, yielding an effectively parameter-free method for nonconvex-strongly-concave minimax optimization. We prove that AdaSGDA avoids problem-dependent tuning while attaining the state-of-the-art $\mathcal{O}\left(T^{-1/4}\right)$ convergence rate. We further propose AdaSGDA-VR, which incorporates a carefully designed variance reduction scheme and achieves the faster $\mathcal{O}\left(T^{-1/3}\right)$ rate. Abstract: Earth observation foundation models produce global metre-resolution embeddings that underpin work across agriculture, ecology, hydrology, and beyond. Yet these embeddings are opaque: each dimension entangles unrelated physical concepts, which makes them difficult to interpret and their robustness hard to assess. Here, we propose sparse autoencoders (SAEs) as a principled way to decompose Earth embeddings into sparse, approximately monosemantic features over an overcomplete dictionary. Because LLM judges of interpretations do not transfer to satellite pixels, and spatial autocorrelation matters, we introduce a tailored evaluation protocol. It scores any SAE on reconstruction at matched sparsity, alignment with reference concepts, and spatial scale, each against baselines that include an SAE whose decoder is a frozen random dictionary. On this protocol, SAEs reconstruct AlphaEarth and Tessera embeddings better than every baseline at matched sparsity. Their features align with far fewer reference concepts than raw embedding dimensions (on AlphaEarth, 88% align with at most one of 20 concept groups, most with none, against a median of 12 per raw dimension). They also carry more of their variance at fine spatial scales (23 to 32% within a 640 m patch for BatchTopK and Matryoshka, against 9%). An SAE with a frozen random decoder shares both properties, which therefore follow from sparsity, whereas only a learned dictionary reconstructs the embedding accurately: on AlphaEarth, trained SAEs leave eight times less variance unexplained. We test the sparse code on two applications, discovering land-cover sub-concepts and detecting out-of-distribution pixels by area-of-applicability estimation. Both indicate how sparse features could be used, but neither is yet reliable enough for practical use. Together, these results are a step toward Earth observation models whose features can be inspected.

</details>

Abstract: We study offline policy optimization in finite-horizon Markov decision processes where every deterministic Markov policy has an action-value function linear in known features, with coefficient norm at most $B$. From independent complete trajectories of one unknown Markov behavior policy, and under all-policy occupancy coverage, we give an algorithm with $\widetilde O(\varepsilon^{-2})$ sample dependence and computational complexity polynomial in $\log(2+B)$. The algorithm performs exactly one convex regression per layer. Its stopped-return targets are the values of a single consistent Markov witness policy. We also establish a covariance-coverage variant with uniform approximate realizability and polynomial dependence on $B$. A two-action lower bound shows that the accuracy exponent $2$ is necessary.

Abstract: The order in which propagators are executed can substantially affect the performance of constraint programming solvers, yet propagator priorities are typically determined by fixed, manually designed heuristics. In this work, we explore learning instance-dependent propagator priorities from static problem features. We formulate the task as a learning-to-rank problem, addressing the challenge that the quality of a priority assignment can be measured through solver runtime, while the desired priorities of individual propagators are not directly observable. A self-attention-based model jointly scores the propagators present in an instance and is trained using pairwise preferences derived from solver runtimes measured under randomized propagator priority assignments, with a Plackett-Luce-based ranking objective. Predicted rankings are mapped to the discrete priority levels of the Choco solver before search begins, without modifying its propagation or search procedures. We evaluate the approach on XCSP3 instances and demonstrate that propagator ordering can have a substantial impact on solving time. On a test set of 698 instances, picked out as most difficult by problem class, the learned priorities increase the number of solved instances from 634 to 654, reduce median solver runtime by 27.93%, and reduce aggregate penalized runtime (PAR1) by 12.65% compared with Choco's default priorities. We further observe that improvements vary across problem classes and show that a simple policy selecting between learned and default priorities further increases the number of solved instances. These results demonstrate the potential of learning instance-dependent propagator priorities to improve constraint programming performance, particularly on difficult instances.

</details>

Abstract: On-policy distillation (OPD) trains a student language model on its own responses, guided by a fixed teacher. Generating these responses is expensive, so systems use quantized inference and asynchronous rollouts to reduce the cost. Both, however, introduce training-inference mismatch between the rollout engine and the model being trained. Such mismatch can destabilize reinforcement learning (RL), but its effects on OPD remain unclear. This paper investigates how much mismatch OPD tolerates, where its effects arise, and how training systems can use this tolerance. Measuring all mismatch sources on one scale, we examine quantization across three student-teacher pairs, with additional tests of weight staleness and injected noise on one pair. OPD tolerates substantial mismatch without meaningful loss of training quality but degrades at higher levels, revealing an empirical mismatch budget. Both the quality cost of mismatch and the mismatch introduced by quantization depend on the model, with some layers particularly sensitive to reduced precision. In controlled comparisons, OPD remains stable under rollout configurations that destabilize RL methods. Under weight noise, mismatch also shifts the prefixes reached during generation, introducing errors that per-token corrections cannot address. To put these findings into practice, we introduce a lightweight admission check that screens rollout configurations against a calibrated budget without requiring a full training run for each candidate. Model-specific FP8 recipes preserve training quality while providing capacity for at least $1.93\times$ as many KV-cache tokens as BF16. Combining FP8 with asynchronous rollouts reaches the same training quality $1.62\times$ sooner than synchronous BF16 training. Code is available at https://github.com/benyucong/train-inference-mismatch-opd

Abstract: Temporally correlated noise can substantially improve the utility of differentially private optimization, yet candidate mechanisms are commonly designed or evaluated using only partial information about the optimization process, such as workload structure, learning-rate schedules, or local curvature.   We introduce MOMENT-DP, an optimizer-state-aware framework that models how temporally correlated privacy noise propagates through momentum-based optimization. For locally quadratic objectives, MOMENT-DP expresses privacy-induced final loss through an optimizer transfer operator that jointly captures curvature, learning-rate scheduling, momentum, and temporal noise covariance. NoiseCurve's curvature-aware quadratic criterion is recovered exactly as the zero-momentum, constant-learning-rate special case.

The effect persists outside exact quadratic dynamics in per-example-clipped private logistic regression, while remaining unresolved in the neural regime we study. These results establish optimizer state as an under-modeled, decision-relevant axis in evaluating temporally correlated privacy noise.

Abstract: In multi-institutional biomedical data analysis, comprehensive disease profiles are often fragmented across different data silos. Although Hierarchical Clustering (HC) is a powerful tool for exploring such data topologies, its cumbersome recursive process incurs prohibitive computational overhead and renders it highly susceptible to privacy leakage. This paper, therefore, introduces an efficient one-shot hierarchical FC framework that performs client-end (institutions) distribution exploration and server-end distribution aggregation through one-way prototype-level communication from clients to the server. Although the designed one-shot prototype transmission guarantees minimal computational overhead and mitigates privacy risks, it inevitably incurs information loss. To counteract this, a multi-granular competitive learning mechanism is proposed on the server to fuse the clusterlets, effectively maximizing the retention and mining of global distribution topologies under limited, one-way communication constraints. It turns out that the complex cluster distributions across clients can be efficiently explored, and extensive experiments comparing state-of-the-art methods on public datasets demonstrate the superiority of the proposed framework in balancing robust clustering, privacy preservation, and computational efficiency.

Abstract: Predictive algorithms are increasingly used to guide hospital discharge decisions, yet these models can exhibit censoring bias for vulnerable patients due to insufficient historical data or past discharge decisions. When biased readmission risk predictors are used to inform discharges, the training data generated by discharge decisions may perpetuate similar biases when predictors are re-trained, leading to a vicious cycle. We extend a queueing-based Markov decision process for hospital discharge to incorporate periodically retrained readmission risk predictors. Our key insight is that cost-optimal, "capacity-aware" operational decision policies can naturally diversify the lengths of stay at which patient outcomes are observed, enabling consistent estimation of readmission risk without explicit fairness constraints. We prove that repeated retraining under discharge-up-to policies converges to unbiased risk estimates under well-specification. In synthetic experiments and calibrated simulations based on patient-level data from a partner hospital, we find that where commonly used threshold-on-risk policies can remain persistently biased, discharge-up-to policies reduce learning error over status quo decision policies (such as thresholding on risk) by 24%-25%. These improvements to estimation also mitigate some forms of bias, reducing readmission cost disparities (adjusted for baseline risk differences between demographic groups) by 38%-63%. Our results demonstrate how capacity-aware decision policies can improve both predictive performance and fairness by addressing censoring bias.

</details>

Abstract: We study stochastic bandits with reward-dependent delays, possibly infinite, whose reports omit the originating round. For each $c\in[0,1)$, Balanced Historical Optimism (BHO) has a delay term controlled by the largest suboptimal-arm quantile at level $1-cΔ_i$, without requiring optimal-arm reports. Abstract: The performance of language agents depends jointly on model weights and the harness. Optimizing either alone can be constrained by the other component, limiting overall performance gains. Our analysis of naive alternating training reveals progressive incompatibility between the model and harness, which increasingly interferes with task execution and may also undermine model training, leading to performance degradation. To coordinate the evolution of models and harnesses, we introduce JoMoHa, to our knowledge the first framework to jointly train an executable harness generation policy and a task execution policy through reinforcement learning. JoMoHa outperforms the evaluated weight-only and harness-only baselines and achieves the highest success rates among the evaluated methods on ALFWorld, WebShop, and DBBench, averaging 70.3% and reaching 97.0% on ALFWorld. By learning both task execution and runtime improvement from its own generated trajectories, JoMoHa performs recursive self-improvement (RSI).

Abstract: Due to the fundamental problem of causal inference -- the impossibility of observing counterfactual outcomes -- there is always a gap between the training goal (outcome, propensity) and the inference target (CATE, ATE). Traditional causal effect estimation revolves around setting assumptions, selecting identification strategies, and choosing causal estimators, while more recently, Causal Foundation Models (CFMs), built on Prior-Data-Fitted Networks (PFNs), ease this process by pretraining on massive synthetic prior datasets. Despite their success, such amortization makes causal inference less transparent, obscuring which assumptions the model implicitly relies on for a given dataset. The high diversity of real-world data-generating processes (DGPs) and Structural Causal Models (SCMs) makes it hard to know, a priori, which causal inference strategy is compatible with a given dataset. In this work, we focus on this compatibility between an observational dataset and method-specific assumptions, and find that geometric characteristics of data embeddings -- specifically, the variogram -- provide a useful signal of whether a given causal estimation strategy is appropriate. We develop MoE-Learner, a mixture-of-causal-experts framework that embeds a dataset under multiple assumption-specific views and uses the variogram as a counterfactual-free router to assess each expert's validity. Rather than eliminating causal assumptions, this shifts their validation from a fixed, a priori choice to a criterion that can be checked directly from data geometry. MoE-Learner achieves the best average rank on benchmark datasets (IHDP, ACIC, Lalonde). Being orthogonal to (causal) foundation models, our method builds a bridge from data characteristics to causal estimation strategy rather than proposing another CFM.

</details>

Abstract: Lookup-table (LUT)-based neural networks (NNs) offer a compelling path to ultra-low-latency inference on FPGAs. However, existing LUT-based neural networks have largely been restricted to MLP-style topologies, leaving structured vision and audio tasks underexplored. We present ConvLUT, a family of fully unrolled LUT-mappable convolutional architectures targeting the MLPerf Tiny benchmarks. It is comprised of a standard convolutional model built around a hierarchical tree of LUTs and a depthwise separable model following the MobileNet design philosophy. These architectures are enabled by four key contributions: a unified LUT-based convolutional framework supporting standard, depthwise, and pointwise convolutions under explicit fan-in constraints; a LUT-tree that addresses the $k^2c$ receptive field dimensionality bottleneck; a channel-partitioned LUT pooling layer that replaces conventional spatial pooling; and a hardware-aware iterative pruning strategy for stable convergence at scale. Evaluated on CIFAR-10, Visual Wake Words, and Keyword Spotting, ConvLUT achieves competitive accuracy with fully unrolled inference latencies below $\sim 50$ ns. On CIFAR-10 it reaches at least a $8844\times$ latency reduction over non-logic-based prior implementations, and outperforms existing MLP-style LUT-based models on the same task by at least $20$ pp in test accuracy.

Abstract: Understanding how an athlete moves can improve both technique and safety, yet outside the laboratory technique is still assessed by eye. We present a pipeline that detects named golf swing faults from ordinary video, without markers or calibration. Instead of learning end to end, it tracks body pose and the clubhead in a body-centered frame, and classifies the biomechanical quantities a coach would measure, such as the height of the clubhead above the swing plane, with shallow boosted trees. We demonstrate the approach on two common faults, coming over-the-top (OTT) and early-extension (EE). On 160 swings and 143 swings from public sources it detects OTT and EE with a cross-validated ROC-AUC of 0.975 and 0.911 respectively. Because every feature is a biomechanical quantity a coach already uses, measured at a known phase of the downswing, each decision can be traced to the signals and the moments of the swing that drove it. The signals the model relies on most are the same ones a coach checks when diagnosing these faults. The pipeline processes phone video on a consumer laptop in seconds, so it could feasibly be developed into an app for golfers of all abilities and extended to other sports in which technique is judged against a biomechanical reference.

This leaves the challenge of multi-task graph property prediction in TGs largely open. This design enables joint learning of local and global information under a multi-task objective. In multi-task classification, Hydra achieves an 8.9% relative gain in AUC over the strongest competitor. In multi-task regression, Hydra achieves competitive results in all three tasks, while obtaining the best results in two tasks with a 8.2% relative gain in MAE compared to the strongest baseline. Moreover, Hydra delivers these gains with a 22% reduction in training time compared to temporal transfer models. These results provide the first systematic evidence that multi-task transferable learning on temporal graphs is effective. Abstract: Low-label transfer is often decided by a practical question: after a representation looks useful, how many labels are still needed to learn the downstream boundary? Transferability and probe-quality scores characterize global alignment, likelihood, margins, and calibration. Boundary-near target mass complements these diagnostics by measuring the ambiguous regions where scarce labels are most costly. We propose effective boundary complexity, a label-budget diagnostic for frozen representations and linear probes. It measures mass in a thin strip around the learned boundary and normalizes it by class balance, so dense ambiguous regions and predicted-class imbalance are both counted. We prove that, under a smooth representation map, density change and tangential surface change cancel at first order, leaving boundary-normal stretch or compression as the governing factor. This yields a cached-feature estimator that fits a probe on one split and counts independent held-out features near its boundary. Experiments support the diagnostic at three levels. Controlled transformations match the predicted law across 74 maps with 0.38% median relative error. On real frozen features, normal-direction changes move both measured boundary mass and label demand, while tangent controls preserve the first-order coefficient. A differentiable surrogate reduces measured complexity by 16-36% at matched top-1 utility. Finally, CIFAR-100 and Tiny-ImageNet experiments support boundary mass as a second-stage diagnostic for near-tied representations. Five-way experiments show strong OvR-interface backbone-ranking agreement (Spearman 0.872), while acquisition experiments show high correlations between initial boundary mass and realized label budgets across three sampling policies (0.779-0.830). Abstract: AdamW is ubiquitous in deep learning, yet its behavior remains poorly understood. We analyze its dynamics through the lens of dynamical systems and show that AdamW admits an implicit fixed-point objective: its fixed points coincide with the stationary points of a constrained and regularized optimization problem. However, not all of these fixed points are stable under AdamW's dynamics, and stability depends sensitively on curvature, weight decay, and momentum parameters. Even in one-dimensional settings, equilibria may be unstable, and trajectories can exhibit persistent oscillations rather than convergence to them. We further extend the analysis to higher dimensions, deriving sufficient conditions for local stability of the continuous-time dynamics, and propose a convergent modification that preserves AdamW's fixed points. These results clarify what optimization problem AdamW is associated with, when its convergence can be expected, and how its dynamics could inspire more reliable optimizers.

The goal of this work-in-progress paper is to stimulate conversation and forge new collaborations regarding the security of compositional learning.

Abstract: Which representation should be probed in a transformer-based foundation model? Recent work shows that the standard practice of probing the final layer is often suboptimal, as intermediate layers can provide better features. Yet, representations are still largely studied along depth alone, treating each transformer block as an atomic unit. In this work, we analyze transformer representations layer by layer, module by module. Through more than 144,000 linear probing evaluations of vision foundation models (VFMs) and large language models (LLMs), we show that layer and module choices should be considered jointly: the best layer-module pair yields substantially larger gains over the final-layer residual stream than the best layer with the residual stream fixed. Moreover, module performance can vary substantially across layers, showing that useful representations cannot be characterized by depth alone. Building on these findings, we evaluate simple fixed probing strategies: the final-layer feedforward activation (Act@100) for vision and the second normalization layer at 60% depth (LN2@60) for language. These strategies improve over the probing baseline in a majority of evaluations and recover, on average, 42% and 86% of the gains from probing the best layer-module pair for vision and language, respectively. For VFMs, these gains increase with the transfer difficulty between pretraining and downstream data.

Abstract: LLM agents can use feedback from repeated attempts to improve their decisions without updating model parameters. However, positive action credits can continue to favor actions that repeatedly achieve the same score, even when higher scores are achievable. Under a fixed interaction budget, trying new actions in the final attempts can also lower performance. We introduce EARN, a method that uses past experience to guide further progress and retain earlier performance through conditional reuse of effective action sequences. EARN reduces action credits for reaching scores repeatedly attained in earlier episodes and assigns larger credits to action segments that reach higher scores. These credits adjust the probabilities of LLM-proposed actions. During the final episodes, EARN directly replays stored actions when the current context matches the recorded context and the actions remain available, bypassing LLM candidate generation. We evaluate performance at the end of the interaction budget using the average score over the final four episodes. Across Jericho, ScienceWorld, and Crafter, EARN achieves the best score on this metric in 31 of 33 task--budget settings among the evaluated methods, including ties. Across the evaluated Jericho budgets, replay bypasses 78.9--86.3\% of candidate-generation calls in the final four episodes, averaged across games.

Using a real electron energy-loss spectroscopy dataset, we run two complementary campaigns. In the first, the controller chooses between imaging and spectroscopy, image size, and scan speed. In the second, the instrument drifts and the controller also decides when to recalibrate. The campaigns show that response discovery and structure-property learning favor different surrogate models and acquisition strategies. They also show that simple acquisition rules form strong, practical baselines. Independent error analysis reveals where model uncertainty is and is not a reliable learning signal. In the drift campaign, explicit registration greatly improves positional accuracy, and the framework measures what this costs in spectroscopy throughput. Together, these results provide a reproducible way to design, compare, and audit autonomous experiments, and they define what microscopes must provide for deployment: independently executable actions, characterized scan and detector responses, timestamped registration telemetry, and complete action records.

</details>

Abstract: This study investigates the potential of TimeGPT, a pretrained foundation model for timeseries, for forecasting daily emergency call volumes. Real-world emergency call data, comprising 2,101 daily observations, are used to evaluate both univariate and multivariate forecasting settings under one-step-ahead and seven-day multi-step-ahead scenarios. The multivariate forecasting incorporates temporal, meteorological, demographic, and socioeconomic variables. A chronological training, validation, and test split is adopted to prevent information leakage, while hyperparameters are selected using the validation set through grid search and early stopping when applicable. Forecasting performance is evaluated using multiple error metrics. Its forecasting performance is compared with several established forecasting approaches, including Seasonal Autoregressive Integrated Moving Average with Exogenous Regressors (SARIMAX), Poisson regression (PR), Negative Binomial regression (NBR), Extreme Gradient Boosting (XGBoost), Multilayer Perceptron (MLP), and Long Short-Term Memory (LSTM). The results show that SARIMAX achieves the best overall performance in the univariate setting. In the multivariate setting, NBR and PR provide best performance. These findings indicate that the large-scale pretraining of TimeGPT do not necessarily translate into superior forecasting accuracy for emergency call volumes. Frugal methods trained solely on the available data remain better for this application.

Abstract: Ship design requires coordinating coupled subsystems to satisfy engineering constraints and balance competing performance and cost objectives. Generative artificial intelligence has supported component design, but integrated ship generation must also account for interactions among hull geometry, structure, propulsion, and operation. This paper presents GenISys, a framework for generating integrated systems, demonstrated through early-stage bulk-carrier design. Its dataset contains 217,999 proposed designs, including 16,247 that satisfy the formulated engineering constraints. The proposed simultaneous conditional denoising diffusion probabilistic model (SCDDPM) generates subsystems together, allowing each subsystem model to respond to changes elsewhere during generation. Experiments compare this approach with monolithic whole-ship generation and sequential subsystem generation across five independent training runs per architecture. In a study targeting lower cost per tonne of cargo transported, designs were generated for a required voyage range of 5,000 nautical miles. SCDDPM produced designs satisfying the formulated engineering constraints and meeting a common low-cost target in 55.70% of generation requests, compared with 51.10% for the monolithic model and 47.54% for the sequential model. These correspond to 9.0% and 17.2% more qualifying designs, respectively, while maintaining similar estimated transport costs. These results show how generative artificial intelligence can coordinate subsystems to generate feasible, high-performing integrated systems.

Abstract: We study the numerical approximation of Schrödinger Bridges in the setting where samples from the target distribution are unavailable and only an unnormalized target density is given. In this regime, the widely used iterative proportional fitting (IPF) procedure (and its variants) often exhibits severe numerical instabilities, stemming from ill-conditioned underlying control problems and from the fact that the optimal solution may lie far from the initialization. To address these issues, we propose a trust region variant of IPF that constrains successive updates to remain at a fixed Kullback-Leibler divergence from the current iterate. This restriction significantly improves numerical stability. Moreover, we show that the resulting method admits an interpretation as a mirror descent scheme with an adaptively chosen step size. We demonstrate the effectiveness of our approach on several challenging sampling tasks.

Abstract: We study reconstruction attacks, which recover training data from a learned model. In our adversarial model, the adversary designs a hard instance, drawn from a parameterized family of data distributions, and is given the parameter used to generate it. Our main contribution is a general reduction showing that, in this model, membership inference (determining whether a given datapoint was used for training) implies reconstruction: for a training set of size $n$, a membership test with recall at least $q(n)$ and false-positive rate $O(δ^2/n^2)$ yields a reconstruction algorithm that, with probability $1 - O(δ)$, outputs only training points, and recovers $Ω(n \cdot q(n))$ of them in expectation. This reduction applies to any learning algorithm. For stochastic convex optimization (SCO), we make the reduction computationally efficient over exponentially large domains via structured distributions whose support is partitioned into polynomially many categories of exponentially many elements each, so that reconstruction iterates over categories rather than the full support. Combining this with an existing membership inference result for SCO, we prove that any learning algorithm achieving low excess risk over all distributions admits a polynomial-time reconstruction attack recovering $Ω(n)$ training points, even when the underlying distribution has exponentially large support.

</details>

Abstract: We study Minty set inclusion on a compact convex set $\mathcal{X} \subseteq \mathbb{R}^d$ with a set-valued operator $A : \mathcal{X} \rightrightarrows \mathbb{R}^d$. Under the Minty promise, the goal is to compute $x \in \mathcal{X}$ and $u \in A(x)$ such that $\sup_{y \in \mathcal{X}} \langle u, x - y \rangle \le \varepsilon$. Our approach is geometric and we work in the fixed-scale constrained-resolvent oracle model, where a query at anchor $a$ is assumed to return a resolvent point $x^+$ together with the Yosida vector $g_η(a) = (a - x^+)/η$. We prove that each oracle response yields a sharp dichotomy: either the returned point provides an $\varepsilon$-SVI certificate, or the Yosida vector defines a strict separating normal for the Minty set. Thus, a single black-box resolvent query supplies either a certified candidate solution or a valid geometric cut. Combining this separation principle with the ellipsoid method, we obtain RESOLVENT-ELLIPSOID, an algorithm that computes an $\varepsilon$-SVI in $\mathcal{O}\!\left(\operatorname{poly}(d, \log(1/\varepsilon))\right)$ black-box oracle calls to the fixed-scale constrained resolvent. For monotone inclusions, our algorithm gives, to our knowledge, the first $\mathcal{O}\!\left(\mathrm{poly}(d, \log(1/\varepsilon))\right)$ black-box guarantee in the fixed-scale resolvent model.

</details>

Abstract: Energy claims for efficient neural networks are often inferred from operation counts. We report direct GPU energy measurements on two workloads that batch very differently, recurrent policies in GPU-vectorised multi-agent reinforcement learning (MARL) and mixture-of-experts (MoE) language models, and ask one question of both: can the rows of a batch be made to share computation, across the batch and over time, at a measured saving? On recurrent policies the answer so far is no. Shared activity patterns exist but rarely last one step, masking a frozen policy fails, and trained-in masks succeed only because a fixed mask is equivalent to a narrow GRU, at 11x the energy of a small dense policy in our implementation. Test-time steering adds nothing beyond re-picking on either workload. On MoE models the answer is yes, and granularity decides how much: on Qwen3-30B-A3B, batch-aware routing reads 25-31% fewer expert weights within KL 0.05, with benchmark changes within noise at 500 items, and uses 12-18% less energy per token on a fused forward. We also introduce persistence-aware OEA, a one-parameter rule that keeps the batch-aware cut with about 40% fewer new experts per step than native routing at batch 32-64, though its energy has not yet been measured. These are pilot measurements on one GPU type, and we state their limits.

Abstract: Generating counterfactual (CF) explanations for time series is challenging due to complex temporal dependencies and the computational inefficiency of traditional instance-specific optimization. We propose a policy learning framework for counterfactual explanation in time series, trained as a one-step contextual-bandit policy-learning problem. By training an actor-critic agent in the latent space of a pre-trained autoencoder, our method learns a transferable policy that captures global structural regularities. Unlike existing methods that rely on iterative optimization for each new input, the learned policy enables direct generation of valid and plausible counterfactuals through a single forward pass, avoiding per-instance optimization at inference time. Experiments on 13 UCR benchmark datasets show that our framework achieves high validity and strong plausibility across multiple anomaly detection metrics. Overall, by decoupling counterfactual generation from per-instance optimization, the proposed method provides an efficient and scalable alternative to existing approaches for time series counterfactual explanations.

Abstract: We study off-policy evaluation (OPE) in contextual bandits with data collected from multiple logging policies. Inverse propensity scoring (IPS) is a standard approach to OPE and extends naturally to the multi-logger setting. However, as highlighted by Agarwal et al. [2017], neither balanced nor weighted IPS consistently outperforms the other in this setting. We resolve this dilemma by deriving an optimal IPS estimator with sample-dependent weights that minimize variance subject to unbiasedness. Using a variational calculus approach, we obtain closed-form optimal weights, yielding an estimator that is unbiased and achieves asymptotically optimal variance within a generalized weighted-IPS estimator class. Instead of a reward model, the estimator needs only a $K\times K$ matrix and a $K$-vector of moments, where $K$ is the number of logging policies, and we quantify how much efficiency this costs relative to the semiparametric oracle. Experiments on benchmark datasets show that our estimator generally improves on existing multi-logger IPS methods. We also extend our estimator to a doubly robust form by incorporating a conditional reward estimator. We compare the resulting DR extension with the semiparametrically efficient DR estimator of Kallus et al. [2021], both theoretically and empirically. We show that the two estimators achieve the same asymptotic variance when the conditional reward estimator is consistent, while our estimator can retain semiparametric efficiency under certain forms of reward misspecification. Empirically, the proposed DR estimator often achieves lower estimation error than the existing DR baseline.

Abstract: Unlike the brain, artificial neural networks (ANNs) suffer from "catastrophic forgetting": complete overwriting of solutions when re-training on new functions ("concept shift") or on a disjoint domain of the current function ("domain shift"). ANNs require each batch of data to be roughly representative of the complete distribution, a typically expensive and impractical requirement. In this paper, we introduce Functional Task Networks (FTNs), a method of continual learning and knowledge transfer modeled after several properties of the mammalian neocortex. FTN is formulated as a distributed, stochastic mixture of experts in which a sparse, binary routing mask is computed at inference time using a small support sample of input-output pairs. Per-batch reconfiguration of the router during training, together with mask-overlap-filtered replay, shapes a basis of overlapping, partially trained subnetworks. Unrelated tasks route through mostly separate assemblies, related tasks overlap on shared components. On block-sequentially trained continual learning and knowledge transfer tasks FTN retains solutions nearly as well as disjoint-mask isolation and attains the lowest pre-update head-start loss among matched-architecture comparators. The learned mask-overlap geometry tracks the known concept-sharing structure where available. We show experimentally that under correlated concept shift, support-set mask reconfiguration is necessary even for retention. Under domain shift, the distributed bank with replay is sufficient to handle retention without the configurer. Forward transfer, or head-start, and backward transfer, are also contributed by support-set mask configuration.

</details>

Abstract: Representations learned from electronic health record (EHR) data encode patient physiology together with the clinical process that decides what is measured, when, and for whom. We formalize the data-generating process with latent physiology, ordering intent, laboratory values that exist only when measured, a learned representation, and clinical environments, and analyze two strategies for undoing this entanglement. First, no division of the information carried by laboratory "value" and "ordering" channels is identified from a single dataset: when ordering depends on the analyte, its distribution among untested patients is not recoverable, and a sentinel code for the undefined recorded value makes it carry all the entropy of the ordering indicator, fixing partial information decomposition (PID) for every redundancy measure satisfying the Williams-Beer axioms and invariant under injective re-encoding. Second, cross-site invariance of representation-conditional outcomes is a falsifiable implication of biological invariance taken jointly with exclusion and representation-sufficiency conditions, but not an identification strategy: we construct observationally equivalent models that disagree about biological invariance, whether the invariance test fails or passes. For binary physiology this happens exactly when case mix differs, the representation is lossy, and physiology affects the outcome. A worked example yields a 9.6-percentage-point cross-site gap under an unchanged biological mechanism. Tests performed for every patient remove the ordering gate, and measured tests can restore identification: biological invariance is identified when a test exactly summarizes outcome-relevant physiology, or, for binary physiology, when two tests with independent errors are available at sites with different case mix. Cross-site analyses should be reported as tests of a stated joint hypothesis.

Abstract: Population game dynamics describe how aggregate behavior evolves in response to payoff signals. In many applications, the payoff function may change, and the goal is to predict the population trajectory induced by a new payoff design before trajectory data under that design are available. We study this problem as $\textbf{payoff-aware prediction of population game dynamics}$. Standard dynamics learning methods fit the motion observed under training payoffs, but do not separate the payoff-dependent incentive from the payoff-independent response structure needed for prediction under new payoffs. Building on the existing framework of Riemannian game dynamics, we propose PA-RmD, a payoff-aware learning method that keeps payoff information explicit and learns the payoff-independent response structure from data. Theoretically, we show that the learned model preserves fixed-payoff Nash equilibria and derive finite-time one-step prediction bounds under shared-response and payoff-coverage conditions. Empirically, we evaluate PA-RmD on synthetic zero-sum and potential games, as well as a YouTube Trending multi-country dataset, showing that our method performs well under changing payoff designs.

Abstract: Preference optimization has emerged as a promising alternative to reward-based training for neural combinatorial optimization. However, existing methods typically rely on binary pairwise labels, which discard the magnitude of solution-quality differences and provide supervision only at the complete-solution level. We propose Advantage-aware Preference Optimization for Two-level Constructive Search (APO2CS), a preference-based training objective for constructive neural solvers that integrates both macro-level and micro-level supervision. At the macro level, APO2CS enriches pairwise preferences with an advantage term derived from objective-value differences. At the micro level, it compares partial trajectories, thereby providing denser supervision throughout the sequential construction process. The resulting objective can be used either to train a policy from scratch or to fine-tune a pretrained one. Experimental results show that APO2CS improves the expressiveness and search capability of preference learning for combinatorial optimization, while demonstrating strong effectiveness and adaptability across multiple combinatorial optimization tasks.

Abstract: We study online monotone submodular maximization under a cardinality constraint with bandit feedback. On each round, the learner selects at most $k$ elements from a ground set of size $n$ and observes only the value of the selected set. Abstract: Nonseparable costs and coupled constraints in distributed online convex optimization often arise when system performance and operational constraints depend on the joint configuration of all agents and cannot be decomposed into individual contributions. Such nonseparability leads to fully distributed decision-making with challenging partial local information, where each agent evaluates its local primal-dual update using estimated global information via a local belief of the joint action and the shared Lagrange multiplier to be exchanged with neighbors. While the belief consensus established by prior works ensures no-regret learning, exchanging beliefs comprising full-dimensional primal and dual states at every round imposes a communication overhead that scales with both the joint decision dimension and the network size. Existing event-triggered schemes typically activate communication according to state variation or consensus error. Under nonseparable coupling, however, errors of equal magnitude can produce markedly different perturbations after passing through the KKT operator. We therefore propose a randomized KKT-perturbation-aware communication mechanism, where the triggering of each agent's primal and dual communication channels depends on their impact on the KKT operator. The proposed mechanism transfers the adaptive-sampling principle of coordinate descent from allocating computation across decision blocks to allocating communication across information channels: channels with larger estimated perturbations receive greater priority for refresh. Under standard regularity assumptions, the resulting algorithm achieves $O({T}^{2/3})$ expected static regret and $O({T}^{2/3})$ expected cumulative constraint violation. Numerical experiments demonstrate improved learning and constraint-enforcement performance per transmitted bit over belief consensus baselines.

</details>

Abstract: Out-of-distribution (OOD) detection is essential for deploying machine learning models in high stakes scientific applications, yet methods that flag OOD inputs without retraining the model have been validated almost exclusively on image classification. We introduce MOOD (Materials Out-of-Distribution Detection), a benchmark for evaluating whether these post-hoc OOD detectors transfer to graph neural networks (GNNs) on materials property prediction. MOOD evaluates 15 post-hoc detectors and 2 supervised diagnostic probes across 4 GNN architectures, 5 MatBench tasks, and 15 physically motivated compositional and structural shifts, yielding 264 evaluation settings and over 13,000 AUROC measurements across three encoder training seeds. Our evaluation reveals three key findings. First, current post-hoc detectors perform near chance; all 15 detectors achieve an AUROC around 0.5. Second, detector failures decompose into two regimes: method bottlenecks and encoder bottlenecks. Method bottlenecks arise when supervised probes can separate in-distribution (ID) from OOD samples but unsupervised detectors fail, meaning that the embeddings contain OOD signal that current detectors do not extract. Encoder bottlenecks arise when even supervised probes fail to separate ID from OOD samples, indicating that the relevant shift is not accessible in the frozen GNN representation. Third, when OOD prediction error exceeds ID error, the AUROC of distance-based and density-based detectors correlates with the size of that gap. Collectively, these findings indicate that reliable OOD detection in scientific domains requires representation learning strategies that explicitly preserve OOD relevant signal, particularly for tasks where current GNN architectures encode no extractable signal at all.

</details>

Abstract: Fleets of driving vision-language-action (VLA) models are developed one domain at a time: each operator finetunes a shared base model on private data, producing specialized experts that are costly to consolidate into a single model. Weight merging loses accuracy as heterogeneous domains accumulate, and dense mixture-of-experts routing is prohibitively expensive to combine them. We present DeltaForge, to our knowledge the first post-hoc mixture-of-experts VLA for driving, which consolidates N independently finetuned per-domain models into a single multi-domain model from their weights and a small proxy set alone. When different operators finetune the same base model, their weight updates end up sharing a lot of structure. We exploit this by decomposing all the updates together using singular value decomposition (SVD), splitting each one into a shared part (the same across all domains) and a tiny domain-specific part. The router then only needs to work with those tiny domain-specific pieces, not the full expert weights, which is what keeps the approach efficient. Then two cooperating clip-level blind routers, one over the VLM reasoning experts and one over the flow-matching dynamics experts, are trained from soft labels on the proxy dataset. Across six diverse domains spanning urban, off-road, and highway driving on cars, trucks, and all-terrain vehicles (ATVs), DeltaForge approaches the accuracy of the per-domain experts and surpasses every merging, routing, centralized, and federated baseline at close to single-model parameter and compute cost. Adding a new domain requires only projecting its weight update onto the shared basis with no end-to-end retraining needed. The resulting model also generalizes to domain combinations it was never explicitly trained on.

Abstract: Implicit in much research in deep learning is the assumption that if a network achieves low loss, then it must have learned a good representation of its training data. Recent work by Kumar et al. (2025, arXiv:2505.11581) questions this assumption, arguing that networks trained with conventional gradient descent achieve low loss despite learning fractured entangled representations (FER), leaving related concepts disconnected and unrelated concepts entangled inappropriately. Kumar et al. further show that dramatically better representations are possible: a network evolved through an open-ended, human-in-the-loop process learned unified factored representations (UFR) that capture the regularities of the domain far better than a counterpart with nearly identical outputs trained with conventional gradient descent. Inspired by UFR, this paper introduces unified factored organizers (UFOs), networks trained to model regularities in the representations of other networks. A network can then be trained both to achieve low loss on its task objective and to align its representations with the model learned by the UFO. Networks trained with UFOs are shown to learn qualitatively superior representations in an image-regression domain where representations are easily interpretable. To provide quantitative evidence for the impact of UFOs on representation learning, a transformer is trained with and without UFO regularization to grok a modular arithmetic problem set. When combined with weight decay, UFOs can reduce the number of transformer training steps needed to grok by more than 10x relative to weight decay alone. Without weight decay, UFOs can reduce the number of transformer training steps needed to grok by more than 100x relative to an unregularized baseline. If UFOs are real, they suggest a new paradigm where loss functions themselves are trained to learn an effective model of good representation.

</details>

Abstract: Sensory delays create temporal misalignments between the state of the environment and the observations available to an agent, turning Markovian control problems into partially observable ones. In this work, we inquire whether such misalignments can be mitigated during training by using hindsight to recover aligned state-action transitions. We introduce Hindsight Critics, an asymmetric extension of the Soft Actor-Critic for reinforcement learning under partial observability. The critics are trained on hindsight-aligned transitions whereas the actor learns from the resulting privileged value estimates despite only having access to delayed observations. Comparisons against delay-aware and unaware baselines on the DelayBench suite show that Hindsight Critics can partially compensate for observation delays without requiring modifications to the agent's observations during deployment.

Abstract: We study the minimax regret of full-information online decision making in extensive-form games. For prediction with expert advice, the optimal regret is $Θ(\sqrt{T\log K})$, where $K$ is the number of experts, or equivalently, the number of normal-form actions. For treeplex strategy spaces, existing efficient algorithms, including KOMWU and online mirror descent with the weight-one dilated entropy regularizer, achieve regret $\mathcal{O}(\sqrt{T\log|\mathcal{V}|})$, where $\mathcal{V}$ is the set of reduced normal-form strategies. We prove that this logarithmic dependence is minimax optimal in the worst-case full-information reward model. The main difficulty is that pure plans in a treeplex are not independent experts: their rewards are correlated through the recursive structure of decisions and observations. To capture this correlation, we introduce tropical Gaussian formulae, recursive Gaussian expressions built from maxima and normalized sums which preserve the variance. The index count of such a formula matches the number of reduced normal-form continuation plans in the corresponding subtree. Our main analytic result shows that every log-balanced tropical Gaussian formula with index count $N$ has Gaussian width $Ω(\sqrt{\log N})$. While a direct approach struggles to recover this lower bound, our proof uses a spherical entropy profile to measure scale-dependent separation among the Gaussian directions, and then applies Turán's theorem and Sudakov minoration to obtain the width lower bound. Applying this result to treeplexes gives an oblivious hard distribution with valid transition outcome and Rademacher terminal rewards, establishing a regret lower bound of $Ω(\sqrt{T\log|\mathcal{V}|})$. This matches known upper bounds up to universal constants and shows that the DilEnt/KOMWU dependence on the reduced normal-form complexity is unavoidable.

</details>

Abstract: Traffic state estimation (TSE) reconstructs traffic density at unmonitored locations from a sparse set of sensors. Data-driven models work well when sensors are dense but degrade sharply as coverage thins. We show that imposing the Lighthill-Whitham-Richards conservation law as a physics-informed prior makes TSE more data-efficient. On four real corridors (NGSIM, I-80, PeMS04, PeMS08), physics-informed estimators generalise to held-out sensors where data-driven models, including classical and deep kriging, overfit the observed ones. On the two shock-rich corridors the effect is a clear crossover: as more sensors are held out, data-driven models collapse while the physics-informed estimator degrades gracefully, leading the no-physics network by about 26-38% at 85-90% held out and the strongest classical kriging by about 10-21% at 90%. A matched smoothness prior does not reproduce the gain, and an extended Kalman filter on the same LWR model is outperformed on the shock corridors, so the advantage comes from how the conservation law is imposed. Finally, one loss-conditional PINN removes the per-corridor loss-weight search, matching a 20-point search at about 5x lower cost.

Abstract: One-step stochastic world models avoid the mode averaging of deterministic feature predictors without the tens of network evaluations per step that diffusion and flow matching require. The simplest such objective is nearest-of-m matching (conditional implicit maximum likelihood estimation, IMLE): draw m candidate futures and pull only the one closest to the observed outcome. Recent work conjectures that a small m suffices for feature-space world models and that a strictly proper energy score should replace the hard minimum. We test both claims with one-step world models on frozen DINOv2/v3 patch tokens, using benchmarks whose futures can be enumerated. Under mode-like uncertainty, nearest-of-m with m=16 covers the enumerated futures better than 50-step flow matching (at about 1/30 of its latency), one-step flow generators and an energy-score generator at matched parameter counts, and its rollouts stay on the token manifold where flow rollouts drift. One quantity governs the choice of m: the nearest-of-m objective is the expected distortion of a random codebook of m draws from the generator. This view yields three predictions, recorded before the corresponding runs: (i) memorization of the realized outcome in contexts that never repeat, whose strength is governed by the codebook's miss distortion I(m) rather than its miss probability, and which an energy-score generator does not escape by raising m; (ii) a flattening of mode probabilities with m, matching a known optimum within 0.01; and (iii) under-dispersion on continuous high-dimensional outcomes, which bounds the method on real robot video. Per-step matching on self-rollouts cuts long-horizon error by a third on a stochastic Push-T variant; an ablation separates its manifold and coverage benefits. Planning success does not improve despite accurate risk estimates, a negative result we report with its diagnosis.

This pattern is not universal. On DD, planted-signal controls show that the reader recovers an injected final-answer signal at one tenth of full dose. Answer-candidate decodability is therefore target-relative and protocol-dependent: a strong answer readout does not by itself establish when a model commits, and a probe that fails to train is not evidence that the information is absent. ECDP is a practical control for distinguishing imminent text emission from evidence of a settled decision.

Abstract: The Adam optimizer is widely used to train neural networks with highly nonlinear and nonconvex objective functions. Remarkably, Adam often drives the empirical training loss nearly to zero. This phenomenon has been partially explained by neural tangent kernel (NTK) analyses of gradient descent (GD) and stochastic gradient descent (SGD). However, analyzing Adam presents additional challenges. Its first-moment momentum couples stochastic gradients across iterations, while its adaptive second-moment preconditioner depends on the same stochastic gradient used in the update. In this paper, we establish linear convergence guarantees for Adam in training sufficiently wide two-layer neural networks with either full-batch or minibatch gradients. For full-batch Adam, we construct a Lyapunov function to control the effect of historical momentum. For minibatch Adam, our analysis builds on an exact virtual-iterate representation, a conditional analysis of the dependence between stochastic gradients and adaptive preconditioners, and uniform concentration bounds for the second-moment estimates. We prove geometric decay of the empirical loss for full-batch Adam and geometric decay in restricted expectation on a high-probability event for minibatch Adam. Beyond regression, we extend our convergence analysis to physics-informed neural networks (PINNs) for solving the Poisson equation, where differential operators and separate sampling of interior and boundary points introduce additional challenges. We establish analogous global convergence guarantees for both deterministic and stochastic Adam under explicit conditions on network width and Adam's parameters, characterizing the effects of minibatch size on convergence. These results help bridge the gap between Adam's empirical success and its theoretical understanding, providing sharper convergence guarantees for PINNs than existing analyses.

</details>

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) increasingly plan and run experiments in automated laboratories. These experiments keep running while the model reasons and run on schedules with hard deadlines. If an LLM-generated answer arrives late, the system executes a safe fallback action instead, such as rejecting a sample. This creates a systems problem that standard serving metrics do not capture. An answer that matches the fallback adds no value, and answers that differ from the fallback may require more reasoning time. Reporting answer quality and latency separately can therefore overstate the value of reasoning. We test this on synthetic sample decisions, where the best choice is known exactly and a runtime enforces the deadline. We compare one LLM's fast mode, which answers directly, with its thinking mode, which reasons first. In a prespecified replay of 1,200 new decisions, thinking answers that missed a deadline were worth about five times as much as on-time ones. Multiplying the on-time rate by the mean answer value predicted about twice the value that thinking delivered. This prediction overstated thinking's advantage over fast mode by 0.0241 per decision, and by 0.0191 in a repeat on 300 new decisions. Both 95% confidence intervals excluded zero. In a single-GPU Qwen3-8B experiment using simultaneous request bursts, raising the number of requests from 8 to 56 reduced the share of thinking answers that met a 45 s deadline from 79.2% to 1.4%, although total output tripled. Evaluations of LLM agents for laboratories should therefore measure each answer's value and arrival time together and score the action that actually runs.

</details>

Abstract: Feature subsampling is used to accelerate gradient-boosted trees, yet standard approaches fix the feature count in advance and largely ignore the split-gain evidence accumulated during training. We introduce CRAFT-GBM (Confidence-Ranked Adaptive Feature Tracking), a native LightGBM extension that uses this evidence to adapt which---and, by default, how many---features are searched each boosting round. CRAFT-GBM transforms root-split gains into bounded observations and constructs joint time-uniform bounds valid under adaptive, non-stationary training histories. In its default endogenous mode, after initialization, CRAFT-GBM retains exactly the features that cannot yet be ruled out as best, letting the selected feature-set size $K_t$ emerge from training history rather than a pre-specified fraction. With probability at least $1-δ$, every feature with the largest expected gain is retained jointly across all rounds after initialization. When a feature fraction $f$ is specified instead, CRAFT-GBM ranks features by their interval point estimates and provides an approximate top-$K$ guarantee for feature budget $K_f$. We implement CRAFT-GBM within LightGBM while preserving its native feature-bundling representation, and evaluate it across 14 public and synthetic dataset families and four noise-augmented settings. In the fully endogenous setting, CRAFT-GBM reduces per-fit training time by 39.74\% with 1.04\% lower test loss in the geometric mean across all 18 RQ1 experiments. Under pre-specified feature fraction $f$, Exogenous CRAFT-GBM also yields aggregate training-time reductions up to 13.52 percentage points larger than random feature subsampling, with no increase in aggregate test loss relative to full-feature LightGBM. Accumulated training evidence can thus support adaptive feature search with statistical guarantees while retaining the practical advantages of native gradient boosting.

</details>

Abstract: EEG-based fatigue detection requires modeling spectral information across bands and its temporal variation. Recent methods combine multi-band modeling with adaptive temporal processing, yet how spectral structure across bands should influence the contribution of each temporal interval remains insufficiently explored. We propose LBSNet, a lightweight network that uses prototype-based brain-state modeling to link local spectral structure with temporal aggregation. LBSNet extracts compact multi-band features through band-specific temporal convolution, shared spatial projections, and cross-band recalibration. Within each window, relative spectral activity is encoded as a normalized descriptor and softly matched to learnable spectral prototypes. Prototype assignments and learned prototype importance jointly determine temporal weights for power aggregation, allowing the spectral pattern of each window to guide its contribution to the prediction. Subject-independent experiments on SEED-VIG and SADT show that LBSNet outperforms seven baselines while maintaining low computational cost. Ablation and visualization analyses support prototype-guided temporal weighting, while the learned prototypes reveal a gradual redistribution of relative cross-band spectral states with increasing fatigue. The code is publicly available at https://github.com/zy0929/LBSNet.
