Abstract: World models are widely considered to be a potential key component of model-based reinforcement learning and robot planning, but typically require task-specific training on substantial interaction data. We investigate whether a pretrained tabular foundation model (TabFM) can serve as a training-free dynamics model for world modeling by framing next-state prediction as in-context regression over transition pairs, and where such a model helps and where it breaks. Across synthetic dynamical systems and robot locomotion benchmarks, TabFM is more accurate than LSTM and MLP models trained from scratch on the same limited transitions in almost all settings, and among regressors fitted to the same data it is the most accurate on locomotion, while a Gaussian process is more accurate on smooth low-dimensional systems. However, models trained on the full dataset remain substantially better on most systems. When rolled out autoregressively over multiple steps, TabFM's accuracy depends strongly on how much context it is given. With a short context, its rollouts quickly degrade to the level of a trivial baseline, whereas with a long context they improve substantially and, on one locomotion task, exceed a trained LSTM. Inference is also about three orders of magnitude slower than an LSTM. Overall, in their current state, we find that training-free priors are useful as predictors when data are scarce and enough context is available, but they are not yet a substitute for trained world models in planning.

Existing constrained hybrid-action methods typically optimize factorized policies directly in parameter space, whereas Constrained Variational Policy Optimization (CVPO) separates nonparametric improvement from parametric projection in its original continuous-action formulation. For approximate updates, we establish the existence of a dual minimizer under continuity on a compact domain and use residual-based admissibility to determine whether to project the target. Abstract: Transformers stop learning graph search as graphs grow, and learn the card game SET only after many distinct questions, although attention can represent both. We read these failures, and the uneven record of higher-order attention, from what a layer sums over: each layer we study computes the partition function of a family of witnesses and reads it at a temperature. Two laws follow. The depth law is a known result of logic, which we state for these layers: L layers over two-step walks reach exactly 2^L - 1 steps, just as a logical statement with L nested clauses of the form "there is a vertex such that" sees exactly that far. We build a construction that reaches this far, checked in Lean, and, when vertices carry no names, no such network reaches further. The size law is the cost of reading at a temperature, which logic does not have: existence is read correctly at every size, in the worst case, if and only if inverse temperature times margin is at least the logarithm of the number of candidates. Layers built on these laws, and on two principles that we test, learn where Transformers fail. One propagator layer trained on 49-vertex graphs answers every question at every search depth on 250-vertex graphs. Given the modulus 3, exponential Newton computes the softmax of 2-simplicial attention over pairs exactly in 3^d n operations for d clocks; with six clocks it learns SET from as few deals as 2-simplicial attention and transfers from 8- to 20-card tables. On CLRS-30 without hints, a 186,000-parameter block of OR-join and power-mean channels matches (within half a point) or beats a model of at least 73 million parameters on 9 of 12 graph tasks of the official test set, and on 8 of 12 with freshly drawn test graphs. The same account explains where pair layers did not help and where parallel layers must loop.

</details>

Abstract: Origin-destination (OD) demand plays an important role in transportation system design and management applications. Conventional OD estimation methods typically judge estimation quality based on how estimated demand aligns with observed traffic data and historical demand patterns. We argue that data alignment alone is insufficient; how errors in OD estimates influence downstream decision quality should also be considered. This paper develops a decision aware OD demand estimation framework that leverages structurally constrained traffic data to characterize an admissible demand region, within which we identify OD estimates that would lead to least regret in the downstream decision. Therefore, our OD estimates are both data-consistent and decision-relevant. Methodologically, we approximate the admissible demand region induced by physical network constraints through a geometric representation, which regularizes the search over feasible OD estimates. Within this regularized region, we use a convex surrogate of downstream regret to obtain a tractable decision aware optimization problem. Numerical experiments show that the proposed framework consistently reduces decision regret relative to conventional estimation benchmarks, demonstrating the value of aligning OD demand estimation with downstream decision quality.

Abstract: Unplanned 30-day intensive care unit (ICU) readmission is a critical benchmark for evaluating healthcare quality. Most predictive models assume readmission risk depends solely on a patient's individual pathophysiological state, ignoring hospital operational context and resource capacity. In practice, discharge decisions are dynamic trade-offs between patient stability and bed turnover pressures. To address this gap, we develop an Operation-aware Multimodal Clinical Decision Support System (CDSS) for ICU discharge risk assessment. The system combines multi-agent collaborative extraction with self-evolving retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) to eliminate template noise from progress notes, while constructing departmental workload indicators alongside admission flow features, continuous vital signs, and sparse medical codes (ICD/DRG). Operational indicators supply independent risk signals, laying an engineering foundation for high-utility clinical decision support tools.

Abstract: Synthetic data can scale visual pretraining, but current selection criteria only partially explain which datasets train strong representations. We present ShiftCache, an exact reuse system that encodes each shifted reference once and shares it among all variants scored in the same window with the same shift. ShiftCache copies every encoder output a variant can reuse and recomputes the rest in batched tiles. On held-out AlphaGenome windows shared by many indels, in strict fp32 on one A6000, ShiftCache is 3.5 to 6.5$\times$ faster than deduplicated dense execution and 1.6 to 2.7$\times$ faster than our adaptation of layer-by-layer reuse to indels. It is 3.5 to 4.1$\times$ faster than dense reimplementations of two published analyses with Borzoi and AlphaGenome, and 2.6$\times$ faster than dense execution with Enformer. Scoring each variant in its own centered 131 kb window makes ShiftCache only 1.36$\times$ faster than deduplicated dense execution, and highly diverse insertion lengths or many dispersed edits can make it slower than layer-by-layer reuse or dense execution. For encoders that meet our conditions, ShiftCache computes the same predictions as dense execution in exact arithmetic. On an A6000, every checked output of the default AlphaGenome and Borzoi runs matches dense execution within an absolute and relative tolerance of $10^{-5}$. With larger tile batches, with Enformer or on an H100, a small fraction of values exceeds this tolerance.

</details>

Abstract: Neural Combinatorial Optimization (NCO) is a promising paradigm for solving routing problems, yet its generalization across diverse data distributions remains a critical bottleneck. Existing methods typically tackle cross-distribution generalization through complex multi-distribution training or intricate meta-learning schemes, implicitly presuming that single-distribution training yields a weak zero-shot baseline. In this paper, we demonstrate that the potential of single-distribution training has been substantially underestimated and can be unlocked by a simple architectural inductive bias: Mix Normalization. To approach this, we first construct a comprehensive benchmark spanning three representative routing problems and 179 fine-grained datasets, capturing diverse shifts in node coordinates, customer demands, capacity and time windows. Building on this benchmark, we propose MixRoute, a neural framework that adaptively combines normalization statistics at different granularities to mitigate varying distribution shifts. Trained exclusively on uniform distributions, MixRoute generalizes effectively to a wide range of unseen distributions without any distribution-specific adaptation. Extensive experiments demonstrate that MixRoute consistently achieves state-of-the-art zero-shot generalization. Our work revisits normalization as a parsimonious alternative for zero-shot generalization against complex training-adaptation schemes.

</details>


### [333] [FireGate: Bounding Behaviour Change Before Training in Continual RL Fine-Tuning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.11979)
*Md Anwar Hossen,Ali Jannesari*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: 为防止多任务微调导致的遗忘，FireGate在每个任务更新前部署门控，使用未标注提示进行PAC校准，使得旧行为变化受门误报率限制。


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: 连续在新任务上微调已部署的语言模型会导致模型忘记先前任务中的技能和约束，且通常只有在训练后才通过基准检测到失效。需要一种在训练前就能保证旧行为不被破坏的方法。

Method: 在每个任务的LoRA更新前，将一个分类器（基于冻结的底层模型层）作为门控器，决定是否应用该更新。门控器在未标注的“不可改变”提示上进行校准，保证在任何提示分布上改变受保护输出的比例受门的误报率上限。

Result: 在RLLib工作流中的五个任务上，门控训练仅在8.3%样本上改变了旧聊天回应，而无门控训练则导致91.8%的改变；在七个模型上，门控效果与门在新任务提示上的触发率直接相关，符合PAC保证。

Conclusion: FireGate通过在任务更新前设置门控器，能够在不影响已学习行为的前提下有选择性地使用新模型更新，从而显著降低连续微调导致的遗忘。

Abstract: Fine-tuning a deployed language model on one task after another can change behaviour that should stay fixed, such as earlier skills, general chat or refusals. Today, such forgetting is found only after training, on the benchmarks someone chose to run. This work bounds, before training, how often its outputs change on chosen prompt distributions. FireGate places each task's update, such as a new LoRA, behind a gate, which is a classifier on the model's frozen lower layers that decides for each prompt whether the update is used. Where no gate fires, the model computes exactly what the old one computes. As a result, on any prompt distribution, every bounded behaviour score changes by at most the gates' firing rate there over a whole task stream. Calibrating each gate on unlabeled prompts of what must not change fixes that rate before training, with a PAC guarantee, whatever weights training produces. Forgetting is thus bounded by the gate's false-alarm rate, and its price is the gate's power: no update, gated or not, that changes as few protected outputs can change new-task outputs more often than the best test of that level tells new-task prompts from protected ones. On reinforcement-learning streams of up to five tasks, gated training keeps the earlier tasks and changes 3.3% of held-out chat responses, whereas ungated training changes 91.8%. Across seven models, the gain kept behind the gate follows its firing rate on new-task prompts, measured before training. The certificate covers the gated model as deployed, on the prompt distributions sampled for calibration, not against an adversary who searches for prompts that fire the gate.

</details>


### [334] [HyInv Technical Report](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.11997)
*Haowei Lin,Cai Zhou,Xiangxin Zhou*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: HyInv是一种大规模图网络 + 先进训练方法，能快速高效地为给定蛋白骨架生成多样化且折叠良好的氨基酸序列，比现有方法快50倍以上并取得更优性能。


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: 传统的反向折叠设计需大规模、多样化且折叠良好的序列候选；提高设计效率与多样性成为关键挑战。

Method: 使用1.96百万参数的后向框架图网络（编码-解码架构），通过Hyra 2.0预训练的随机顺序自回归序列建模与基于结构奖励的强化学习，推理时采用分组起草与重写采样器，并利用BF16数值实现加速。

Result: 在单块NVIDIA H20 GPU上，对402位残基骨架生成128个序列仅需50.6 ms（BF16），比参考采样器快约52倍；FP32路径更快8.7倍；在五个基准集和七个数据集-预测器组合中，HyInv在保持或提高序列多样性的同时，平均scTM均优于10个外部基线，并大幅超越ProteinMPNN和ProteinZero V2。

Conclusion: HyInv提供了在众多蛋白骨架类型上可实现快速且高质量、多样化的氨基酸序列设计，显著优于现有多种基线方法；

Abstract: Inverse folding designs amino-acid sequences for a given protein backbone, and screening needs many candidates that are both diverse and well folded. We present HyInv, a 1.96-million-parameter backbone-conditioned graph network with an encoder-decoder architecture, trained by Hyra 2.0, the research agent developed by our team: pretrained with random-order autoregressive sequence modeling, followed by structure-reward reinforcement learning. At inference, a grouped draft-and-rewrite sampler that uses separate draft and rewrite temperatures improves refolding TM-score at matched diversity. An optimized implementation with BF16 numerics designs 128 sequences for a 402-residue backbone in 50.6 ms on a single NVIDIA H20 GPU, about 52 times faster than the reference sampler, and an exact FP32 path that returns the reference sequences is 8.7 times faster. Across five benchmark datasets and seven dataset-predictor combinations, HyInv reaches a higher average scTM than all ten external baselines at comparable or higher sequence diversity, with large margins over ProteinMPNN and ProteinZero V2. The same default setting supports de novo backbones, pocket-constrained libraries, enzyme redesign with the catalytic site kept, and loop redesign. We have released our code and model at https://github.com/Tencent-Hunyuan/Hyra-results/tree/main/AI4Science/hyinv.

</details>


### [335] [Reward-Estimated Hypergradient for Bilevel Reinforcement Learning with Black-Box Follower](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.11998)
*Shigeki Kusaka,Mikoto Kudo,Takumi Tanabe,Akifumi Wachi,Youhei Akimoto*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: RE‑HG 在黑盒双层 RL 中，通过逆强化学习恢复奖励并聚合多样动态消除潜在偏差，截断协方差小特征值保证估计稳定，实验验证与白盒Oracle相当，并避免局部最优陷阱。


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: 实际场景中领导层无法获知跟随者奖励函数，只能观测轨迹，而传统HG方法假设奖励已知；需要在黑盒设置下实现有效的超梯度估计。

Method: 先使用逆强化学习从跟随者轨迹估计奖励，再将估计的奖励梯度在多样动态下聚合，并对特征协方差的极小特征值做截断以提升可识别子空间，获得稳定的超梯度。

Result: 实验显示RE‑HG梯度与白盒Oracle极为对齐，性能相近；截断技巧抑制超梯度发散，使RE‑HG能避开Oracle陷入的局部最优。

Conclusion: 在黑盒跟随者奖励未知的双层强化学习中，RE‑HG可通过逆强化学习恢复奖励并在聚合动态下消除潜在变换导致的梯度偏差，从而实现与白盒Oracle相当的梯度与性能。

Abstract: Hypergradient (HG) methods for bilevel reinforcement learning (RL) typically assume a white-box follower whose reward is known. In practice, however, the leader rarely knows the follower's reward function and can only observe its trajectories. We address this black-box setting and propose Reward-Estimated Hypergradients (RE-HG), which recovers the follower's reward from these trajectories via inverse RL (IRL). Yet IRL identifies the reward only up to potential-based shaping, a transformation that leaves the follower's behavior unchanged but biases the HG. We prove that, for linear rewards, this bias cancels when IRL estimates are aggregated across sufficiently diverse dynamics, which the leader's own updates naturally supply. To handle finite samples, RE-HG truncates small eigenvalues of the aggregated feature covariance, restoring the identifiable subspace and stabilizing the estimation. Experiments show that RE-HG estimates gradients aligned with a white-box Oracle and achieves comparable performance; empirically, the truncation also suppresses HG explosions, letting RE-HG avoid local optima that trap the Oracle in sharp reward landscapes.

</details>

Abstract: Protein language models (PLMs) are standard tools for variant-effect prediction, protein annotation and design, but the compute needed to train and serve them keeps growing. We present HyPLM-Flash, an efficient and open PLM trained by Hyra 2.0, the research agent developed by our team. HyPLM-Flash is a 650-million-parameter masked language model trained on 726 billion tokens, 0.73 times the data of ESM-2 650M and a fifth to an eighth of the data of E1-600M and ESM C 600M. It runs on an inference engine with fused kernels and INT8 matrix multiplication, embedding about 130,000 residues per second on a single NVIDIA H20 GPU, 1.9 to 2.7 times faster than other 600M-class encoders. In a matched evaluation against 28 public checkpoints on 32 tasks, HyPLM-Flash reaches a ProteinGym zero-shot Spearman correlation of 0.409, statistically tied with ESM-2 650M, ESM C 600M and E1-600M, and the highest organismal-fitness score among self-supervised single-sequence models. It gives the best long-range contact precision of all models evaluated, including ESM-2 15B and ESM C 6B, and a lower masked-residue loss than ESM-2 650M, ESM C 600M and E1-600M on held-out sequences and on recently released PDB chains. Matching state-of-the-art accuracy at a fraction of the training and inference cost, HyPLM-Flash sets a new Pareto frontier for protein language models. Abstract: Large materials design spaces often contain rare high-performing configurations that are difficult to identify through trial-and-error exploration or purely representative machine-learning (ML) sampling. In this work, we present FruBO, a flexible Bayesian Optimization framework for accelerating the discovery of optimal configurations in computationally demanding materials design problems. As a case study, we investigate multilayer Moiré patterns generated by the superposition of six rotated hexagonal lattices, where the objective is to identify the rotational conformation that maximizes pore-size uniformity, quantified using the Jain fairness index. The resulting benchmark contains 600,000 unique configurations and exhibits a highly skewed objective distribution, making the global optimum difficult to locate by random sampling. We compare random exploration, Gaussian Process-based Bayesian Optimization, and Bayesian Optimization using a lightweight CatBoost surrogate with committee-based uncertainty estimation. Random sampling identifies the global optimum only after 533,012 evaluations, requiring approximately 31 hours, while Gaussian Process-based BO becomes impractical due to GPU memory exhaustion after 290 acquired samples. In contrast, FruBO with CatBoost identifies the global optimum after 1,470 acquired samples and approximately 1.15 hours. These results demonstrate that scalable surrogate models can substantially improve the feasibility and efficiency of BO in large nanomaterials design spaces.

Abstract: Many real-world applications require language models to internalize knowledge that was absent from pretraining, to incorporate continually updated facts, or to learn new domain skills. Self-distillation has emerged as a natural approach for this task. A model conditioned on the source material acts as the teacher, and an unconditioned student is trained to reproduce the teacher's behavior closed-book. We propose Speculative Self-Distillation (SSD), an efficient per-token mixed-policy method for self-distillation. The student generates each rollout by default, while the teacher intervenes only at positions where its next-token distribution substantially diverges from the student's. Unlike off-policy distillation with teacher-generated prefixes, this design keeps training rollouts close to the student's test-time behavior, while avoiding the low-signal states that fully on-policy rollouts drift into. In effect, SSD lets the teacher steer the rollout exactly where the privileged context changes the prediction. On three closed-book QA benchmarks measuring fact recall, knowledge updates, and skill acquisition, SSD nearly matches on-policy distillation while using 38-69% fewer supervised tokens and consistently outperforms off-policy distillation on the held-out test set. On a forgetting stress test, SSD retains the most general capability while also achieving the best in-domain accuracy and using less supervision than the on-policy baseline. Overall, SSD establishes a new accuracy-efficiency-forgetting Pareto frontier for self-distillation.

</details>

Abstract: Verifying the local robustness of neural networks under $\ell_0$ perturbations is inherently combinatorial, as it must reason over all sparse subsets of $ε$ perturbed pixels. This paper presents FocusBranch, a combinatorial branch-and-bound framework for $\ell_0$ robustness verification. The key insight is to make the $\ell_0$-ball decomposable and branchable using a node abstraction, which consists of a focus pixel set $\mathcal{S}$ and a level $l$, permitting at most $ε-l$ pixels outside $\mathcal{S}$. This abstraction unifies two branching mechanisms: concrete nodes branch through upper shadows to fix additional perturbed locations, while super nodes branch through covering designs to cover many perturbation cases with fewer child nodes. FocusBranch proves node safety by concretizing linear relaxations over node-constrained $\ell_0$-balls and recursively branches on unknown nodes. Rather than relying on a fixed verification plan, it samples nodes across levels to estimate safe rates and costs, then selects a starting level and branching strategy predicted to minimize verification time. Experiments on fully connected and convolutional classifiers for MNIST, Fashion-MNIST, and CIFAR-10 show that FocusBranch verifies $\ell_0$ robustness effectively across architectures and outperforms state-of-the-art verifiers. The code is available at: https://github.com/simpl-group/FocusBranch.

</details>

Abstract: ReLU networks are well-known to have continuous scale symmetries; infinitely many reparameterized weight vectors that represent the same function. Dinh et al. (2017) have shown that, by reparameterizing, the geometry of the loss landscape (e.g., curvature) of these networks can be changed arbitrarily without affecting the underlying function. Thus, any quantity that depends on the network's geometry should be defined with care. In this paper, we show that the Empirical Bayes (EB) Laplace's approximation is not well-defined for ReLU networks, mainly due to Hessian dependence in computing the marginal likelihood, which is not invariant under ReLU scale symmetries. Thus, we can get different prior precisions, posterior covariances, and predictive uncertainties for a network having identical predictions under ReLU scaling. We define family of prior precision fields indexed by $γ$ and introduce $γ$-canonicalization, which first converts the network to its canonical representation by balancing the ratio of incoming to outgoing weights, and then fits the Empirical Bayes (EB) Laplace to the canonical representation. This procedure is equivalent to transporting the isotropic Gaussian prior from the canonical representation to the original coordinates, by producing a ReLU-scale-compatible precision field. This makes the marginal likelihood invariant under ReLU scaling, while allowing $γ$ to be selected by maximizing the marginal likelihood. The resulting EB Laplace approximation is well-defined on ReLU scale-equivalence classes and yields consistent prior precision selection and predictive uncertainties across functionally identical parameterizations.

</details>

Abstract: Constrained preference-conditioned MORL typically assumes full task observability, treating preferences and safety budgets as stationary task signals. In real-world deployment, however, these signals are often unreliable proxies: intent may drift, safety tolerances may shift with context, and exposed descriptors may be noisy, delayed, or incomplete. We therefore formulate constrained preference-conditioned MORL as a partially observed task-specification problem, where the agent must navigate a fundamental conflict between unreliable external signals and latent interaction dynamics. We propose \textbf{BECON}, a belief-conditioned framework for constrained MORL with drifting preferences and budgets, which infers latent task context from recent histories to estimate preferences and safety budgets. These estimates are fused with the exposed task signal via uncertainty-dependent gates, enabling the policy to defer to observations under high epistemic uncertainty while correcting them when history is informative. The fused context jointly conditions preference optimization and context-adaptive constraint enforcement. Experiments on diverse multi-objective control tasks demonstrate that BECON consistently improves over state-of-the-art MORL baselines, achieving stronger robustness and constraint satisfaction under task drift.

</details>

Abstract: The Natarajan dimension is a fundamental measure of the capacity of multiclass hypothesis classes and plays an important role in analyzing their generalization ability. In this paper, by establishing lower and upper bounds, we show that the exact Natarajan dimension of homogeneous multiclass linear predictors with pairwise distinct class weights is \(n(k-1)-1\), where $n$ is the dimension of the feature space and $k$ is the number of classes, for \(n,k\ge3\), \(n,k\in\mathbb N\).

Abstract: A model that takes over another model's conversation must normally re-read the whole history; a text hand-off shortens the re-read only by dropping content. KV-cache translation instead maps the source's cache into the target's own format, so the target continues from the full context without re-reading it. Abstract: Machine learning (ML) services increasingly expose explainability outputs alongside model predictions. While such explanations are intended to improve transparency, they may also reveal information about the underlying training data. In this work, we show that the local surrogate geometry employed by Local Interpretable Model-agnostic Explanations (LIME) can be repurposed as a proxy for a sample's distance to the model's decision boundary. We extend this boundary-distance view to tabular counterfactual explanations and, for the first time, to visual counterfactual explanations for image classifiers. We present XposedMIA, a one-shot, explanation-based black-box membership inference attack (MIA) framework that converts the returned explanation into a proxy for the queried sample's distance to the model decision boundary. After a one-time calibration step on a small labeled calibration set of as few as 30 samples, our attack requires only a single query for each target sample. Unlike prior label-only attacks requiring iterative queries or shadow training, our method relies solely on the predicted label and its accompanying explanation. Our evaluation indicates that XposedMIA achieves high effectiveness, with success rates of up to 0.95 using only a single query per target, revealing that releasing explanations introduces an additional channel for membership leakage.

Abstract: We study how much the interaction strategy around a fixed, locally hosted 7B-parameter code model (Qwen2.5-Coder-7B-Instruct) affects its ability to replace PyTorch operators with custom CUDA kernels. On 30 KernelBench tasks (levels 1-3, first 10 each, evaluated on a V100), we compare four regimes of increasing structure: zero-shot prompting, profiling-guided prompting, iterative refinement on execution feedback, and a multi-agent loop (analysis, retrieval, generation, evaluation, feedback triage) in which the same model fills four roles around a non-LLM evaluator. Three findings stand out. (1) Correctness, not speed, is the binding constraint: even correct generated kernels average 0.97-0.99x the speed of eager PyTorch, because a 7B model cannot out-write cuBLAS/cuDNN on dense GEMM and convolution; the only real wins are fused memory-bound epilogues. (2) The best strategy depends on difficulty: profiling-guided prompting is strongest overall (43% correct, 27% faster than baseline), but the agent loop is the only method that solves any level-2 fusion problem (3/10 vs. 0/10 for all others). (3) Naive agentic scaffolding is actively harmful: our first agent scored 3% correct, below zero-shot's 30%, and the decisive repairs were matching the agent's ambition to the model's competence and spending compute on best-of-n sampling under a real execution oracle (3% to 33% correct from n=1 to n=8), not on more reasoning steps.

The host replays a run exactly from its seed and actions, so training episodes can restart from states the learner has visited, and a failure-driven curriculum allocates these restarts in proportion to where complete runs are lost. On 12,800 held-out games at Ascension 10 (A10), the released policies win 8.05% averaged over the five characters (Ironclad 15.6%, Regent 12.5%), and AlphaSpire's released networks used without search win 0.55% on a common panel. Longer A10 training raised Ironclad from 12.4% to 15.6% and Defect from 3.0% to 8.1% on these games. At Ascension 0 the released policies win 80.2%, level with the 80.3% that the tree-search agent Spire Mentor reports, while Mentor leads by 5 to 8 points from A4 upward. Run in our host on the same 2,000 A10 games, Mentor's search wins 8.8% against 8.2% for our policies, which need 21-27 ms per decision on one CPU core. GPT-6 Astra at maximum reasoning won none of 11 A10 games through our text interface. In a controlled study of 18 training runs from six random initializations, failure-driven allocation exceeds uniform restart allocation on each of three Regent initializations, by 3.6 percentage points on average, while the Silent effect is small and changes sign. Code, trained policies, an in-game mod and the records behind every table are at https://github.com/HalfVulpes/sts2ai.

Abstract: Wearable Inertial Measurement Units (IMUs) enable pervasive Human Activity Recognition (HAR), but traditional Deep Neural Networks (DNNs) suffer from an "arithmetic-latency deadlock" due to dense floating-point MAC operations. It introduces two key innovations: (1) a MultiHeadFourier attention that performs frequency-domain global mixing, reducing complexity from \(O(V^2)\) to \(O(V\log V)\), eliminating spatial blind spots, and saving \(\sim\)50% GPU memory; and (2) a dual-compartment Temporal Spiking LIF (TSLIFNode) that utilizes dynamic thresholds to adapt to non-stationary rhythms and mitigate gradient degradation. Abstract: External tool calls are a major running cost of an LLM agent, while its training objectives align almost exclusively with task completion. Some methods add an efficiency reward to the objective to reduce the call count, but introduce problems of their own: the anchor they price calls against is computed from the policy's own rollouts (e.g., the group's shortest correct rollout) and so moves with the policy; training can drift toward a collapsed policy that issues no tool call at all, a degeneracy seen in published reports and in our 27B baseline; and a training run delivers a single operating point, so the user cannot trade efficiency against capability. We present CARE-PO (Controllable Anchored Relative Efficiency Policy Optimization) to mitigate these problems. The efficiency reward prices calls against a reference budget anchored to the task's own difficulty and fixed before training. Injecting a successful trajectory from the training data into each online GRPO rollout group and charging for evidence a trajectory never gathers ease the drift toward a collapsed call count. Shaping the cost curve by an effort tag in the prompt yields an efficiency and a capability operating point from a single training run. On GAIA, five of the six operating points across three model sizes improve on their base model in success rate and call count at once. On four held-out multi-hop benchmarks the result is size-dependent: at 27B and 4B success rate is held or raised at a lower call count, while at 9B both operating points trade success rate for fewer calls. The call count remains well above zero throughout training at all three sizes.

Abstract: One-shot Federated Graph Learning (OFGL) has emerged as a communication-efficient paradigm for federated graph learning under strict bandwidth constraints, compressing collaboration into a single transmission round. Nevertheless, this extreme setting suffers from a fundamental Single-Transmission Bottleneck, which limits both the quality and utility of exchanged knowledge. First, while existing methods attempt to distill private graphs into shareable proxies, their aggressive one-shot compression inherently triggers a fidelity crisis. This compression distorts local graph topology and semantics across clients, leading to externalized knowledge underexpression. Second, although the server aggregates these proxies to update global models, current integration schemes suffer from transferability agnosticism. The server cannot reliably identify cross-client collaboration potential, thus resulting in integrated knowledge underutilization. To tackle these dual challenges, we propose FedHFT, an effective One-Shot Personalized Federated Graph Learning (OPFGL) framework that addresses this bottleneck from two perspectives, namely High-Fidelity Knowledge Externalization and Transferability-Ware Knowledge Integration. We use Fidelity-Driven Proxy Refinement to preserve client-side knowledge fidelity, and Transferability-Steered Personalized Collaboration to enable adaptive server-side collaboration. The superiority of FedHFT is validated through extensive experiments on both homophilic and heterophilic graphs. The code is available at https://github.com/Jumpy-Juice/FedHFT.

A quantile-trimmed UCB index that knows the visibility constants attains the central-moment benchmark on this class, and no single strategy adapts across all visibility levels. The same index achieves logarithmic expected regret on every instance with a visible optimal arm and finite-mean rewards. Abstract: Autoregressive graph generators typically linearize graphs through flat node-edge walks, which can obscure higher-order structure in large and complex graphs. We introduce a hierarchical graph abstraction and coarse-to-fine generation pipeline that separates coarsening, tokenization, and decoding. This exposes three design axes: community coarsening, hierarchical tokenization, and order randomization. Across these axes, traversal-based hierarchy consistently improves distributional and structural fidelity, while its V.U.N.\ cost depends strongly on the coarsening and randomization policy. In particular, the fidelity--V.U.N.\ trade-off observed under within-community randomization is substantially reduced when traversal order is randomized across the full hierarchy. With a fixed backbone, training protocol, and matched training time across five molecular and five non-molecular benchmarks, hierarchical serialization yields consistent distributional and structural gains on all molecular datasets, while the non-molecular results further show that these gains depend on how well the supplied hierarchy aligns with structure in the target distribution. Together, these results characterize when and how hierarchical serialization benefits autoregressive graph generation.

Abstract: Graph transformers can capture long-range interactions, but their quadratic attention cost limits their use on large graphs. Abstract: Continual Test-Time Adaptation (CTTA) methods update models online using a stream of unlabeled data to overcome distribution shifts encountered at test time. However, CTTA is often unstable, as it can reinforce biases in early predictions and increasingly predict only a few classes, resulting in drift or even collapse. In this paper, we identify narrowing pseudo-label class usage as an indicator of CTTA instability and quantify this behavior. Most existing methods have attempted to address this issue by either selecting reliable adaptation samples or re-weighting the loss to encourage diverse pseudo-labels, but they do not explicitly enforce a class marginal over a temporal window. Instead, we propose Sinkhorn Class-Marginal Regularization (SCALAR), a simple plug-and-play module that controls the pseudo-label distribution over a short temporal window. SCALAR works by maintaining recent predictions in a buffer, balancing their class distribution via the Sinkhorn algorithm to match a specified target distribution, and using the balanced soft targets for adaptation. Without changing the model architecture, SCALAR maintains the per-step runtime and increases the peak allocated GPU memory by only 10 MB on ImageNet-C. Our method is particularly effective on skewed, non-stationary data with severe distribution shifts, where SCALAR significantly alleviates drift and collapse when combined with existing stabilization methods based on entropy or diversity.

</details>

Abstract: We present a general method for discovering symbolic local learning rules that can replace backpropagation, applicable, in principle, to any deep learning architecture and task. The method automatically discovers local loss functions specific to each layer by (1) constructing a dictionary of monomials of local tensor variables, inspired by Penrose diagram notation, (2) regressing over coefficients weighting these monomials to build layerwise polynomial losses whose gradients together minimize a target global loss. Across all tasks, the discovered rules induce non-trivial interactions between layers, which lead to significant reductions in the corresponding global losses. Abstract: Reinforcement learning (RL) from raw sensory inputs such as images or onboard sensors can be challenging due to high-dimensional observations and sparse rewards. A common strategy is to train a teacher policy with access to privileged state information and then distill it into a student policy that acts from raw inputs alone. However, when the teacher relies on information unavailable to the student, exact imitation may be impossible, creating an irreducible imitation gap. Existing approaches typically mitigate this mismatch through careful reward shaping or additional reinforcement learning on the student policy after the teacher has been trained. Instead, we introduce Representation-Aligned Imitation Learning (RAIL), which addresses the mismatch during teacher training itself. RAIL learns a latent representation shared across teacher and student observations using contrastive learning, and trains the teacher policy directly in this space. Because the teacher policy is constrained to operate on this shared space, it learns behaviors that are reproducible from student observations. This substantially reduces the imitation gap while preserving task performance. Across multiple environments, RAIL outperforms strong baselines without reward modification or post-hoc student fine-tuning, and surpasses direct reinforcement learning from raw observations. The learned representations further enable zero-shot transfer to new tasks through teacher-only training. Project site: https://merajmammadov.com/rail/.

</details>

Abstract: Time-to-event prediction is fundamentally challenged by partial observability and aleatoric uncertainty. In clinical medicine, for instance, the timing of disease progression or acute readmission is contingent on highly dynamic and largely latent health trajectories. Rather than forcing a deterministic point forecast, distributional regression offers a principled means to address this complexity by predicting a probability distribution over time. However, explaining these distributional predictions presents a key obstacle: raw distribution parameters output by the model are abstract mathematical quantities that do not align well with domain concepts. To gain interpretability, we propose a novel paradigm where output parameters are designed to be additive in log-odds---a widely established measure in clinical risk modeling. Our approach, called ODDS-DR, moves beyond technical convenience to deliver clear domain alignment as well as the layer-wise computational structure required for robust, time-dependent feature attribution. Validated across two real-world clinical cohorts, our framework matches state-of-the-art predictive performance while resolving distributional outputs into interpretable risk markers across flexible prognostic horizons.

Abstract: LLM-driven time series forecasting has become increasingly promising by leveraging contextual features to generate future values directly or refine forecast priors. However, incorporating contextual features alone does not ensure that models can identify which features matter for forecasting and understand what they suggest about future dynamics. We argue that the fundamental challenge is how to extract predictive evidence from contextual features and when and how to leverage it to guide forecasting decisions. In this paper, we propose CastReasoner, an evidence-grounded reasoning model for context-aware time series forecasting. Specifically, CastReasoner uses a structured chain of thought (CoT) to extract predictive evidence from contextual features, infer the future dynamics suggested by this evidence, and assess these dynamics against the forecast prior to guide adaptive forecasting decisions. To enable this capability, we design a reasoning-centric two-stage post-training framework including supervised fine-tuning (SFT) and reinforcement learning (RL). First, we synthesize reasoning trajectories through controllable multi-teacher guidance and select high-quality ones using a novel student-aware difficulty metric. To address heterogeneous learning needs across domains in both stages, we introduce a convergence-guided data balancing strategy. Extensive experiments across multi-domain forecasting benchmarks demonstrate that CastReasoner achieves superior forecasting performance.

Abstract: The heavy-ball method with a constant step size and a constant momentum parameter accelerates gradient descent on quadratics, but no choice of the two parameters does so on the class of smooth strongly convex functions. We determine how much faster than gradient descent it can be made on this class. Writing the best worst-case rate over all tunings as $1-c(κ)/κ$, where $κ$ is the condition number, we prove that $c(κ)$ tends to $8$ in every dimension of two or more. The lower bound on the rate holds for every tuning, because a tuning that is faster than $1-8/κ$ on quadratics follows a slowly decaying spiral on some function of two variables. The matching upper bound comes from an explicit tuning, with nearly the momentum of Polyak's tuning and a step size about $\sqrtκ/8$ times smaller, and from a Lyapunov function whose positivity is verified in exact arithmetic. Optimally tuned heavy-ball is therefore asymptotically four times faster than gradient descent on this class.

Abstract: The growing complexity of Multi-Agent Reinforcement Learning (MARL) has fragmented the literature, obscuring underlying architectural choices behind diverse implementations. This fragmentation makes it difficult to evaluate critical modeling dimensions in isolation, identify how components affect the resulting system dynamics, and compare, reuse, or combine contributions across the literature.
  This paper proposes a modular perspective for modeling, analyzing, and implementing MARL systems. We decompose them into eight functional modules: agents, environments, learning, reward modeling, training and execution modes, communication, modeling of other agents, and evaluation criteria. For each module, we clarify its role and review representative approaches.
  We instantiate this decomposition in MARLKIT, a framework whose modules are explicit and interchangeable. MARLKIT demonstrates how the proposed perspective supports transparent experimental assumptions and the controlled variation and combination of MARL mechanisms. Experiments illustrate its ability to prototype, compare, reuse and combine modules while making differences between configurations explicit.
  Overall, the paper provides a structured perspective on MARL modeling and implementation, offering a common language for making modeling choices explicit, supporting clearer descriptions of MARL approaches, and supporting more controlled experimentation through the systematic isolation and combination of MARL mechanisms.

Abstract: World models enable agents to plan and learn from imagined experience, but in an unfamiliar environment they must learn its mechanics from limited interaction. A mechanic is a rule for how an action or an interaction changes part of the environment. Each occurrence of a mechanic that the model observes is one exposure. We ask how many exposures a world model needs to predict the effects of a new environment's mechanics. We address this question on ARC-AGI-3, a benchmark of interactive games defined by their programs. We introduce MechEval, which labels 69 recurring mechanics across 20 games from these programs. It scores each prediction on the cells the mechanic changes at each exposure. As a test bed, we build WARC (World model for ARC-AGI-3), a 35M-parameter vision transformer that is pretrained on other games and updates all of its parameters after each transition. On held-out games, WARC learns to predict four in five mechanics with at least 80% accuracy on the cells they change and keeps that accuracy to the end of the recordings. Half of them reach that accuracy within about 80 exposures, in a single play of the game, and without updates only one in seven does at all. In closed-loop play on the public games, WARC-Agent, which pairs WARC with a fixed graph explorer, triples the explorer's official efficiency score from 0.15 to 0.46, mostly through test-time training. On the hidden games of the Kaggle leaderboard, our entry scores 0.20.

Abstract: When a neuron's input is lost, or known through a belief $q$, what should it output? For a neuron storing a joint polynomial density $N$ with input density $D$, two formulas compete. $p_\mathrm{CD}=E_q[N]/E_q[D]$ costs one matrix-vector product; Hierarchical Correlation Reconstruction introduces it as a constant-denominator approximation to $p_\mathrm{EU}=E_q[N/D]$. For a density model both are exact answers to different questions: $p_\mathrm{CD}$ is Bayesian updating with the belief as virtual evidence; $p_\mathrm{EU}$ installs the belief as a new marginal (Jeffrey's rule). With a missing input $p_\mathrm{CD}$ returns the model's conditional; $p_\mathrm{EU}$ generally does not. This post hoc reading is proved, and illustrated outside the pair family up to $k=8$ inputs; it has no pre-registered test, only one exploratory real-data pilot. Post hoc, closed forms fall within a factor 2 of all 112 measured gaps, and negative density begins near input correlation $1/k$. In a pre-registered synthetic study (up to 32 inputs), 5 of 15 hypotheses are supported, 2 inconclusive and 8 not supported, including all three on transfer beyond equicorrelated Gaussians. Post hoc, where $p_\mathrm{EU}$ is defined, truncation usually outweighs the gap for Beta beliefs in pair neurons; triple neurons under flat and data beliefs are exceptions.

However, its sample complexity has a suboptimal dependence on $k$. We propose the first algorithm that achieves optimal sample complexity in both $d$ and $k$, while retaining properties (2) and (3) of \kdppca. Additionally, prior utility guarantees require a minimum sample size with exponential dependence on the eigengap. Our method removes this dependence. It also improves the dependence on spectral parameters, matching known lower bounds for the spiked covariance model. Unlike deflation-based approaches like \kdppca, our algorithm updates the full $d\times k$ subspace jointly rather than one eigenvector at a time. This non-deflation structure simplifies the algorithm, reduces the number of hyper-parameters, and improves computational efficiency, particularly when using block linear algebra libraries.

TL;DR: RSBI是一种基于扩散的变分方法，用于无似然贝叶斯推断，利用Schrödinger桥实现高效采样，并通过一次性代理似然避免多轮推断，适用于大先验体积和多模态后验。


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>

Result: 在标准SBI基准上表现优异，尤其在先验体积增大情况下，仅约5%性能下降；在引力波环降后验估计中也展现出良好效果。

Conclusion: RSBI通过一次性高效proposal结合SB采样和精细化校正，实现了在大先验空间和多模态后验中的高采样效率，且不易受先验泄漏影响；可扩展到真实物理问题如黑洞谱学探测。

Abstract: We introduce Rapid Simulation Based Inference (RSBI), a diffusion-based variational approach to likelihood-free Bayesian inference that achieves high sampling efficiency under large prior-to-posterior volumes. RSBI builds on advances in Schrödinger Bridge (SB) diffusion sampling to handle multi-modal posteriors, with an initial likelihood supplied by a distance measure via a differentiable simulator. The key innovation of this initial surrogate likelihood is that it gives a highly efficient proposal distribution, replacing sequential rounds of other methods with an efficient one-shot proposal. Subsequent refinement then transports the proposal to the true posterior as specified by the simulator and the original prior. The SB sampler is warm-started from the proposal, and its energy is corrected by a neural likelihood ratio that is learned only where the proposal places mass. As a variational method, RSBI does not suffer from prior leakage commonly observed in standard sequential posterior estimation methods. To improve mode coverage we optionally utilize the well-tempered meta-dynamics framework, which also encourages exploration of the prior volume. We observe strong performance on standard SBI benchmarks, particularly when the prior volume is increased relative to the ground truth posterior, achieving a performance degradation of only $\sim5\%$ on Two Moons under a highly constrained simulation budget. Additionally, we evaluate RSBI's performance for gravitational wave ring-down posterior estimation, in a real-world physics benchmark inspired by black hole spectroscopy.

</details>

Abstract: Multi-armed bandits are a natural framework for many real-world sequential decision problems, from recommender systems and clinical trials to educational test recommendation and online advertising. The performance of these algorithms depends on how they explore different arms, and exploration strategies range from variance-independent (e.g., UCB1) to variance-aware (e.g., UCB-V). In practice the reward variance of each arm is rarely known and varies widely across arms, and the reward support is often underspecified. In these cases no single rule is uniformly best: variance-independent methods are robust but loose when variances are small, while variance-aware methods are tight only when their variance estimates and the assumed support bound are reliable. Such heterogeneous, unknown variances arise in many settings, for instance when multiple objectives are scalarized into a single reward. We propose UCB-min, a per-arm adaptive meta-algorithm that selects, for each arm, the tighter of a variance-independent and a variance-aware exploration bonus, without prior knowledge of the variance regime and without reward normalization. We prove that, with UCB-V as variance-aware base, its regret is bounded by the sum of the two base bounds at the standard exploration constant, and by their per-arm minimum, up to a constant, for a larger one. We validate it on synthetic data, an educational tutoring task, and two group-recommendation benchmarks (MovieLens, Steam), showing that UCB-min consistently tracks the best base algorithm across diverse variance regimes and datasets.

</details>

Abstract: Probabilistic circuits (PCs) have emerged as generative models guaranteeing tractable probabilistic inference. However, tractability comes at the price of larger model sizes than in other generative models. As such, to scale PCs to high-dimensional data such as images, previous work has devised several parameter sharing techniques as well as fast evaluation protocols that consider learning PCs on independent image patches. In this work, we revisit this practice to highlight a surprising learnability issue: models that assume independence counterintuitively can yield better likelihoods than models that do not. To break this apparent bottleneck and to scale PCs further, we introduce a recursive parameter sharing construction (RePA) that fosters efficiency without assuming independence. We show empirically that RePA can greatly reduce time and memory consumption while yielding competitive likelihoods. Inspired by these results, we further generalize the approach to data modalities that do not inherently exhibit patch-structure (NoPA), such as tabular data, showing similar efficiency improvements, with marginal changes in likelihoods and sample quality on seven tabular datasets. Code available at https://github.com/april-tools/RePA.

Abstract: Accurate prediction of electronic Hamiltonians would enable broad property inference while avoiding the high computational cost of Density Functional Theory (DFT). However, progress toward general-purpose materials foundation models is limited by a data bottleneck: existing Hamiltonian datasets are typically small, lack structural diversity, and often omit essential relativistic physics such as spin--orbit coupling (SOC). We therefore construct UniHam, a large-scale Hamiltonian dataset and benchmark suite comprising 100,000+ DFT-computed complex-valued Hermitian Hamiltonians with full SOC, covering 72 elements and a wide range of crystal geometries and symmetries (spanning diverse lattice types and space-group families). Building on UniHam, we benchmark two representative state-of-the-art models under a standardized protocol and introduce complementary evaluation metrics that jointly assess three dimensions: (i) Hamiltonian reconstruction accuracy, (ii) out-of-distribution (OOD) generalization across composition/symmetry shifts, and (iii) the ability to support downstream property prediction from the predicted Hamiltonians. Experiments on UniHam demonstrate that the proposed benchmark and metrics effectively differentiate model capabilities, revealing intrinsic SOC- and element-dependent failure modes, large variations in compositional OOD robustness, and the necessity of spectral-level evaluation to assess whether Hamiltonian predictions reliably support downstream electronic-structure properties. Overall, UniHam provides a reproducible, SOC-complete benchmark that can sharpen model comparisons and accelerate the development of next-generation foundation models for quantum materials.

Abstract: Many tasks compose transformations by a known rule: chained rotations in robotics and navigation. Learned models relearn it, compounding error; classical estimators hardcode it and miss hardware deviations. We impose the known structure and learn only the remainder: an exact non-commutative group law sits in the node of a self-contained layer, its edges learn local increments, and a zero-initialized correction psi learns unknown deviations of the law. A pre-training criterion predicts exact representability. On fixed-angle SU(2) composition, a 54-parameter dense edge is the most accurate dense model, at 27x fewer parameters than the strongest fixed-matrix baseline, and lies 0.05 deg above the Bayes floor. On real robot arms, an isolated 306-parameter psi reaches 1.12-1.14 mm versus 1.29-1.33 mm for Gauss-Newton at N >= 60, and the recipe transfers across three vendors and two protocols. The recipe matches classical fits where the data obey the law and loses where the imposed law is not the task's.

Abstract: Likelihood-based methods are widely used to learn the parameters of partially observable Markov decision processes (POMDPs). While finite-sample guarantees for policy suboptimality exist for certain POMDP subclasses, analogous guarantees for parameter recovery remain less well understood. For any candidate POMDP parameter, we bound its parameter recovery error in terms of its likelihood gap to the ground truth, the structural conditioning of the observation and transition kernels, and the behavior policy used to collect data. The bound applies in both the undercomplete and overcomplete regimes, with the latter following naturally from the undercomplete analysis. Because the result applies to any sufficiently good candidate, it yields finite-sample guarantees for the maximum-likelihood estimator and any procedure producing a parameter with controlled likelihood gap. This is particularly useful for near-optimal learning in infinite-horizon POMDPs, where existing approaches largely rely on model-based planning with accurate transition and observation kernel estimates. The bound separates the contributions of the behavior policy and model, showing how data-collection choices, including action coverage, and their effects on mixing can accelerate parameter learning.

Abstract: Optimizing molecules under small evaluation budgets requires deciding both where in chemical space to search and which candidate within that region to evaluate. We introduce Trust Region Adaptive Chemical Exploration (TRACE), a framework for open-ended molecular Bayesian optimization (BO), where candidates are generated on demand rather than selected from a fixed library. TRACE combines an objective-free graph-perturbation mechanism with a fixed, chemistry-aware Gaussian process (GP) surrogate using fragprints, a representation combining molecular fingerprints and fragment descriptors. Rather than learning the search geometry itself, TRACE makes the scale of chemical exploration explicit through a Tanimoto-similarity threshold controlling chemical extent and a cardinality budget controlling candidate-search breadth. A simple success/failure controller expands or contracts both quantities during optimization, while the GP and acquisition function select which admissible molecules to evaluate. Across six challenging molecular optimization benchmarks under matched 1,000-evaluation budgets, TRACE achieves the strongest overall sample efficiency as measured by the best molecule found as evaluations accumulate. Crossed ablations on two objectives further show that adaptive region control contributes beyond the proposal mechanism, while its benefit depends on the proposal distribution. We further apply TRACE to the stability of iron phthalocyanine (FePc), a non-precious molecular electrocatalyst whose degradation can involve loss of the Fe center. Using a first-principles density functional theory (DFT) workflow designed for accurate energy evaluation, TRACE increases the computed thermodynamic resistance to Fe dissolution from 0.42 to 2.59 eV over 80 designed evaluations.

Abstract: Reinforcement Learning (RL) algorithms often rely on data collected from a target environment, which can be expensive and noisy. Although simulators can generate large amounts of additional data, standard simulation mixing approaches, which directly combine simulated and real experience, can degrade performance due to simulator inaccuracies, especially when they bias the solution away from the optimal policy. Abstract: Routing a melanoma case to routine follow-up is a serious action error that diagnostic coverage alone does not rule out. We present Harm-Constrained Risk-Optimal Conformal Prediction (HC-ROCP), a training-free framework that uses frozen classifier probabilities and adds a harm penalty when choosing prediction sets and actions. We test the resulting policies on disjoint calibration splits with multiplicity adjustment. HC-ROCP certifies supported claims, reports descriptive fallback policies, and refuses unsupported subgroup claims. We evaluate these outputs retrospectively on COVID-19 radiographs, ISIC 2019 dermoscopy, and EyePACS-to-APTOS retinopathy transfer. Across ten calibration seeds, HC-ROCP reduces COVID-19 severe-action errors from the exact risk-optimal baseline's 4.68\% to 0.54\%. At a 5\% budget, global claims are certified for COVID-19 and ISIC, but the rare squamous-cell-carcinoma claim is refused. At 1\%, all settings receive descriptive reports. For each budget, we also report the minimum severe-case count needed to pass the global harm gate with zero errors, providing a starting point for further data collection.

Abstract: Trade promotions consume a large share of consumer-goods revenue, yet most planners still set discounts with a single, often unreliable, price elasticity per item. We present an end-to-end Trade Promotion Optimisation (TPO) system built as one chain: analogue SKUs, point elasticity, elasticity curve, price recommendation, promotional lift and net lift. Elasticity is measured at post-promo time from directional price-dip events against a rolling regular price, and at promo time by a causal estimator that residualises trend, seasonality and promotion, handles stock-out censoring with a Tobit likelihood and updates itself from the first clean promotion week. Abstract: Stellarators are fusion devices that use externally generated, three-dimensional magnetic fields to confine hot plasma and offer a promising route to commercial fusion. Their electromagnetic coils pose a challenging design problem: they must reproduce the target magnetic field while satisfying engineering constraints and remaining practical and cost-effective to manufacture. Finding suitable geometries is a highly nonlinear inverse problem that generally has neither a closed-form nor a unique solution. This problem lies at the heart of stellarator design. We introduce CoilStellaration, a dataset and benchmark for learning the mapping between coil geometries from target plasma magnetic fields and engineering requirements. The dataset contains 177,000 optimized filamentary coilsets targeting plasma equilibria from ConStellaration, with sampled engineering requirements and achieved geometric and magnetic-field metrics. It spans multiple coil counts and field-period numbers, supporting studies of both specialized and shared models across configurations. The benchmark asks models to predict coils conditioned on a target equilibrium and requirements on coil-coil and coil-plasma spacing, curvature, and magnetic-field error. We show that magnetic-field accuracy remains the main challenge, with learned models rarely satisfying all requirements simultaneously. However, using learned predictions to initialize conventional coil optimization can accelerate early progress. We release baseline models and evaluation code to support reproducible research on both direct coil generation and learned optimization initializations. By lowering the barrier for machine learning and optimization researchers to engage with stellarator coil design, we aim to accelerate progress toward practical, cost-effective stellarator power plants.

</details>

Abstract: We study the problem of binary classification in the marginal-nonuniform learning framework in the agnostic setting. In this model, the constants in the learning rate may depend on the marginal distribution of the unlabeled data, but the guarantee must hold uniformly over all conditional distributions of the labels. We also study fine-grained rates.
  We consider two criteria.
  (1) The standard agnostic setting, where the learner competes with the best concept in the class. In this setting, an immediate consequence of standard agnostic PAC learning results gives us the optimal rate of $\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{n}}$ for every class $\mathcal{H}$ with finite $\mathrm{VC}(\mathcal{H})$ and shows that arbitrarily slow rates are necessary otherwise. This also gives us the optimal fine-grained rate of $\sqrt{\tfrac{\mathrm{VC}(\mathcal{H})}{n}}$. Therefore, the dependence on the marginal distribution does not help, which is in contrast to the realizable setting.
  (2) In the relaxed agnostic setting, where the learner competes with $(1+λ)$ times the error of the best concept for a fixed $λ\in (0,1]$, the picture from the realizable setting reappears. We obtain a trichotomy of the achievable optimal rates: exponential rates of the form $e^{-n}$ arise precisely when the class is finite; linear rates of the form $\tfrac{1}{n}$ arise when the class is infinite but $\mathrm{VC}(\mathcal{H})$ is finite; and arbitrarily slow rates occur when $\mathrm{VC}(\mathcal{H})=\infty$. We also obtain the optimal fine-grained rate of $\tfrac{\mathrm{uVCE}(\mathcal{H})}{λn}$, where $\mathrm{uVCE}(\mathcal{H})$ is a recently introduced combinatorial complexity dimension called the uncentered $\mathrm{VC}$-eluder dimension.

</details>

Abstract: Not all mistakes are alike. For example, in medical diagnosis, false positive and false negative mistakes have a different impact and should not be aggregated. By revisiting the classical theory of boosting through this lens, we introduce the notion of $Z$-learner, which returns weak hypotheses achieving joint false-positive/false-negative guarantees $(\mathrm{FP},\mathrm{FN})$ from some set $Z \subseteq [0,1]^2$. This formulation generalizes the standard notion of $γ$-weak learning--recovered by taking $Z=\{(z_1,z_2): z_1+z_2<1/2-γ\}$--and allows for more flexible notions of weak learnability that account for the impact of the distribution on the achievable learning guarantees. Finally, we demonstrate that $Z$-learners can achieve confidence amplification. However, the analysis is significantly more involved than in classical boosting and relies on the centerpoint theorem from discrete and computational geometry.

Abstract: Accurate classifiers can fail when background cues change without altering the correct labels. For example, given a new image of an outfit, we would like to generate insights that the image depicts "earth tones" and "linen pants" and allow users to explore similar ideas along these themes. Traditional strategies to generate such insights would typically involve building taxonomies producing a golden set labeled by humans, training models on these datasets, and deploying these models for online inference services. Such workflows are constrained by fixed taxonomy, high labeling cost, limited labeling coverage in sample space, and human errors and expertise gaps in labelers. In addition, these methods are expensive and cumbersome to iterate over time. Recent trends have started exploring generative AI models for online insights, but this method suffers from long latency and prohibitive costs to support a high volume of requests or large amounts of offline labeling needs.
  We present Generative Labeling On Clusters (GLOC) as a solution to provide highly scalable, cost-effective, low latency, and high accuracy online image insight services. GLOC leverages highly optimized image embeddings to form image clusters that are semantically close to each other for the purpose of the type of insights, and summarizes the insights at the cluster level, which effectively generalizes to members in the cluster. At Pinterest, GLOC powers image descriptors and pivot query suggestions for Hybrid Search, which is a multimodal search service enabling users to search with both an image and a text query. Our offline and online A/B tests demonstrate that GLOC significantly outperforms previous methods, delivering improvements in quality and efficiency.

Abstract: Reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards is increasingly used to improve language model reasoning, yet its effects on sequential decision procedures remain poorly understood. We study this problem in clinical multi-turn agents that must decide what information to request, when to stop gathering evidence, and which action to take. We introduce three controlled environments: medication refill triage, acute diagnostic work-up, and dosing titration, each with hidden clinical states, query costs, exact adaptive oracles, and deterministic outcome verifiers. Across these tasks, outcome-only group relative policy optimization often improves aggregate reward while producing undesirable procedures, including premature commitment, one-question collapse, and failures on rare high-risk cases. In diagnostics, assigning dense credit to questions that reduce uncertainty improves held-out value and moderate-case support across three training seeds relative to outcome-only reinforcement learning, without the high-risk seed collapse observed under outcome-only training. Separating exploration from exploitation further increases high-risk support at its best checkpoint, but at the cost of excessive information gathering on low-acuity cases. Additional experiments show that the optimal strength of process credit is task-dependent; that after a supervised warmup that already gathers carefully, reinforcement learning raises aggregate reward by trimming questions while degrading high-risk performance; and that reinforcement learning does not reliably repair rare behavior gaps inherited from biased supervised data. Verifiable final outcomes are therefore insufficient for training reliable multi-turn agents: reward design must account for the procedure used to reach an answer, and evaluation must distinguish adaptive information gathering from reward-exploiting shortcuts.

</details>

Abstract: Post-LayerNorm Transformers have seen limited practical adoption due to enduring difficulties in scaling them to large depths. While prior research has focused on stabilising Post-LayerNorm by improving conditioning at initialisation, stability often deteriorates when models are trained with large learning rates, forcing additional compromises. In the decoder-only settings we study, existing Post-LayerNorm methods fail to outperform strong Pre-LayerNorm baselines. We propose KiteNorm, a post-norm training method designed to break this trend by constraining hidden-state variance. This approach is motivated by a bound on the LayerNorm Jacobian suggesting that variance growth contributes to gradient vanishing, alongside empirical evidence linking it to rank collapse. KiteNorm combines residual scaling and variance regularisation to maintain stability throughout training, supported by scalarised double-normalisation that provides separately learnable scales for the skip and residual branches. Across decoder-only Transformers up to 1.3B parameters, KiteNorm improves training stability and outperforms leading baselines at their optimal learning rates, with scaling laws favouring KiteNorm across depth, width, batch size, and training budget. Ablations show that each component is necessary for the full stability and performance gains.

Abstract: Exact score-based causal structure learning supports explicit structural constraints and optimisation certificates, but its branch-and-cut search can be computationally demanding. We augment exact causal structure learning methods with an upper bound on the maximum clique size of the learned undirected skeleton, enforced at integer candidates through lazy cycle and clique constraints. On static linear-Gaussian Erdős-Rényi and scale-free DAGs, we evaluate callback order, alternative clique-cut policies, incumbent repair, fractional separation, and Big-M bounds. We report causal-recovery metrics jointly with optimality gaps, primal and dual bounds, node throughput, MIPSOL events, and callback cost. Two variants receive particular attention: B3 selects the most violated minimal (k+1)-clique inequality at each callback, whereas C3 repairs an infeasible integer candidate by deleting violating edges and refitting affected regression columns using QR-based least squares. The results do not indicate that clique constraints uniformly accelerate optimisation. However, their benefit depends on graph density, sample size, and cut-management policy, while large collections of correlated constraints can reduce node throughput. Tighter valid Big-M bounds reduced the fixed-budget optimality gap in most tested settings, without necessarily changing SHD or F1 at a fixed time limit. These findings characterize the computational trade-offs of clique-constrained exact causal learning, and identify selective separation and incumbent repair as promising directions.

Abstract: Prompt-based continual learning adapts a frozen pre-trained vision transformer by tuning a small set of prompt tokens. Recently, one-prompt methods which share one single prompt across all tasks make this paradigm maximally parameter efficient. The open question is how a shared prompt can keep the knowledge of old classes while being updated for new ones. Existing methods protect old knowledge in parameter space, where they freeze task-specific prompts, expand new components, or sparsely gate which tokens receive gradients. However, this method sacrifices plasticity, yet the drift it still permits in shared tokens can corrupt the prompt's function on past classes. What must be preserved is the prompt's behavior. We propose TEA-Prompt, which couples (i) a token evaluation module that assigns each prompt token a continuous, input-conditional relevance score injected into the attention logits, softly specializing tokens across tasks without discarding any of them, and (ii) an attention-behavior anchoring loss that constrains each token's attention output around class-specific query regions using only compact Gaussian query statistics without stored old images, weighted by the token's past relevance. Anchoring the attention value allows the parameters to continue updating freely, as long as their interaction remains unchanged. TEA-Prompt achieves state-of-the-art accuracy with markedly lower forgetting on CIFAR-100, ImageNet-R, and CUB-200 with only 0.38M parameters. Code is publicy available at https://github.com/cuonga1cvp/TEA-Prompt.git

Abstract: Reinforcement learning is now the mainstream paradigm for training LLMs on agentic tasks, yet standard practice keeps only the best-validation checkpoint and discards the rest. We show that this is lossy: in GRPO training of LLM agents, the best-validation checkpoint fails on problems that earlier checkpoints from the same run had already solved, and the union of problems solved across checkpoints far exceeds any single one. The discarded checkpoints thus form a teacher pool obtained at no additional training cost, which on-policy distillation (OPD) can mine to recover the lost coverage. Mining this pool raises two challenges: i) which checkpoint to distill from, as no checkpoint dominates the others and complementarity to the student is problem-specific; ii) where to trust it, as even a well-chosen teacher is only locally superior, so indiscriminate distillation overwrites behavior the student has already mastered. We propose Agentic-OPD, which treats the best-validation checkpoint as the student and all saved checkpoints as frozen teacher candidates, using the student's evolving capability gaps to guide both teacher selection and supervision filtering: the HCN policy (Highest Coverage Next) dynamically selects teachers by weighted coverage of the student's unsolved problems, while the LAD module (Locality-Aware Distillation) confines transfer to failed student rollouts on problems where the teacher performs better. Across five agentic task domains and 24 benchmarks and task subsets, Agentic-OPD improves over its initialization checkpoint by up to 7.4 points, with a 4B student surpassing the latest frontier models GPT-5.6 and DeepSeek-V4.1 on deep search. Agentic-OPD recasts a finished RL run as a reusable pool of policies and establishes post-RL capability recovery as a stage orthogonal to improvements in the RL algorithm itself.

</details>

Abstract: Counterfactual inference is the task of answering hypothetical "what-if" queries about a unit, given an observational dataset, modeling assumptions, and a causal graph. This enables a modeler to reason about hypothetical scenarios that cannot otherwise be answered without assumptions or a causal graph. The simpler case involves a causal graph with scalar 1-D variables that denote different attributes in the data-generation process. While the literature is clear in the 1-D case, extending this to settings with high-dimensional nodes (e.g., images) remains challenging. Conventionally, in the literature, methods recover the latent state from the factual image (inversion), generate counterfactual attributes from the graph, and render the counterfactual image from the latent state using the updated attributes. In our work, we investigate the necessity of the inversion step in computing high-dimensional counterfactuals. We first discuss moving from the scalar 1-D case to the higher-dimensional case. Secondly, we discuss the relevance of the inversion step and evaluate an inversion-free counterpart repurposed from the image-editing literature. Finally, on image datasets such as CelebA and ADNI, we benchmark our approach using two measures: namely, effectiveness and identity preservation. While effectiveness is a measure of how faithfully the counterfactual attribute is rendered, identity preservation measures whether the same unit (image) is being rendered, both of which are crucial for high-dimensional counterfactual inference. We present the tradeoffs of using inversion-based and inversion-free approaches in our work.

</details>

In a controlled experiment at twice the NVFP4 error, noise fed only to Adam's second-moment estimate acts like a lower learning rate and its cost fades, whereas noise in the update lowers its signal-to-noise ratio and carries about two thirds of the cost through the end of training. Abstract: As large language models become more capable agents, they can exhibit deceptive behaviors, cheating human for higher rewards while appearing helpful. We attribute this failure to evaluation-side partial observability, where models are evaluated mainly from final outcome, without checking the faithfulness of underlying reasoning, tool use, and environmental effects. Prior mitigations address this partial observability by predefined evaluator latent belief space or one-shot monitoring, which do not extend to free-form language space and vulnerable to stronger deceptions that evade simple monitoring. In this work, we formulate deception mitigation as an Evaluation-Side Partially Observable Extensive Form Game (e-PoG), a max-min framework in which an agent maximizes reward, a monitor minimizes the agent's payoff by exposing deception from the full interaction trajectory, and a fixed verifier validates the monitor's claims and converts them into penalties. We define the solution concept as a Subgame-Perfect Oversight Equilibrium (SPOE) between agent and monitor, and prove its existence in finite games. With a perfect verifier, every SPOE is faithful; with an imperfect verifier, the guarantee persists if verifier errors do not reverse the ranking of monitor actions. To achieve faithful SPOE, we derive a two-timescale policy gradient algorithm and enhance the pipeline empirically with NLI verification and deterministic checks. Experiments with Gemma3-4B-IT and Qwen3-4B/8B on Agentic Role-Playing, SearchQA, and WebShop show that e-PoG mitigates multiple emergent deception modes, outperforming the strongest baselines by 26.0% in deception mitigation and by up to 39.8% in task success. These gains persist under weak-to-strong supervision. Code is available at https://github.com/Toyoid/deceptive-agent.

</details>

Moreover, it has been shown that representation similarity metrics can be confounded by model depth and width, requiring appropriate baselines for interpretation. In this work, we first investigate where alignment emerges between unimodal models across model layers and representation dimensions. Next, we find that the PRH capability-alignment trend persists for newer models as long as the captions are sufficiently clean and informative. Finally, we contextualize cross-modal alignment, introducing unimodal and multimodal baselines on various datasets. Our results show that the cross-modal representational alignment of unimodal models can surpass that of explicitly aligned vision-language models, but remains substantially behind the alignment observed for models of the same modality. Code is available at https://github.com/ExplainableML/prh-closer-look.
