Abstract: Anomaly detection (AD) is a critical for trustworthy machine learning and scientific discovery. Existing methods typically define anomalies through local density, isolation heuristics, or distance from a fitted distribution, making them sensitive to neighborhood scale, partitioning choices, or restrictive distributional assumptions. In this work, we introduce a new paradigm by formulating anomaly detection from a diversity perspective. We propose the $\textit{Vendi Anomaly Score}$ (VAS), which detects anomalies by quantifying how much a sample changes the diversity of the dataset, as measured by the Vendi score, when removed. VAS captures both local redundancy and global data structure without relying on density estimation or partitioning heuristics. Moreover, VAS requires no hyperparameter tuning, instead adapting automatically to the spectral structure of the data. VAS is non-parametric and scales linearly with dataset size. Across 148 benchmark embedding datasets derived from 10 source datasets and 4 embedding architectures, VAS achieves state-of-the-art AD performance. We further validate VAS on large-scale ImageNet experiments, where it remains robust as both contamination rate and dataset scale increase.

</details>

Abstract: Motivated by reinforcement learning subject to communication constraints, we study a policy evaluation problem with linear function approximation over a rate-limited channel. In our setup, an agent interacting with an unknown environment (modeled as a Markov Decision Process) transmits quantized estimates of a temporal-difference (TD) learning update direction to a server over a finite-capacity channel. Our goal is to investigate to what extent one can preserve the statistical convergence rates achievable when the channel has infinite capacity. To that end, we propose a new algorithm called Batched Adaptively Quantized TD (BAQ-TD), which uses batching to reduce the variance of the transmitted information, and adaptive quantization to encode innovations. We prove a high-probability finite-time error bound for BAQ-TD, and show that, above a certain finite channel capacity, BAQ-TD preserves the convergence rate of unquantized TD up to logarithmic factors. Specifically, for a $d$-dimensional value function parameter, we establish that transmitting a total of $\mathcal{O}(d \log(T))$ bits over a horizon of length $T$ suffices to achieve the order-optimal rate of $\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(1/T)$, marking an exponential improvement in communication-complexity over prior work.

</details>

Abstract: Stochastic differential equations (SDEs) describe stochastic processes across a wide range of scientific domains, including physics, biology, and finance. Although individual numerical integration steps are inexpensive, observing meaningful events or transitions can require millions of sequential steps, resulting in substantial wall-clock simulation time and poor hardware utilization. Deep learning offers an alternative by amortizing many integration steps into a single neural network forward pass, potentially enabling much faster simulation. However, existing supervised approaches typically require long simulated trajectory data, which restricts their applicability. In this work, we introduce Kolmogorov-Chapman Maps (KC-Maps), a method for learning the transition kernel of an SDE that relies only on the trajectory-free marginal samples together with the known drift and diffusion coefficients. Given an initial time and state of a process, KC-Maps models its distribution integrated to any given time, corresponding to the weak solution of the SDE. To recover the correct dynamics, KC-Maps satisfies two key properties: (i) it satisfies the boundary conditions, which we incorporate directly into the neural network architecture, and (ii) it respects the Kolmogorov--Chapman equation, which provides an efficient training objective. We empirically demonstrate that KC-Maps learns to integrate the dynamics of peptide molecular systems and outperforms classical molecular dynamics in terms of GPU-hours.

</details>

Abstract: Machine learning models often achieve high average accuracy in the training data while performing poorly on test data due to subpopulation shift. A common data-centric solution is to enforce balanced subgroup proportions, but full balance can be costly, infeasible, and not always necessary. In this work, we study whether balanced training data is the unique optimal configuration for robust subpopulation generalization. We extend a Gaussian feature-model framework to theoretically characterize the performance landscape across general training subgroup distributions. We further derive gradient-based directions for adjusting subgroup proportions to improve adjusted accuracy and worst-group accuracy more efficiently. These findings suggest that strategically allocating data offers a more flexible and principled path to robust performance than simple balancing. Abstract: Open Radio Access Network (O-RAN) improves radio access network flexibility through disaggregation, open interfaces, and programmable RAN Intelligent Controllers (RICs). However, the same openness and distribution increase the attack surface and make centralized intrusion detection difficult to scale across heterogeneous RAN sites. This paper presents a federated lightweight Transformer framework for privacy-preserving network-layer cyberattack detection in O-RAN. Each participating site trains a compact Transformer locally on temporally ordered network-flow records, while only model parameters are shared with a federated coordinator. To model realistic site heterogeneity, the client datasets are partitioned according to a Dirichlet distribution, resulting in non-independent and identically distributed (non-IID) local data. The local detector uses feature projection, positional encoding, multi-head self-attention, feed-forward layers, and temporal pooling to classify five network-layer attack categories: ICMP, Slowloris, SYN, TCP-ACK, and UDP. Experiments on the NetsLab 5G O-RAN intrusion-detection dataset show an overall accuracy of 94.89 % and a macro-F1 score of 81.09 %.

Abstract: Secure aggregation in domains such as federated learning, Internet of Things, and voting requires operating on private inputs while preserving confidentiality, verifying that each input satisfies constraints, and avoiding trusted parties. Existing protocols achieve at most two of these requirements. We present zk-DEAP, a protocol combining lifted ElGamal homomorphic encryption, FROST threshold cryptography, and zero-knowledge proofs (Bulletproofs, zk-SNARKs, or zk-STARKs) for fully decentralized, verifiable, privacy-preserving aggregation. Our core technical contribution is a cryptographic binding mechanism preventing substitution attacks, where a valid proof for one input is paired with a ciphertext encrypting a different, malicious value. For Bulletproofs, we bind via ciphertext-derived randomness modification. For zk-SNARKs/zk-STARKs, to bind across algebraic structures (a well-known challenge in practical cryptography), we introduce a novel blinded arithmetic hash commitment with an embedded Schnorr constraint. These binding techniques force the ZKP's private witness to equal the encrypted value, ensuring any circuit-verified constraint applies to the ciphertext. We implement three production-ready, defense-in-depth variants in Rust and evaluate across 5-500 participants. Compared to unverified aggregation, zk-DEAP adds modest overhead while providing cryptographic guarantees against input manipulation that statistical defenses cannot match.

</details>

Abstract: Large language model (LLM) agents increasingly perform complex tasks using high-privilege tools, making indirect prompt injection (IPI) a critical security risk. However, IPI benchmarks lag behind their rapidly evolving utility counterparts, only offering limited task and environment coverage and relying on narrow, static attack scenarios. In this paper, we propose meta-benchmarking for IPI: transforming existing utility benchmarks into end-to-end IPI evaluation suites that reuse their realistic tasks, environments, and infrastructure. We present MetaPI, an extensible toolkit that automatically identifies plausible injection points, generates realistic attack objectives, optimizes adaptive payloads, and validates attacks in the real environment. Its modular interfaces support heterogeneous environments, agents, defenses, and attack algorithms, allowing new components to be integrated without redesigning the evaluation pipeline. We apply MetaPI to AppWorld, VitaBench, and Terminal-Bench and evaluate representative agents and defenses across multiple backbone LLMs. Our results show that no evaluated defense simultaneously achieves strong utility and strong security in realistic, adaptive agentic IPI settings. MetaPI thus enables scalable and consistent security evaluation across diverse and evolving agent ecosystems

Abstract: This technical report describes an alignment investigation into Mythos 5's behaviour during a security incident in UK AISI's cyber evaluations. During an evaluation, Mythos 5 attempted to execute a supply-chain attack against real people not involved in the evaluation task. Our investigation set out to understand the specific behaviour in this incident from an alignment perspective and identify broader lessons about failures of alignment. To do so, we conducted experiments in simulated replications of the original evaluation, and analysed model behaviour and reasoning when situated in the evaluation or asked questions about its scope. We find that, as in the original incident, Mythos 5 frequently reasons that the people it's targeting are simulated as part of the evaluation and provides invalid justifications for this conclusion. We also observe that Mythos 5 frequently attacks even in situations where, when directly asked, it states doing so would harm real people. While these issues are particularly prominent in Mythos 5, we find similar results in other models across providers. Our investigation suggests that models providing invalid justifications for their actions could make monitoring more difficult and complicate the analysis of misaligned behaviours observed in simulated environments, and we recommend model evaluators treat observations of undesired behaviour as important evidence of alignment failures even in the presence of simulation awareness in model reasoning.

</details>

Abstract: Autonomous agents increasingly execute consequential actions on behalf of users, but ordinary middleware guardrails do not provide cryptographic proof that a policy was actually enforced. Their verdicts leave counterparties dependent on the operator unless they repeat the checks themselves, potentially exposing sensitive policies and inputs. We present zkARc, a modular proof-carrying framework that wraps each stage of Automated Reasoning checks (ARc) in an independent, succinctly verifiable zero-knowledge proof. ARc is a neurosymbolic system that translates natural language policies into formal logic and verifies AI-generated content against them using SMT solvers. zkARc certifies the planner's output, the policy's formalization, the action's formalization and both solver queries. The receipt binds these computations to the exact action received and, under the stated assumptions, reveals no additional information about private policies and intermediate model values beyond the declared public information and verdict. This lets a third party check the claimed policy enforcement without re-running the pipeline or trusting the operator. We prove completeness, knowledge soundness and zero knowledge for the composed receipt under explicit assumptions. An implementation on Qwen2-0.5B enforces a spending policy end to end, approving two purchases and denying a third. A full receipt, which also proves model inference, takes about an hour to issue with CPU-only proving and verifies in under a minute, and the solver proof alone is 88 KiB and verifies in 0.3 seconds.

</details>

Abstract: Open-weight LLMs are routinely fine-tuned from public repositories, yet task-specific adaptation can silently erode safety alignment inherited from instruct-tuned bases. This is a supply-chain risk when organizations ship third-party checkpoints without full re-alignment. Prior work rarely audits diverse public fine-tunes under a shared multi-axis safety-utility protocol or offers training-free mitigation at deployment time. We propose Safety Direction Adapter (SDA), a training-free post-hoc safety patch that extracts a safety direction once from an aligned base and transfers it one-to-many within that base family as a small PEFT-compatible weight update, evaluated under the same multi-axis protocol. Across six direction extractors, we evaluate 14 checkpoints from two 1-2B lineages and probe 8B and 27B fleets (n=5 each) on 15 benchmarks (12538 prompts). On Llama, SDA improves the four-axis mean by about 2.3 points against a 4.2-point instruct-relative gap (about 55 percent recovery; partial Avg), with Safety and Cyber recovering toward or beyond instruct; Safety gains about 8.5 points against about 4.9 points mean degradation. On Qwen, degradation is larger and excess-driven; SDA still lifts Avg (about 36 percent of the gap) while trading Safety and Cyber against Excess and General. We report dual-use risks of publishing direction adapters and do not claim robustness to adaptive or white-box attacks.

</details>

Result: 在 150 项和 290 项 hold‑out 项目上两种模型差异无统计学意义；第四次迭代在 300 项拆分上部分模型安全率显著下降(12.9%→7.1%, p=0.035；10.2%→3.6%, p=0.036)，其余四模型无显著变化；另一方面，引发了两种模型的 agentic‑harm 标签上升，且启用有益条款反而加剧 agentic‑harm。

Conclusion: 文献没有证明在六个基准上整体安全性可显著提升，结果尚未显示出普遍有效性。

Abstract: We study a safety harness that improves itself without touching model weights. Enmo Safety wraps a chat model with a deterministic input assessment, trigger-matched safety guidance, spotlighting of untrusted content, and an output guard. A reflector model turns failures into one bounded update per round, and an update is kept only if it lowers harm without raising refusal of benign prompts on a held-out dev split. Every version is snapshotted and every decision ledgered. We evaluate a bare model, the seed harness and the evolved harness on six open benchmarks (HarmBench, AdvBench, JailbreakBench, XSTest, AgentHarm, deepset prompt-injections). On 150 items and on a 290-item holdout, none of the before/after differences on two models was statistically significant. A fourth iteration ran six policy models on a fresh 300-item split: the harness cut the unsafe rate significantly on two models (12.9% to 7.1%, p = 0.035; 10.2% to 3.6%, p = 0.036), not on the other four, and raised agentic-harm labels on two. Whether the rules fired was unrelated to outcome (Fisher p from 0.30 to 1.0), and the helpfulness clauses made agentic harm worse than no harness on obfuscated attacks. A reworded clause and an input-normalisation step form harness v22, at a measured cost in benign tool-task refusal. Each cell is one run, so these estimates do not establish a general safety improvement. Code, harness versions and ledger: https://github.com/rioryugen/enmo_safety

</details>

Abstract: Concept erasure, or concept unlearning, in text-to-image diffusion models is usually audited with prompt attacks. A prompt attack tests whether one conditioning string still retrieves the erased concept. It does not measure the probability that the frozen generator still assigns to that concept. We introduce least-action auditing of this residual probability, and evaluate it on Stable Diffusion concept-erasure checkpoints. The least-action barrier is the minimum control cost of driving the frozen reverse process into a detector-defined concept event. This cost equals the path-space KL divergence of conditioning the model on the event, and therefore the event's negative log-probability. The estimate $ΔS_{\mathrm{rob}}$ uses a CLIP$\wedge$DINOv2 consensus terminal, with no retraining and no adversarial prompt. Across $18$ erased concept-mechanism pairs spanning object classes and NSFW nudity, $ΔS_{\mathrm{rob}}$ predicts deployment leakage counted on $1{,}500$ fresh samples per model by detectors excluded from the audit (pooled Spearman $ρ{=}{-}0.76$, $p{=}5{\times}10^{-4}$). Within every audited concept, the eraser with the higher barrier leaks less ($18/18$). On NSFW nudity the barrier orders the standard erasers exactly: UCE $ΔS_{\mathrm{rob}}{=}3.9$ (leak $16.4\%$) $<$ ESD $75.5$ ($9.8\%$) $<$ RECE $224.5$ ($1.4\%$), while Safe Latent Diffusion (SLD) stays near zero and leaks $85.5\%$ under explicit prompts. The same trajectories repair deployed checkpoints. An audit-guided closed-form patch reduces UCE nudity leakage from $16.2\%$ to $0/1{,}500$ and RECE church leakage by $3.3\times$, where direction-count-matched random edits fail.

</details>

Abstract: Commutative operads have been proposed as a platform for public-key cryptography, with protocols for key agreement, key exchange, encryption and authentication, together with a masking technique and families of algebras with secret parameters intended to strengthen them. We show that breaking key agreement, key exchange and encryption reduces to two problems over the centralizer of the public operations: an interpolation problem for key agreement and a division problem for the other two. Neither problem requires recovering a secret, and the set from which secrets are drawn plays no role in these attacks. For the operad platforms in the two original papers (fields, the integers, residue rings, the tropical semiring with finite entries, convexors and the masked tropical algebras), the relevant problems can be solved in polynomial time in the input models specified here, and masking does not affect these attacks. The authentication protocol fails for a more elementary reason: as originally stated it does not bind the prover to any public key, and even with a registered public key the response expected by the verifier can be computed from public data. In the masked variant, verification requires the secret mask; on the concrete string platforms, one observed session with an invertible challenge entry suffices for forgery. We also observe that one of the auxiliary constructions satisfies the algebra axioms only in a degenerate case, although the corresponding protocol remains correct. The attacks are implemented and verified on random instances of representative platforms.

</details>

Abstract: Broken Access Control (BAC) has been the foremost category in the OWASP Top Ten since 2021, primarily due to the challenges in enforcing authorization at the application layer. Traditional mechanisms depend on logic embedded in the application, referencing relational database information such as permissions, roles, or ownership. However, missing or incorrect checks can go undetected by external systems, leaving applications vulnerable. This paper presents BACGUARD, a reverse proxy designed to enforce authorization for unmodified applications. BACGUARD reconstructs authorization relations by leveraging authenticated user data from session stores and object ownership from relational schemas. It evaluates whether users are permitted to access resources and blocks unauthorized requests in real time. Unlike existing approaches, BACGUARD bases its authorization on database joins, making the system resilient to forgery. For most requests, decisions are made deterministically. In cases where access control violations are only apparent in response data, such as cross-user data leakage, a language model is used as a fallback. Evaluations on six applications, including those with known vulnerabilities, demonstrate that BACGUARD's hybrid deterministic and language model-based approach blocks all tested attacks with a false-positive rate below 0.7%. Competing solutions like Web Application Firewalls are less effective and more disruptive. The results highlight BACGUARD's primary contribution: the external reconstruction of authorization facts, enabling robust enforcement even when the application itself is flawed.

</details>

Abstract: It is often desirable to generate and release synthetic graphs, which resemble real-world data, in a privacy-preserving way. While substantial progress has been made in designing private graph synthesizers (PGS), opportunities remain for more explicit connections between synthesizer models and privacy evaluation, and better accounting for output variation. This paper proposes GSynPEF, a practical framework for empirical evaluation of privacy and utility in settings for which a real-world graph is given as input and output synthetic graph(s) are observed. Our framework links a broad class of graph synthesizers (both statistical and deep-learning based) with privacy metrics, expanding evaluation dimensions to practical utility and risk, through empirical analysis of multiple actual outputs. The framework accommodates both an Exact Solution scenario, where synthesizer behavior is directly observable, and a Monte Carlo Approximation scenario where only synthetic outputs from a black box synthesizer are available. Extensive experiments on four real-world datasets and seven widely used graph synthesizers demonstrate framework's versatility, enabling privacy evaluation for different attack goals (e.g., to infer an edge or node) and capabilities (e.g., observing one or multiple synthetic graphs). We analyze evaluation results by examining the relationship between graph statistics and synthesizer design choices, providing insights for data curators in PGS selection and researchers for improved PGS design.

Abstract: Whether almost perfect nonlinear (APN) permutations exist in even dimension $n\ge 8$ is a long-standing open question, the big APN problem. The only example known in even dimension, on six bits, was found from a quadratic APN function that is not itself a permutation, by Carlet--Charpin--Zinoviev (CCZ) equivalence. It is therefore natural to test APN functions for CCZ-equivalence to a permutation. This amounts to finding two complementary $n$-dimensional subspaces among the zeros of the Walsh transform, which lie in $\mathbb{F}_2^{2n}$. A known necessary condition only uses their projections to $\mathbb{F}_2^n$ and cannot decide every case. We give an exact test for functions whose Walsh supports are affine, including all quadratic functions. It reconstructs the subspaces from their projections, through searches for subspaces of $\mathbb{F}_2^n$ and for constrained bilinear maps. On the benchmarked functions in dimension twelve, it lists them $39$ to $55$ times faster than the generic search of the commonly used sboxU library. We then study the functions $x^3+a^{-1}\mathrm{Tr}(a^3x^9)$, which Göloğlu and Pavlů proved not to be CCZ-equivalent to permutations when $4$ divides $n$. With their result, our computations show that this holds for every even $n\le 24$. In dimension $18$ the necessary condition is satisfied, and the new test decides the case. We conjecture this for every even dimension. We also compile $25$ classes of quadratic APN functions in dimension ten from published instances, with equivalence certificates. No function in these classes or in their switching closure is CCZ-equivalent to a permutation. All code and data are public.

</details>

Abstract: Information-flow control (IFC) is a promising approach to enforce policy in agentic AI systems. However, IFC suffers from label creep, a phenomenon where monotonically increasing security labels propagate through a system and progressively limit its utility. Label creep in agentic AI systems can be mitigated using two major strategies: (1) improved system design and (2) label declassification. In this paper, we characterize how far system design alone can mitigate label creep in agentic systems. Specifically, we measure how system topology, scale, and agent composition affect utility loss and security enforcement cost proxies for conservative IFC enforcement in a stateful agentic system simulator. We develop a pipeline to systematically vary these features and measure their impacts on label propagation. Our results demonstrate strong associations between structural graph features and our IFC utility loss metric (up to a Spearman correlation $|ρ|$ of $0.96$) and show that system size and tool allocation substantially affect how labels propagate. These findings identify design choices to reduce IFC utility loss, but they also demonstrate that system design alone cannot fully prevent utility loss due to monotonic label propagation. This motivates the development of label declassification mechanisms that aid these design choices and diminish IFC utility cost.

</details>

Abstract: We present QUASI, a newly conceived class of protocols that enable a group of parties to produce a publicly verifiable attestation about a pre-validated statement. Designed for quorum-based systems, QUASI enables parties to attest to external entities that the statement is valid. Surprisingly, each party need not sign the entire statement: authenticating only a small subset of symbols suffices for the quorum to attest to the whole. A simple deterministic construction illustrates this possibility. Built purely from hashing and error correction, QUASI achieves small per-party contributions and low attestation generation and verification costs, without relying on SNARKs for aggregation.
  Our main contribution is a randomized construction achieving reductions of more than $10\times$ in per-party contribution size and hash computations for generation and verification, compared with full hash-based signatures.
  QUASI is a generic primitive supporting diverse applications. Two principal use cases are external attestation for signature-free consensus, enabling clients to verify statements about committed state; and preprocessing for SNARK-based aggregation, compounding QUASI's savings with succinct-proof compression.

</details>

Abstract: Local-first security tools let organizations analyze code without sending it to third-party services, but the small language models they can run locally are weaker than hosted ones and are hard to improve without breaching the privacy guarantee that motivates them. We present Tantular Cyber, an open-source, read-only repository security analyzer that keeps deterministic scanner findings authoritative and confines a locally served language model to advisory explanations in Bahasa Indonesia. We report its measured v0.3.0 baseline on a synthetic, ground-truth benchmark: combining Semgrep with an interprocedural call-graph scanner yields precision 0.843 and recall 0.915 (Semgrep alone: 0.818 and 0.766). The advisory layer is the weak point: 57% of explanations met an evidence-grounding check, against 95% for a different 8B model under identical prompts. A forensic re-scoring of retained outputs shows that every grounding failure cites a code identifier absent from the evidence shown, and that a prompt change which appeared to raise grounding from 0.56 to 0.72 did so partly by citing nothing. We document two evaluation-validity hazards we encountered: silent model-file migration during a run and non-reproducible published figures. Building on these lessons and on constraint-aware evaluation from security operations research, we propose a governed self-improvement loop (code-free feedback, curated data, a cheapest-first intervention ladder from prompts to LoRA and weight-space edits, hard gates, and human release approval) and preregister the protocol by which its first cycle will be judged. We report no results for the loop itself.

Abstract: Autonomous vehicles can use information exchanged with other vehicles and roadside infrastructure to support driving decisions. Studying the resulting traffic effects requires following received information through application decisions, vehicle reactions, and subsequent interactions. We present an extensible closed-loop adversarial evaluation framework built on VaN3Twin, a platform for traffic and wireless network simulation, with a diverse attack library and a labelled dataset covering benign and attack conditions. A key advantage of the framework is that it can be extended with additional attacks, message paths, receiving applications, and logging fields. The added library contains 76 configurable attack profiles adapted to message formats defined by the European Telecommunications Standards Institute. These profiles are organized into nine groups of related attacks, referred to as suites. The framework connects live attack injection and simulated network delivery to receiving applications, whose selected commands update vehicle movement. The resulting state feeds subsequent message generation, closing the loop. The dataset links physical vehicle states and message exchanges to attack labels, application decisions, selected commands, and traffic outcomes. Evaluation with individual and combined attack suites confirmed execution of all profiles, and every suite produced requests linked to attacked messages. A recorded sequence also showed a vehicle reacting to an attacked message and subsequently influencing another vehicle through an unmodified message describing its updated movement. These findings demonstrate how live injection and vehicle feedback support the study of direct and indirect effects as traffic evolves.

Abstract: Large language model (LLM) agents increasingly improve themselves through self-evolution, distilling execution experience into persistent, reusable skills. However, this persistence creates a new attack surface: malicious rules implanted into skills can compromise subsequent agent behavior. Existing skill backdoor attacks typically assume direct manipulation of the victim's skills, which is impractical when skills are constructed internally by a trusted evolver. In this paper, we study a novel problem of task-level backdoor attacks, where the attacker can only inject poisoned evolution tasks, inducing the agent to implant backdoors into its own skills. Since the attacker controls neither skill induction nor downstream execution, a natural approach is to iteratively optimize poisoned tasks based on attack success. However, our preliminary analysis reveals two key challenges: (i) failures at different stages of skill evolution and downstream execution can produce indistinguishable outcomes, providing little guidance for targeted poisoned-task updates; and (ii) unsuccessful updates may undermine earlier progress, while simply discarding them may lead to repeated ineffective updates. To address these challenges, we propose BadEvolve, a failure-aware task-poisoning framework against self-evolving LLM agents. BadEvolve diagnoses attack failures across four stages, i.e., Induction, Activation, Following, and Effect, and directs updates toward the failing stage. It further employs rollback to preserve earlier progress and failure memory to avoid repeating ineffective updates. Extensive experiments across diverse self-evolution frameworks, benchmarks, and backbone LLMs demonstrate the effectiveness of BadEvolve. Our code is available at https://github.com/ventr1c/BadEvolve.

</details>

Abstract: The limited computational resources of Internet of Things (IoT) devices present challenges for secure image processing. This paper presents a distributed chaotic image encryption system using independent Wi-Fi-connected ESP32-S3 microcontrollers. A master-worker architecture distributes image blocks through master-coordinated chaotic sequence management, maintaining one logically continuous chaotic sequence by assigning block-specific starting states independently of worker assignments. The implemented system uses reversible chaotic substitution (SUBSTITUTE), while Elliptic Curve Digital Signature Algorithm (ECDSA)-signed discovery messages and Transport Layer Security (TLS) 1.3 with mutual authentication secure device communication. Experimental evaluation using one, two, and four workers demonstrates a reduction in execution time from 43.215 to 15.878 seconds, achieving a speed-up of 2.72 times. The encrypted image achieves a Shannon entropy of 7.999940 bits/byte and near-zero adjacent-pixel correlation. Exact image recovery is verified through matching Secure Hash Algorithm 256-bit (SHA-256) hashes and a Structural Similarity Index (SSIM) value of 1.0. Additional experiments demonstrate successful encryption and decryption using different worker counts and block sizes, automatic task reassignment following worker failure, and rejection of a mismatched private key during TLS authentication. These findings demonstrate the feasibility of secure, flexible, and distributed chaotic image processing across independent resource-constrained IoT devices.

</details>

Abstract: Modern cyber threats commonly unfold as multi-step attacks comprising temporally and causally related phases. Current defenses commonly follow a detect first and correlate later paradigm. Intrusion detection systems classify events independently, while downstream systems reconstruct attack progression from the resulting alerts or audit records. In this context, learning-based detectors learn attack-step dependencies implicitly as statistical regularities in the data and cannot guarantee that their predictions follow the real attack progression. Conversely, correlation systems represent attack relationships explicitly, but apply this knowledge only after the underlying attack observations have been detected. We present DEEPCHRONOS, a neuro-symbolic network intrusion detection system that shifts the paradigm from detect first and correlate later to detection through logical progression. DEEPCHRONOS combines learned flow-level evidence with explicit attack-phase rules, temporal prerequisites, and previously inferred phases, allowing the evolving attack history to inform and constrain each detection decision. Evaluations on DARPA 2000 and AIT-LDS V2 show that DEEPCHRONOS improves macro F1 over purely neural baselines by up to 11.3%, eliminates causal violations under strict temporal constraints, and maintains millisecond-scale inference latency. These results demonstrate that integrating explicit attack progression knowledge into detection produces more accurate and temporally coherent multi-step attack predictions.

</details>

Abstract: As enterprise systems grow in scale and complexity, manual log analysis and conventional SIEM tools struggle to keep pace with the volume and diversity of incidents. To address this challenge, this paper proposed a multi-agent AI framework supporting in analyzing the system log to trace the attack forensic in the system more effectively and efficiently. The framework is designed using n8n platform and Gemini large language model. The framework consists of 7 AI agent layers. Each agent work on a specific task. When the task is done, the agent will forward the result to the output-associated agent of the next layer. The multi agent model improves the analysis velocity, so it is able to support numerous log analysis requirement at the same time. We deployed 16 test cases to test operation of the multi agent workflow, and evaluate the accuracy per test case and the operative performance. The result findings are very promising.

</details>

Abstract: Vector search on untrusted infrastructure exposes the \emph{query embedding}, a faithful summary of the user's intent. Fully homomorphic encryption can hide the query, but existing approaches still require a full scan followed by ranking of encrypted scores. Scalable private systems avoid encrypted ranking by either sending scores from a single cluster to the client or moving the search to the client, which must first stream the entire vector index for preprocessing. We present \textsc{Carousel}, which instead scores the full corpus under encryption and compresses the results for client-side ranking. The server scores every vector under encryption, raises the scores to a power that preserves their order by magnitude while suppressing all but a handful, and compresses the resulting sparse vector into a logarithmic number of ciphertexts using homomorphic additions alone. Because every vector is scored, \textsc{Carousel} incurs no recall loss from discarding candidates. Since clients hold no database-dependent state, insertions and deletions take effect immediately at the cost of a single vector write. \textsc{Carousel} searches $1$ billion vectors of BIGANN SIFT1B across multiple GPUs and achieves recall@100 of $0.9715$. At $100$ million SIFT vectors, it achieves recall@100 of $0.9845$ with a query latency of $3.735$ seconds on a single GPU.

</details>

Abstract: Machine learning-based intrusion detection systems (IDSs) can improve IoT security but often rely on large feature sets that increase computational overhead and limit deployment on resource-constrained devices. This paper presents a resource-efficient and explainable feature optimization framework for lightweight IoT intrusion detection using the CICIoT2023 dataset. A Random Forest model was used to rank feature importance and identify compact feature subsets. Experimental results show that a three-feature model achieved an MCC of 0.9622 while reducing the feature space by 92.1%, whereas a six feature model achieved an MCC of 0.9675 with an 84.2% reduction. Furthermore, a fifteen-feature model achieved an MCC of 0.9744, preserving 99.97% of the performance of the full 38-feature baseline model while reducing the feature space by 60.5%. SHAP-based explainability analysis validated the selected features and provided interpretable insights into model decisions. The results demonstrate that lightweight, explainable, and high-performance IoT intrusion detection can be achieved using only a small subset of carefully selected features, significantly reducing resource requirements with minimal loss of detection effectiveness.

Abstract: Security fixes usually reach users as compiled updates, but deployment delays leave unpatched systems exposed. During this window, comparing patched and unpatched binaries can reveal how to exploit a vulnerability, turning a defensive update into actionable information for attackers. Existing benchmarks do not measure this capability: they expose source code, rely on human-crafted challenges, or evaluate binary comprehension without requiring exploitation. We introduce CyberBinGym, a benchmark containing 100 tasks derived from real vulnerabilities in 53 widely used projects, spanning six vulnerability families and three levels of analysis complexity. Agents receive stripped vulnerable and patched binaries without source code or vulnerability information, and must combine static and dynamic analysis to recover and exploit the vulnerability. Evaluation separately measures vulnerability recovery through differential crash testing and end-to-end exploitation through flag retrieval, with trajectory audits to reject reward hacking. The strongest evaluated agent recovers the target vulnerability in 88% of tasks and exploits it in 20%. Providing the patched binary raises its exploit rate from 10% to 25% on a matched 20-task subset; with standard binary hardening enabled, it still exploits two of its 20 previously successful tasks. These results demonstrate that current agents can turn released binary patches into working exploits in a controlled setting, although exploitation remains unreliable. These capabilities lower the barriers to targeting unpatched software and compress defenders' effective response window, increasing the urgency of timely patch deployment.

</details>

Abstract: Large Language Models (LLMs) are increasingly deployed as shared multi-tenant services, relying on advanced optimizations like KV cache and prefix sharing to improve throughput and reduce latency. However, these optimizations introduce a critical vulnerability: attackers can exploit time-to-first-token (TTFT) variations between KV cache hits and misses to infer sensitive prompt tokens via prefix-matching side-channel attacks. Existing defenses indiscriminately restrict cross-tenant cache sharing, which incurs prohibitive performance overhead and negates the fundamental efficiency benefits of the KV cache.
  We propose Mirage, a topology-aware emission obfuscation framework that provably bounds timing leakage without sacrificing the performance gains of cache sharing. Instead of stalling the GPU or forcing redundant recomputations, Mirage delays token emission at the gateway so the attacker observes a natural cache miss distribution, while the GPU seamlessly continues serving other requests at full speed. Mirage uses a Privacy Odometer that persistently tracks the topological novelty of prefix fan-out expansions to distinguish aggressive probing from benign traffic. Once a prefix exceeds a safety threshold, Mirage engages a two-phase policy that uses a dynamic estimator to pad the TTFT to a calibrated cache-miss baseline. On a 500-sample public corpus across three models, Mirage suppresses the timing side-channel to near the random-guessing floor, while maintaining a significantly lower TTFT than defense baselines. We further show that Mirage withstands a strong adaptive adversary and the same principle carries over to semantic caching layers.

</details>

Abstract: Agentic AI systems extend large language models with tools, memory, retrieval, browser or computer-use interfaces, code execution, external services, and multi-agent coordination. Although these capabilities enable agents to act beyond text generation and perform tasks, they also create attack paths through which adversaries can exploit untrusted content, deceptive metadata, poisoned state, compromised skills, mutable dependencies, or malicious code. Such attacks can manipulate planning and tool use, corrupt memory, abuse code-execution environments, and trigger harmful downstream effects.
  In this paper, we present a systematic review of malicious attacks targeting agentic AI systems. We screened 186 candidate sources and retained 98. Following independent coding and resolver adjudication, we identified 284 atomic attacks, 452 atomic safeguards, and 660 explicit attack--safeguard relationships. We organize attacks into eight causal-mechanism classes while retaining insertion points, activation conditions, abused capabilities, objectives, impacts, persistence, and evidence as orthogonal dimensions.
  Our analysis shows that tool, MCP, and action-layer attacks account for the largest share of atomic attacks, while the prompt-injection and action-hijacking class has the broadest paper coverage. We identify distinct class-specific activation patterns and separation between insertion and later activation. Safeguard coverage remains uneven: only 278 of 660 relationships are directly assessed, and strong coverage is uncommon. These findings support lifecycle-aware threat modeling, agent evaluation, tool governance, and defense-in-depth security for agentic AI systems.

Abstract: Cloud infrastructure threat modeling lacks empirical validation of AI-discovered threats. We present a three-phase methodology of multi-agent threat discovery, automated exploit synthesis, and non-destructive live validation, and apply it to three commercial foundation models on two cloud architectures, validating each threat natively on the cloud (AWS, GCP, or Azure) it was discovered against. Of 233 AI-discovered threats, 103 were confirmed and 34 refuted (75.2\% precision, 95\% CI [67.3, 81.7]); 44 were conditional (the named mechanism refuted but an equivalent impact shown through another path) and 52 indeterminate. Multi-model discovery helped through breadth, not agreement: the three models together found 2.2$\times$ the confirmed threats of the best single model at comparable precision, while threats reported by several models were confirmed no more often than single-model ones. False positives were not architecture-specific: they asserted access or system state the deployment did not have. Threats coded in advance as observable configuration resolved cleanly more often than multi-step behavioral threats (72.5\% vs.\ 51.6\%), which were more often conditional, suggesting that models more often misidentify the exploitation mechanism than the underlying weakness. AI-assisted discovery is a practical input to security workflows when paired with empirical validation.

Abstract: As post-quantum cryptography (PQC) standards evolve, developers must adapt implementations, tests, and protocols. Studying this work requires connecting code changes to problem reports and developer reviews. We present PQC-Corpus, a dataset of linked development histories from 179 public GitHub repositories, spanning cryptographic libraries and general-purpose projects. It connects issues and closing pull requests to commits and full patches, with comments and reviews. We combine Git histories with GitHub threads, reconstruct 23 pull-request commit lists truncated at 250 entries, and check file integrity, schemas, and links. We provide full and filtered views with removal ledgers, and quantify how filtering reduces links from issues to commits. A patch-free view supports inspection before downloading patches, while traversal scripts follow issues to code changes. We make the dataset and collector publicly available for studies of specification changes, hybrid interoperability, cryptographic review, and migration assistance.

Abstract: As a fundamental cryptographic primitive, secret sharing distributes a secret among multiple parties and allows certain authorized parties to recover the secret while preventing unauthorized parties from knowing the secret. However, local leakage from each party member allows an adversary to learn partial information from each share, which is not guaranteed by traditional security. Causal reference-set interventions show that changing reference composition can substantially alter family-level detection and that improving coverage of one attack family can reduce coverage of others.

Controlled-Vocabulary Error-Correcting Codes encode buyer-specific fingerprints through controlled lexical choices within the capsule. Across three domains, six models, and three agent frameworks, SkillCODER achieves 99.2% owner verification and 99.3% buyer Top-1 attribution accuracy while preserving task fidelity, substantially outperforming prompt-level baselines. Abstract: Security tools raise findings across cloud environments, and AI agents now investigate and prioritize them. A finding is still resolved by people: it has to reach the people responsible for fixing it and accountable for the resource it concerns. Ownership is the organizational context that decides who that is. Where recorded, it is declared by hand in configuration management databases or owner tags and goes stale unless kept current; accounts are shared, and automation creates much of the infrastructure. The knowledge sits in work records, code history, audit logs and identity directories, encoded in each organization's own conventions. Recovering it poses four challenges: the signal is scattered across sources, none of them authoritative; the conventions that carry it differ between organizations; reaching an owner takes multi-hop links; and those related to a resource differ in the strength of their relation. We define ownership as a set of entities, people or teams, each related to the resource by one of five patterns ordered by strength. We present a reference architecture for agentic ownership attribution that derives ownership from these records instead of declaring it: it profiles an organization offline into tested signal procedures and an ownership playbook, then attributes each resource with a coordinator agent and parallel investigators, with confidence computed by rules. On 100 resources of 50 Amazon Web Services (AWS) resource types in one organization, we pool the architecture's candidates with those of an unprimed general-purpose agent and verify every evidence piece against the records. The architecture reaches 92.1% weighted recall and 86.6% weighted precision, against 54.8% and 77.4%, at 0.55 times the agent's cost per unit of coverage. The difference lies in people who build and change resources without an explicit owner, found through work records and code history.

</details>

Abstract: CAPTCHAs can protect websites from malicious attacks. As a typical representative of behavior CAPTCHAs, slide puzzle CAPTCHAs have become increasingly popular in recent years due to their user-friendliness and high security. Previous attack methods on slide puzzle CAPTCHA mainly face challenges in the following four aspects. (1) The defense mechanisms of previous slide puzzle CAPTCHAs were relatively simple. The current slide puzzle CAPTCHAs have added complex interferences, detected slide trajectory features in the two-dimensional space, and added other defense mechanisms. (2) The previous notch detection methods were difficult to accurately locate the notch positions. (3) Previous attack trajectories were limited to the horizontal dimension, making them impossible to pass the existing detection. (4) The previous attack trajectories were generated by fitting functions, which were relatively simple and could not well simulate the slide characteristics of human users. In response to the above challenges, firstly, we systematically analyze the defense strategies of six most popular slide puzzle CAPTCHAs: Capy, Dingxiang, Geetest, Kyger, Shumei and Tencent. Secondly, for each type of CAPTCHA, we construct a notch detection dataset and a slide trajectory dataset separately. Thirdly, we use advanced YOLOv11 to detect the notch positions and train generative adversarial networks (GAN) to generate attack trajectories. As far as we know, we are the first to generate attack trajectories by directly learning human slide trajectory characteristic. According to the experimental results, this method cracked six most popular slide puzzle CAPTCHAs with success rates of over 90\%, and the attack time is only about 5 seconds, indicating that the security of existing slide puzzle CAPTCHAs is facing challenge. At the same time, we propose various methods to enhance the security of slide puzzle CAPTCHAs.

</details>

Abstract: Digital innovations have strongly improved ship capabilities, including command, control & communication, navigation and combat management. In the wake of digitisation and geopolitical stress, resilience against cyber-attacks has become a key challenge. Autonomous Cyber Resilience (ACR) methods, inspired by features of the human immune system, provide a promising perspective. Proofs of concept of autonomous self-healing and self-protecting software have been realised in cloud environments using containerisation, monitoring and deployment technology. However, developing a suitable "cyber immune system" for more complex digital systems is still an intricate challenge. In this paper, we share insights on how ACR methods can be combined with resilience mechanisms present in critical naval and aeronautical manoeuvring systems. Using TNO's "OpPic" application, we show how TNO's Self-Healing for Cyber Security software can provide autonomous mission- and threat-adaptive resilience. On this basis, six guidelines for the design of autonomous cyber resilient systems are derived. The ultimate goal is to enable operators to oversee and control their system's "cyber immune response", to be able to continuously adapt to evolving threats and mission priorities.

Abstract: Large Language Models (LLMs) present significant challenges to source tracing of generated code, as LLM rewriting can substantially alter the syntactic and structural characteristics of watermarked code. However, existing watermarking methods often suffer severe performance degradation under LLM-based functionality-preserving rewriting attacks. We attribute this vulnerability not merely to the intensity of rewriting, but to a fundamental distribution mismatch between the perturbations encountered during watermark training and the global, non-deterministic rewriting behaviors induced by LLMs. In order to deal with this challenge, we propose RoCoMark, a Robust Code waterMarking framework that explicitly learns and adapts to LLM rewriting behaviors. RoCoMark is a multi-round minimax game between the watermark embedder and LLM rewriter. In each round, the embedder first learns threat profiling from LLM-rewritten code by optimizing the watermark encoder through contrastive learning and knowledge distillation while mitigating catastrophic representation drift. Then the embedder improves strategies by optimizing the watermark encoder and extractor simultaneously. Finally, an adaptive attack-evolution mechanism based on Reflexion and short- and long-term memory pools is applied, enabling continuous interaction between the watermark embedder and evolving rewriting strategies. This iterative attacker-defender process progressively improves the robustness of the watermark against diverse and previously unseen LLM rewriting behaviors. Extensive experiments on four typical benchmarks, under attacks generated by five LLMs, show the effectiveness of RoCoMark. RoCoMark achieves an average detection AUROC of 74.2% and a Rewrite BitACC of 75.3%, while maintaining a code functionality pass rate above 99.7%.

</details>

Abstract: LLM-based coding agents are widely used to develop critical production software. Even though coding agents are known to occasionally write vulnerable code, there has not been any investigation into how code base vulnerabilities evolve over time under coding agent contributions. In this work, we close this gap by investigating whether and how vulnerabilities in existing code lead coding agents to introduce similar vulnerabilities in the code they write. We first evaluate coding agents on 96 tasks that require implementing a new backend application in a repository where other applications already exist. Second, we investigate repository-level code generation on 20 pairs of tasks in real C/C++ projects. In each task, the agent is asked to complete code critical to memory safety. We find that in both settings, insecure existing code leads to agents writing vulnerable code more often. In backend generation, insecure neighbors reduce the share of correct and secure implementations by 3.4% on average, and in C/C++ repository-level coding, the average gap between secure and insecure contexts reaches 35.4%. Prompting to follow existing code-style or to be careful about security increases, respectively decreases, how strongly agents propagate vulnerabilities, but neither stops them. When asked to explain their implementation choices, coding agents attribute them to following another code site in 76.5% of cases, even though they recognize the introduced vulnerability in 69.9% of cases. These findings show that agents use existing vulnerabilities as templates for new ones indiscriminatorily, turning each contribution into an opportunity to introduce more vulnerabilities. As such, as agents write a growing share of production code under decreasing human review, code bases can increasingly degrade in security if security vulnerabilities are not thoroughly fixed continuously.

</details>

Abstract: Swarm Learning improves resilience and removes the need for a single trusted coordinator by distributing model aggregation and validation across participating nodes. However, it also delegates the validation of model updates to peers whose private data may be biased, unrepresentative, or adversarial, and existing Swarm Learning protocols generally do not attest that data, leaving decentralized validation as an attack surface. We present a robust, privacy-preserving Swarm Learning framework that makes verifiable data quality the condition for admission to the learning task. A validator joins only after a zero-knowledge attestation proves, without revealing any raw sample, that a reference model ran correctly on its private data. The attested outputs also yield a label-free class profile, so the network assembles a collectively IID validator pool from individually skewed validators as accurately as an oracle with the true labels (total-variation distance to uniform 0.025, against 0.021 for the oracle and 0.21 for random selection). Admitted validators score client updates and, through a Tendermint-style Byzantine Fault Tolerant consensus, agree on a gradient mask and a quality-derived aggregation weight for each submission, replacing dataset-size weighting with a quality-aware signal. Adapting the cut-layer idea of Split Learning, a challenge-based audit lets peers re-verify that a mask was computed on the attested data without an expensive end-to-end zero-knowledge proof. Across four benchmarks under non-IID partitions the framework matches FedAvg on clean data; under a gradient-scaling attack that collapses FedAvg to random chance, it retains 94% of clean accuracy on CIFAR-100 and over 99% on FashionMNIST, where FedAvg achieves only 2.1% and 11%. The result is a private, robust, and auditable foundation for decentralized learning.

</details>

We propose an approach that reduces the overhead of state-of-art defenses by over 95%, by applying defenses only when attacks are detected. EVAX significantly increases sensitivity to detect and classify attacks in time for mitigation to be deployed with low false positives (4 FPs in every 1M instructions in our experiments). Such performance enables efficient and timely mitigations, enabling the processor to automatically switch between performance and security as needed.

Abstract: An LLM agent that reads untrusted content, such as an email or a web page, may follow instructions hidden in it. Noninterference is too strong for such agents, since a useful agent lets untrusted content influence what it does and may disclose the user's data by design, and declassification needs a release policy written for each task. We instead state security relative to a reference agent, which runs the same plan under the same runtime monitor with a model that never obeys untrusted content. An agent is relatively secure if every leak it has is a leak the reference already has.
  Our framework, PlanTERS, instantiates relative security, a notion from speculative execution, with a small agent language in which the attacker chooses how the model follows instructions, a composition with runtime monitors, and attacker models that differ in what they observe. A fence theorem shows that revoking injectable tools at the first untrusted read, and never granting them back, makes every plan relatively secure at the event level on finite runs, for every model behaviour in which each injection is a single call and the model obeys on a schedule fixed in advance; with a fairness condition, this extends to possibly infinite runs. Abstract: We study interactive proofs for arithmetic circuits over the complex numbers $\mathbb{C}$ in which both the prover and the verifier compute approximately (unlike muggles, wizards can use magic, i.e., floating-point numbers). The goal is to prove that a claimed output $y$ is close to the exact output $C(x)$: for thresholds $0<Δ_0<Δ$, the honest prover is accepted whenever $|y-C(x)|\leΔ_0$, and every prover is rejected with high probability whenever $|y-C(x)|>Δ$.
  Existing proof systems for real-valued data verify rounded finite-field computations. Bitan et al.~[EUROCRYPT~2026] gave an approximate sum-check protocol. We extend the protocol of Goldwasser, Kalai, and Rothblum (GKR) [STOC~2008] to public-coin interactive proofs for logspace-uniform layered circuits over $\mathbb{C}$ of size $S$, depth $L$, and $n$ inputs, under public bounds on gate values and wiring coefficients. At constant soundness error, the verifier runs in time $(n+1)\mathrm{poly}(L,\log S,b)$, the communication is $\mathrm{poly}(L,\log S,b)$, and the prover runs in time $\mathrm{poly}(S,b)$, where $b$ is the bit precision. To the best of our knowledge, these are the first doubly efficient interactive proofs in which both parties compute approximately. GKR reduces two claims about a layer to one on a random line, which leaves the unit circle, where our error bounds hold. We instead label gates by complex roots of unity and combine the two claims with a random root of unity as coefficient. We choose the error thresholds backward from the input layer, so that a large output error survives the verifier's rounding at every step. The honest prover needs only $O(S \log S)$ complex operations. We also obtain zero-knowledge proofs from one-way functions, one-round arguments from private information retrieval, and interactive PCPs whose proof length is polynomial in the witness length.

Abstract: Fine-tuning public pretrained language models with lightweight adapters such as LoRA has become the mainstream way to adapt them to downstream tasks. A service provider often trains its adapter on a data owner's samples, so two assets need protection: the data owner's samples and the provider's adapter. Existing secure fine-tuning protocols protect the samples but reveal to the data owner intermediate results of every training step, such as the hidden representations or logits of its inputs. We show that a single step suffices to copy the adapter: from the hidden representations of one mini-batch of 32 samples, the data owner fits an adapter that agrees with the provider's on 99.2% of test inputs, and logits alone yield about 94%. Protecting both assets therefore requires that no value that depends on the adapter be revealed throughout training. We present VeilTune, to the best of our knowledge the first protocol that fine-tunes a pretrained language model with backpropagation entirely in secure multi-party computation (MPC). VeilTune computes only what the loss depends on, replaces generic MPC operators with training-aware nonlinear operators that stay accurate on the values that training produces, and obliviously skips the steps corrupted by truncation failures. On four few-shot tasks with RoBERTa-large, VeilTune nearly matches plaintext training and is 5.3 points more accurate on average than SecP-Tuning, the state-of-the-art secure fine-tuning protocol. On SST-2, it achieves a 3.5x end-to-end speedup and a 3.6x reduction in communication over SecP-Tuning, and 26x and 17x over full fine-tuning in MPC.

Abstract: We present new information-theoretic protocols for $s$-server, doubly-efficient private information retrieval (PIR) in sublinear server time and one extra bit of space on the servers, for every constant $s \geq 2$. On an $n$-bit database, each of the $s$ PIR servers first builds and stores a preprocessing data structure consisting of $n+1$ bits (which is unique to each server, and after which the server may discard the original database). Then, each server answers a PIR query in time $T_s(n)$, by reading at most $T_s$ bits of its data structure and sending them to the user.
  In the two server setting ($s=2$), our PIR protocols achieve $T_2 = n^{0.82}$, matching the server time of the state-of-the-art schemes of Henzinger and Ragavan (EUROCRYPT 2026) while shrinking their storage from $O(n\sqrt{\log n})$ to $n+1$ bits. With $s \geq 3$ servers, our PIR protocols retain the $n+1$ bits of storage and additionally improve on the server time of all prior information-theoretic $s$-server schemes with $n^{1+o(1)}$ storage: for example, achieving $T_3 = n^{0.74}$ and $T_{10} = n^{0.61}$ (previously $n^{0.79}$ and $n^{0.75}$). Asymptotically, as the server count $s$ grows, we shrink the number of servers required to achieve a given server-work exponent from $s^{3.9}$ to $s$.
  Our results draw inspiration from matching-vector techniques (Efremenko, STOC 2009), which we generalize to handle degree-two computations in the exponent. Using this generalization, we build doubly-efficient PIR from the new intermediate abstraction of Fourier-sparse cone-delta functions. Then, we construct such Fourier-sparse cone-delta functions via a connection to modular forms and complex analysis: if a Fourier-sparse cone-delta function does not exist, then there exists a modular form which has too many roots, contradicting the valence formula.

Statistical analysis further shows that both harness and backend model significantly affect security outcomes, indicating that deployed-agent security is jointly shaped by the harness and backend model (RQ2). Finally, we localize where defensive behavior emerges across workflow stages and identify more secure design mechanisms, providing actionable guidance for building more robust web agents (RQ3). These findings highlight the web-agent harness as an important security boundary and motivate moving beyond model-centric defenses toward harness-level security design.

Abstract: LLM agents increasingly retrieve third-party skills from public registries and follow their procedures to complete user tasks. This reliance allows attackers to manipulate agent behavior by poisoning the skills. However, overtly malicious instructions can provide explicit signals for detection. We present StealthyPoison, a stealthy procedural poisoning attack that corrupts a skill's decision-critical rules to induce incorrect task outcomes while preserving its advertised functionality and normal execution. Unlike prior knowledge corruption attacks that craft poisoned content for known target question-answer pairs, StealthyPoison constructs reusable poisoned procedures from a publicly available third-party skill, without access to actual user requests or their ground-truth answers. To make the poisoned procedure generalize to unseen requests, it first synthesizes requests from the target skill and executes its clean version using a surrogate agent to obtain pseudo-references. These references and surrogate execution feedback guide the iterative refinement of payloads that alter decision-critical rules. To increase the likelihood that the poisoned skill is retrieved and selected over benign alternatives, the attack augments the poisoned skill's retrieval metadata with independently generated request descriptions. We evaluate StealthyPoison on 566 eligible target skills and 5,244 requests against more than 26,000 registry entries. It improves top-1 poisoned-skill placement by 13.6% relative to both HotFlip and GCG. Constructed using feedback from a single surrogate model, the procedural poisoning payloads transfer effectively and without modification to three held-out victim models and achieve end-to-end attack success in a Claude Code workflow powered by Opus 4.8. The defense evaluation further shows that most of these payloads evade detection by both SkillSpector and GPT-5.4-mini.

Abstract: Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) systems improve Large Language Model (LLM) responses by incorporating information from external knowledge bases. However, recent studies have shown that attackers can extract substantial portions of private knowledge bases from RAG systems through repeated querying. Yet, state-of-the-art extraction attacks either rely heavily on external LLMs to generate adversarial queries or require a large number of queries. In this paper, we propose SNAPRAG, a novel and cost-efficient attack for extracting RAG knowledge bases through adversarial perturbations. Our approach algorithmically generates adversarial queries without relying on external LLMs and allows for a strictly configurable query budget. In particular, SNAPRAG uses a discrete token-mutation algorithm to generate novel queries in a strict black-box setting. Our adaptive dissimilarity threshold calibration systematically forces queries into unexplored semantic regions of the target database, enabling extraction with fewer queries. Specifically, to extract half of the target knowledge base, our method requires up to 6.77$\times$ fewer queries than existing approaches. Furthermore, within a fixed query budget, our method recovers up to 96.9%, outperforming the closest approach by 7.7-19.9%. Abstract: Bridge hacks have cost billions, often without exploiting a code bug. In the cross-chain bridge hacks we catalogue from 2021 to 2026, failures of security posture, meaning how a bridge's people, code, controls and off-chain infrastructure are set up, account for 88% of reported losses since 2023, and nearly all repeat earlier failures. Code audits cannot catch these failures, so we propose a bridge security assessment rubric. It scores each bridge deployment from on-chain evidence, and a hard cap drops the score to the lowest value when a fatal risk is present. A token is only as safe as its weakest deployment if all its deployments share one backing. For attacks nobody anticipated, we show that rate limits and an emergency stop still bound the loss. We build an analyzer that applies the rubric to deployed bridges and monitors them for changes, and an agentic analyzer that runs the same checks on any bridge. Operators learn what to fix, and users can compare bridges. Our rubric's controls cut bridge hack losses 105-fold, from \$3.52B to \$33M.

Abstract: Ethical decision-making is central to safe cybersecurity research. Recent studies, one of which is our own, analyzing the source files of arXiv papers bring this challenge to light - arXiv publicly distributes the source files, to the surprise of many authors. Unique aspects reveal ethical challenges and dilemmas that extend beyond established ethical frameworks, institutional processes, and community best practices. In this work, we examine this case and provide insights for similar research on other public live resources, such as Zenodo or the Wayback Machine. While such empirical studies are particularly realistic, they must account for personally identifiable information, leaked secrets, and other sensitive details that could expose, shame, or otherwise negatively affect individuals, possibly leading to both reputational and practical harm. sDrawing on our collective experience and discussions among researchers, affected authors, and readers, we make three contributions: First, we define the ethical setting and highlight that even intentionally-public data with deliberate real-name attribution can raise ethical concerns when repurposed for empirical analysis. Second, we discuss the ethical issues surrounding the use of arXiv data and derive four higher-level dilemmas that researchers may face when working with similar data. Third, we derive practical guidance for ethically navigating such settings and, in doing so, distill four lessons for research on arXiv and similar public, live datasets: public availability should serve as the starting point of ethical reasoning; research should minimize the amplification it induces on exposed information; disclosure should be calibrated to the harm, confidence, and actionability of a finding; and effective immutability must be accounted for. Together, these contribute to community resources for ethically-sound cybersecurity experiments.

</details>

Abstract: We study the fingerprinting of agentic cyberattacks. Our objective is to reliably infer information about the attacker's configuration, such as LLM version and harness, from telemetry. To achieve this, we introduce a technique we call shibboleth traps: modular honeypot services designed to elicit discriminative behaviors from agentic attackers. The traps make informative interactions a condition for obtaining resources the agent seeks, eliciting model declarations and characteristic writing without requiring a successful prompt injection.
  To achieve harness fingerprinting, we introduce general mechanisms to indirectly probe peculiar execution behaviors of harnesses. These probes reveal how a harness masks secrets in tool output, enforces command time limits, and schedules tool calls, using only the effects visible in subsequent requests.
  We show that combined declarative and behavioral inference distinguishes among 15 candidate models with an average 90% top-1 accuracy, with behavioral classifiers evaluated on held-out harnesses or task families. In a separate experiment across four harnesses, fixed probe rules identify the correct harness with 92% accuracy. Abstract: Can power-analysis techniques reveal secret weights of machine-learning models? Multiple recent works demonstrate proof-of-concept attacks that can partially recover such weights. To that end, these works build on techniques from cryptographic engineering, treating the model as a ``cipher'' and its secret weights as keys.
  We question the basic assumption behind such straightforward adaptations of cryptographic attacks and highlight the challenges arising when applying past techniques to fully recover all layers of deep neural networks. Unlike past works, which extract only a few weights, typically from shallow layers, we investigate the recovery of complete networks. Moreover, we target fully trained and functional models, rather than untrained, randomly initialized networks. To focus on fundamental challenges, we assume an unrealistically strong side-channel adversary that can directly observe the output of the leakage model without any added noise. With this adversary, we show that machine-learning models differ significantly from cryptographic implementations, giving rise to unique challenges that have not been observed in the prior art.
  We observe two main differences between machine-learning models and ciphers. First, in contrast to the high-diffusion nonlinear integer arithmetic prevalent in cryptographic schemes, machine-learning models tend to use limited-precision floating-point arithmetic and low-diffusion linear operations. We show that these operations introduce difficulties in weight recovery, often requiring an unexpectedly large number of traces, even under ideal channel conditions. Second, we observe that, unlike ciphers, which aim to increase the data entropy, machine-learning models aim to reduce it. This reduces data variability as the algorithm progresses, hampering weight recovery in deep layers of the model.
  (details can be found in the paper)

Abstract: Access-control policies are written in natural language but enforced as formal models, and translating them into NIST Next Generation Access Control (NGAC) graphs remains unsolved: prior systems cover only subsets of the NGAC model, and none verify the graphs they extract. We present Text2NGAC, a pipeline of cooperating large language models that disambiguates each sentence before extraction, escalates uncertain sentences to a multi-model ensemble, and checks the resulting graph with a five-strategy verifier whose own effectiveness is measured by controlled mutation testing. To our knowledge it is the first system to extract all four NGAC relationship types - associations, prohibitions, event-response obligations, and assignments - from real-world policy documents. Three results anchor the evaluation. The verifier detects 99.7% of 918 injected mutants and every structural fail-open fault, and the fault classes it misses are characterized and weighted by security consequence rather than left unexamined. The extracted graphs are enforceable: one corpus's graph loads unmodified into the NIST reference implementation, which reproduces all 55 access decisions the graph entails and fires an extracted obligation end-to-end. Extraction reaches 97.3-99.6% exact-tuple-match accuracy on the 714 scored sentences of four real corpora, far above three reproduced baselines on matched metrics, and paraphrase probes with semantic re-scoring bound the pipeline's sensitivity to rewording. Reported accuracy is wording- and benchmark-specific, adversarial input is outside the verifier's guarantees, and the intended deployment is extraction plus structural verification feeding expert review. A complete two-tier reproducibility artifact accompanies the paper.

Abstract: AI safety evaluations often involve sensitive prompts and proprietary models. Evaluators want to keep their evaluation prompts secret from model owners to prevent purposeful or incidental data leakage, ensuring longer usefulness of their benchmark. Model owners in turn wish to keep their proprietary model weights and inference code within their own secure infrastructure to protect their IP. We present a way forward for "double-blind evals", which provide cryptographic guarantees that an evaluator's prompts are not shared with the model owner, while simultaneously maintaining the confidentiality of the model weights and inference code. To accomplish this, we use secure enclaves that encrypt data in RAM at the hardware level. In a world-first pilot, we test this approach by evaluating a proprietary Google DeepMind model (Gemini 2.5 Flash Lite) against a selection of the private AILuminate benchmark corpus, using an NVIDIA H100 secure enclave on Google Cloud Platform, and using OpenMined's PySyft to maintain data and model privacy. We believe this effort marks the first use of this technology to provide mutual secrecy for one organization's frontier-class proprietary model weights and another organization's closed benchmark.

Abstract: MCU-based embedded and cyber-physical systems increasingly require protection policies to change at runtime as resources, software, and system composition evolve. Yet widely used COTS MCU protection mechanisms are still largely designed around statically established policies. While they can restrict which domains modify protection primitives, they generally do not constrain what policy an authorized domain may create. As a result, domains may expand protection rules beyond their intended scope or interfere with boundaries established by other domains. We identify bounded configuration authority as a missing primitive for secure dynamic policy evolution and introduce an ownership-aware model in which explicit ownership and rule containment bound both configuration authority and recursive delegation. Changes to active protection-rule ranges are validated before commit, preserving protection boundaries without extending the normal access-checking datapath. We implement the model in an open-source RISC-V World Checker integrated into a CVA6-based SoC. Our FPGA evaluation shows no degradation in access latency or operating frequency, with 7.1% LUT and 1.2% FF overhead for eight rules. In an OTA update case study, direct reconfiguration under delegated authority reduces policy-transition latency by 89.5% over hypervisor mediation and 94.6% over trusted firmware mediation, while preserving established authority boundaries. All artifacts will be open-sourced to support the evolution of RISC-V specifications and system-level protection designs.

</details>
