Abstract: Biometric identity systems for decentralized applications need credentials that resist repeated enrollment and support private, publicly verifiable authentication. Capture and large-scale private deduplication run off chain while smart contracts authorize issuance and use; these stages span distinct trust domains, and a correct verdict covers only the checked inputs and gallery snapshot. Without binding and state checks, concurrent enrollments can issue duplicate credentials from correct verdicts, while substituted inputs can authorize unrelated credentials. We present BioBind, a protocol whose four backend interface conditions bind the deduplication query, gallery insertion and issued commitment to one captured vector, authenticate gallery transitions, and require a sound uniqueness verdict valid at finalization. Its encrypted instance uses bound requests, stale-session rejection and committee-confirmed migration. Capture-bound zero-knowledge authentication links credential use to the enrolled template and a fresh device-originated probe bound to the challenge. We give a conditional multi-session composition argument under explicit capture, backend and cryptographic assumptions. A prototype using LFW face features rejects the tested composition attacks; its unlinkable authentication circuit uses 31,445 constraints and about 305,000 gas. An equal-public-field baseline quantifies the cost of verifiable matching: witness-plus-proving medians are 1.989 s versus 1.119 s with device-trusted matching. Separate tests of encrypted enrollment complete three histories of 16,384 registrations without decoding errors at the tested checkpoints. Biometric accuracy bounds person-level uniqueness: on the Bosphorus database, cooperative subjects who change appearance evade deduplication in 1.8% of re-enrollment attempts at the operating threshold. Full backend integration remains open.

Abstract: In this work, we investigate Maurer-inspired two-way physical-layer authentication. Alice seeks authentication by Bob in the presence of a multi-antenna impersonator, Trudy. Trudy may replace the challenge to Alice, the response to Bob, or both. To create an information advantage, Bob adds artificial noise (AN) to his challenge, and Alice adds spatial AN while forwarding the received mixture. Bob predicts the response from trusted preparation, whereas Trudy observes only a noisy version of the challenge, and Alice's AN interferes with Trudy's preparation reception. Impersonation thus requires matching Bob's fresh randomness beyond the channel consistency checked by public-pilot one-way authentication. For quasi-static Gaussian multiple-input multiple-output channels with time-division duplex (TDD) reciprocity, we derive missed-detection bounds for attacks that replace the authentication challenge, the response, or both. These bounds assume attack-free initial training and preparation and account for estimation error and AN leakage. With equal antenna numbers at all nodes, we show scenarios where the proposed scheme has lower worst-case missed detection than specified one-way tests with/without AN.

Abstract: Authorization of distributed energy resource (DER) commands judges a command by who sent it and whether its value has been seen before. An attacker holding a legitimate credential who writes ordinary values passes both tests. We add a check that judges each DER write against the grid's operating context: the bus voltages around the target, the hour, the day- ahead price, system load, and irradiance, compared with what operators wrote under similar conditions in the feeder's own benign history. We evaluate it live on the IEEE 123-bus and 8500-node feeders, driven by a measured 2024 year of price, load, and irradiance, against nine attack scenarios. A lookup trained on the same history catches none of the writes of the four scenarios that send values already familiar on the targeted device; the context check refuses 91.7% of insider-abuse writes and 64.4% of DER-aggregation writes while denying 1.28% of benign writes. In an offline test that replays recorded moments with curtailment setpoints drawn from the benign range, issued at a positive price, it refuses 47.3% and 50.5% on the two feeders, against 18.5% and 16.0% for the same check without context; live, the few setpoints tested were mostly shallow and were mostly allowed. The context check complements the lookup.

Abstract: This paper introduces TUTTI, a new CKKS bootstrapping procedure that outperforms previous low-latency solutions in multi-threaded latency for any number of messages and, most notably, has a lower latency than TFHE when bootstrapping a single one. Our bootstrapping builds upon SPRU (CK, TCHES 2027), improving it with new methods for exploiting parallelism and increasing the number of messages, as well as with a refined security analysis, of independent interest, of the block-binary secret keys of LMSS (AsiaCCS 2023). TUTTI bootstraps 4096 real-number messages in 46 to 83.4 ms on 192 threads to restore 1 to 9 multiplicative levels, outperforming SHIP (CHKS, Eurocrypt 2025) by a factor of up to 3 on the same machine. Unlike SHIP, TUTTI gains immediately from bootstrapping fewer messages, with a 256-message bootstrapping taking just 15 to 34 ms on 32 threads. For a single message, TUTTI takes 4.6 ms on 32 threads, whereas TFHE-rs needs 7.3 ms for a 4-bit bootstrapping on the same machine and thread count. We show that this advantage persists across different architectures: on an Apple M5 Pro laptop (ARM), TUTTI takes 2.4 ms on 14 threads, against 4.5 ms for TFHE-rs. Differences between CKKS and lightweight schemes such as TFHE remain on several fronts, from functionality to key sizes and security assumptions, but our work shows that multi-threaded latency is no longer one of them, closing a long-standing gap in the practical FHE literature.

Abstract: Immersive computing is poised to become a major paradigm for future human-computer interaction (HCI), enabling spatial and embodied experiences across applications such as education, healthcare, manufacturing, emergency response, and scientific discovery. However, today's Internet and computing infrastructure were not designed for real-time, multi-user, sensor-rich, and privacy-sensitive immersive applications. They require coordination across sensing, representation, compression, networking, edge/cloud computing, rendering, security/privacy, and human experience. This report summarizes key findings from the NSF Workshop on Networking and Systems Challenges in Immersive Computing. The workshop brought together researchers from networking, systems, extended reality (XR), artificial intelligence (AI), graphics, HCI, security, privacy, and applications to identify challenges and opportunities. We conclude that immersive computing cannot be enabled by isolated advances in network bandwidth, device capabilities, AI models, or rendering alone. The field needs end-to-end architectures that adapt to user behavior, application requirements, resource constraints, and social expectations. We organize the agenda into six themes: networked systems for immersive computing; real-time edge-cloud orchestration for XR applications; AI for immersive experiences; privacy, security, and trust in XR systems; human-centered design and user experience in XR; and scalable infrastructure, testbeds, and open ecosystems for XR. We further identify key research questions, future trends, risks, ten-year success metrics, and action items for academia, industry, government, and research community. The report calls for coordinated investment in open platforms, reproducible benchmarks, privacy-preserving systems, and human-centered evaluation to build trustworthy, scalable, and broadly impactful infrastructure.

</details>

Abstract: Integrated sensing and communication (ISAC) can turn radio measurements into networked sensing services, but accurate position estimates alone do not ensure useful tracking. Missed targets, late acquisition, track interruptions and aging observations also shape the information available to an application. This article examines tracking of noncooperative unmanned aerial vehicles (UAVs) from a Third Generation Partnership Project (3GPP) perspective. It connects sensing-service requirements and network architecture to three information interfaces: receiver search regions, measurement context and track validity. A five-UAV case study in a 3GPP urban scenario and a ray-traced San Francisco scene illustrates the tradeoff between target coverage and extra outputs, the distinction between acquisition and ongoing continuity, and the value of testing a static mission envelope before adding adaptation. An observation-age example explains how a state predicted to the present can still rely on old evidence. The resulting lessons are to align receiver search with requested coverage, preserve measurement time and uncertainty, and assess coverage, ambiguity and timeliness together. We conclude by pointing out fruitful avenues for further work in sensing design, calibration and service validation.

Abstract: Governments around the world are increasingly using internet shutdowns to block digital communication. Internet shutdowns are particularly dangerous during political protests, as they inhibit protesters' ability to organize and communicate critical, potentially life-saving information and may be used to prevent awareness of state-based violence. Recent prior works have explored secure, private messaging systems for use during political protests and internet shutdowns but have encountered long delivery delays or low delivery rates under challenging conditions.
  In this work, we explore the use of distributed, adaptive, social- and congestion-aware heuristics and efficient communication patterns to create an intelligent ad hoc routing protocol in which network agents learn and leverage the network social environment and monitor and respond to dynamic network congestion behaviour to route messages in a time- and resource-efficient manner.
  Through this exploration, we contribute an intelligent algorithm and protocol which overall achieves comparable or higher delivery rates relative to the leading dynamic clique routing protocol which it extends while also transmitting messages through notably fewer carriers and, when network devices have larger amounts of buffer storage, transmitting fewer message-related packets. We additionally contribute a further understanding of routing limitations and challenges and conceptualizations of extensions of intelligent dynamic clique routing to overcome them.

Abstract: Image-based remote monitoring over wireless networks faces a trade-off between communication cost and classification reliability. Transmitting complete images is communication-intensive, whereas relying solely on lightweight edge classifiers may compromise accuracy. To address this challenge, we propose Confidence-Driven Adaptation (CDA) for image transmission and classification. An edge device (ED) captures images and applies a lightweight classifier before transmission to a remote server. Based on its classification confidence, the ED selects among three transmission modes with varying levels of information: a short text string reporting its predicted class when confidence is high, a compressed Region of Interest (ROI) at intermediate confidence levels, or an uncompressed ROI when confidence is low. The server accepts the predicted class directly in the first case and performs classification using a more powerful classifier in the other two cases. Numerical evaluations under bandwidth-limited, lossy links and latency-constrained settings demonstrate that CDA achieves high task accuracy at the receiver compared with non-adaptive baselines that transmit only the edge-classifier outcome or the uncompressed ROI.

</details>

This paper presents an investigation of the Long Range (LoRa) LPWAN technology for FL-based structural health monitoring (SHM), a representative remote IoT application. We also examine strategies for allocating the LoRa spreading factor (SF) during model-update exchange. Numerical results show that, with appropriate SF allocation, LoRa-supported FL-SHM can achieve competitive learning accuracy. In particular, jointly accounting for link distances and clients' dataset sizes when selecting the SF provides a favorable balance between convergence performance and communication latency. These preliminary findings offer useful insights into the design of FL systems over LPWANs for remote IoT deployments.

Abstract: The Dragonfly topology is widely deployed in leading supercomputers, where adaptive routing is crucial for high-performance interconnection. Despite years of research, a dilemma remains: state-of-the-art adaptive routing algorithms achieve excellent performance but demand hardware budgets far beyond what real router chips can afford -- prohibitive buffers, logically dependent floating-point operations, or exhaustive path-set traversals -- whereas realizable algorithms deliver unsatisfactory performance. Furthermore, existing algorithms fail to exploit the abundant almost-minimal paths in Dragonfly: when only the local channels of a minimal path are congested, they reroute packets to significantly longer global non-minimal paths, wasting global bandwidth and increasing latency. To address both limitations, we analyze intra-group path contention and propose the Decoupled Incremental Adaptive Routing (DIAR) algorithm, which decouples routing into global path selection at the source and incremental local detours onto almost-minimal paths, guaranteeing deadlock freedom without additional virtual channels, escape rings, or turn models. Requiring only a few comparators, shifters, adders, and multiplexers, DIAR reduces buffer overhead by up to four orders of magnitude compared with state-of-the-art algorithms, while achieving comparable or superior performance across all evaluated scenarios: relative to the strongest realizable adaptive routing algorithm in each scenario, DIAR improves saturation throughput by up to 30.0% across synthetic traffic patterns and improves performance by up to 34.9% (13.2% on average) across HPC workloads.

</details>

Abstract: In managed multi-tenant clouds, RDMA's lack of control-plane (CP) resource isolation lets a noisy tenant exhaust shared NIC microarchitectural caches, degrading victim throughput by up to 96.4\%. Data-path schedulers leave CP resources unprotected, and hardware approaches require FPGAs or SmartNICs rather than the commodity RNICs operators already deploy.
  We present MTRDMA, a lightweight user-space CP isolation mechanism for commodity RNICs, applicable to managed runtimes such as containers and sandboxed processes. MTRDMA intercepts library-level CP operations to enforce hierarchical quotas while preserving the zero-copy data path, using atomic shared-memory quota state with per-process cached admission (${\sim}$0.13\,$μ$s), a hybrid garbage collector that reclaims orphaned quota within seconds, and rdma-cgroup caps with ledger--kernel reconciliation to bound and detect residual tampering. On ConnectX-6 RNICs, MTRDMA cuts victim degradation under Queue Pair (QP) cache exhaustion from 94.5\% to 0.08\%, extends protection to Memory Region (MR) cache exhaustion, and scales to 256 concurrent tenants with negligible data-plane overhead.

Abstract: Classical networks treat every bit as equally important, which wastes time and energy in factories and requires extra traffic engineering for critical traffic. Semantic communications send only what a task needs, but how can they be tested under realistic conditions? This paper examines what can be tested today in 6G industrial IoT, that is, networks of factory machines, robots, and sensors, and what is still missing. It introduces semantic importance, a score attached to each message that states how much the message matters, and uses it in a context-driven design to derive eight testing needs. Existing simulators, emulators, testbeds, codecs, and learning frameworks are assessed against these needs, which reveals five gaps. We propose a toolchain of four components built around the ns-3 simulator for simulated and emulated scenarios.

Abstract: Encrypted traffic classification is essential for network security monitoring and traffic management. Self-supervised pre-training can learn reusable representations from large-scale unlabeled traffic, but TLS 1.3, QUIC, and privacy mechanisms such as Encrypted Client Hello (ECH) increasingly conceal application, handshake, and service-identifying information. Observable upper-layer bytes are therefore dominated by ciphertext, weakening the link between raw-byte patterns and application semantics and limiting model generalization. Moreover, the availability and discriminative value of observable information vary across protocols, protection profiles, and tasks, complicating classification across diverse scenarios. To address these challenges, we propose FLARE, a Field-aware Learning and Adaptive Representation Fusion framework for encrypted traffic classification. FLARE pre-trains a BERT-based packet encoder through Masked Field Prediction, which reconstructs masked tokens from field-structured context. During downstream fine-tuning, the parameters of the pre-trained packet encoder remain frozen, and a packet-level gated module adaptively combines protocol-semantic representations with directional packet-length representations. A flow-level encoder then models inter-packet dependencies for classification. We evaluate FLARE on eight datasets spanning diverse encrypted-traffic tasks, including CipherSpectrum and VisQUIC for strongly encrypted scenarios. FLARE achieves superior performance over baselines across the eight datasets, exceeding the strongest pre-trained baseline by 10.92 percentage points on QUIC website fingerprinting. These results demonstrate the effectiveness of field-aware pre-training and adaptive semantic-sequential fusion under constrained observability.

</details>

Abstract: Edge computing places computation closer to users and data sources, but introduces heterogeneous hardware, limited resources, and variable network conditions. WebAssembly (Wasm) is increasingly considered for these environments because modules are compact, portable, and quick to start. However, cold-start latency remains a challenge: when a worker node does not yet hold the required artifact, it must fetch it before execution, adding transfer overhead that directly increases the end-to-end (E2E) latency, especially for large artifacts or slow network links. At the same time, the geographical distance between the requester and the assigned worker independently contributes to latency through network routing overhead. Together, artifact locality and network proximity do not always point to the same placement decision, and naive scheduling strategies fail to account for either. This work extends a Wasm-native edge orchestrator with state-aware scheduling strategies that jointly optimize for both objectives. Evaluation on a Raspberry Pi testbed with emulated regional network latency shows that a hybrid scheduler combining cache-state and network-proximity signals reduces mean E2E latency by up to 40.8% compared to a default round-robin baseline. Artifact locality-aware placement is most effective when artifacts are large or repeatedly requested, while network-aware placement dominates when artifacts are small and routing latency has a larger relative effect, but combining both objectives consistently yields the lowest E2E latency across diverse edge workloads.

</details>

Abstract: Proactive Open Radio Access Network (O-RAN) slicing requires decisions before an approaching quality-of-service (QoS) loss becomes difficult to reverse. We present FAROS, a Forecast-Aware Risk-Informed O-RAN Slicing framework that combines an eight-step QoS forecast, training-only PCMCI+-supported temporal risk, Multi-Agent Proximal Policy Optimization (MAPPO) under centralized training and decentralized execution (CTDE), symbolic feasibility constraints, and reliability-aware Top-K arbitration. MAPPO supplies the actor proposal, while A5v2 checks admissibility and one-step utility before execution. Evaluation uses ten validation-selected frozen checkpoints, 500 matched start indices per seed under the risky-start protocol, and 150,000 agent-level decisions. FAROS obtains mean reward 0.2778 and ten-step recovery 90.56% across ten seeds. A fixed model-matched diagnostic rule reaches reward 0.3015 and recovery 97.57% under the same transition-reward contract, so we make no superiority claim over direct threshold logic. A same-actor M4 shield control also reaches zero risky-state prohibited actions. In the M5 trace, A5v2 changes 22.61% of actor proposals and reduces the predicate-defined prohibited-action rate from 16.79% at proposal time to zero at execution; this property is enforced by the arbiter rather than learned by MAPPO. Independent ns-3 experiments test the six-action interface at packet level and remain separate from end-to-end FAROS claims.

</details>

Abstract: Unmanned Aerial Vehicles carrying access points or base stations are widely positioned so as to hold line-of-sight links with every ground user at once. The algorithms that do so are evaluated against obstacle information treated as exact, whether that information comes from a surveyed map or, more recently, from onboard vision. This paper analyses what becomes of that requirement when the obstacle information is estimated rather than given, and derives the detection accuracy it implicitly demands. Three results follow. The probability of satisfying the requirement decays geometrically with the number of users, so that a detector accurate enough for one user is not accurate enough for a network. Spatially correlated detection error raises that probability but concentrates failures into systematic blind spots. Relaxing the requirement from all users to ninety per cent of them widens the tolerable detection error by more than an order of magnitude, far beyond what any plausible improvement of the detector could deliver.
